Scattering Theory — Wave Operators, S-Matrix & the Lax-Phillips Framework

Scattering theory studies the asymptotic behavior of waves (or quantum particles) as they interact with obstacles or potentials. The central objects are the Møller wave operators Ω_± = s-lim_{t→±∞} e^{itH} e^{-itH₀} which compare the interacting dynamics generated by H = H₀ + V to the free dynamics H₀ = −Δ. The scattering operator S = Ω_+^* Ω_- encodes the probability amplitudes for transitions between asymptotic states. In the stationary formulation, the Lippmann-Schwinger equation gives the scattering states, and the partial wave expansion reduces the problem to radial equations with phase shifts δ_ℓ(k). The Lax-Phillips theory provides a translation representation for hyperbolic PDEs, linking poles of the scattering matrix to resonances. The Birman-Krein formula connects the spectral shift function to the scattering matrix via det S(λ) = e^{−2πi ξ(λ)}. Inverse scattering (the Marchenko and Gel'fand-Levitan equations) recovers the potential from scattering data.

"Scattering theory is the study of asymptotic behavior in quantum mechanics."
— Tosio Kato
Part 1

📏 Time-Dependent Scattering Theory

Wave Operators
Møller Operators, the Scattering Operator, and the S-Matrix
The Hamiltonian is H = H₀ + V where H₀ = −Δ and V is a short-range potential satisfying |V(x)| ≤ C|x|^{−1−ε}. The Møller wave operators are defined as the strong limits Ω_± = s-lim_{t→±∞} e^{itH} e^{-itH₀}. They satisfy the intertwining property: HΩ_± = Ω_± H₀. The scattering operator S = Ω_+^* Ω_- is unitary on L²(ℝⁿ) and commutes with H₀. In the momentum representation, the S-matrix has the form ⟨k|S|k'⟩ = δ(k−k') − 2πi δ(|k|²/2−|k'|²/2) T(k,k'). The optical theorem relates the total cross-section to the forward scattering amplitude: σ_total = (4π/k) Im f(0). The Enss method provides a phase space analysis of scattering, using geometric decompositions of the Hilbert space into incoming and outgoing subspaces. The wave operators are asymptotically complete: the continuous spectral subspace of H equals the closure of the ranges of Ω_±, meaning all scattering states are describable as free states at ±∞.
The Møller wave operators Ω_± compare interacting and free dynamics at asymptotically early and late times; their product S = Ω_+^* Ω_- is the scattering operator encoding all observable transition probabilities.
Part 2

🌍 Stationary Scattering Theory and the Resolvent

Stationary
The Resolvent, Lippmann-Schwinger Equation, and Partial Wave Expansion
The resolvent R(z) = (H−z)^{−1} for Im z > 0 extends to the continuous spectrum; its boundary values R(λ±i0) as λ ∈ σ_c(H) define the incoming and outgoing solutions. The Lippmann-Schwinger equation for the outgoing scattering state: ψ^+(k,x) = e^{ik·x} − ∫ G₀^+(|x−y|,k) V(y) ψ^+(k,y) dy where G₀^+(r,k) = −e^{ikr}/(4πr) is the outgoing Green's function. The scattering amplitude f(k',k) = −(1/4π) ∫ e^{−ik'·y} V(y) ψ^+(k,y) dy. For spherically symmetric potentials, the partial wave expansion decomposes into angular momentum channels ℓ = 0,1,2,... with the radial wavefunction behaving asymptotically as sin(kr − ℓπ/2 + δ_ℓ(k))/r where δ_ℓ(k) is the phase shift. Levinson's theorem: δ_ℓ(0) − δ_ℓ(∞) = π·N_ℓ where N_ℓ is the number of bound states with angular momentum ℓ. The effective range expansion: k^{2ℓ+1} cot δ_ℓ(k) = −1/a_ℓ + (1/2) r_ℓ k² + O(k⁴) where a_ℓ is the scattering length and r_ℓ the effective range.
Stationary scattering theory reformulates the time-dependent problem via the resolvent; the partial wave expansion reduces the 3D problem to 1D radial equations with phase shifts encoding the interaction.
Part 3

🧮 Resonances and the Spectral Shift Function

Resonances
Resonance Poles, the Breit-Wigner Formula, and the Birman-Krein Theory
Resonances are poles of the analytically continued S-matrix in the lower half-plane of the complex energy plane (or momentum plane). They correspond to metastable states with finite lifetime. The Breit-Wigner formula: near a resonance at energy E_r with width Γ, the cross-section behaves as σ(E) ∼ (Γ/2)²/((E−E_r)² + (Γ/2)²). The lifetime τ = ℏ/Γ, so narrower resonances live longer. The spectral shift function ξ(λ; H, H₀) = (1/π) lim_{ε→0⁺} Im log det(I + V R₀(λ+iε)) quantifies the spectral deformation. The Birman-Krein formula: det S(λ) = e^{−2πi ξ(λ)} — a deep connection between the scattering matrix and the spectral shift. The trace formula: Tr(f(H) − f(H₀)) = ∫ f'(λ) ξ(λ) dλ for suitable functions f, known as the Krein spectral shift formula. The perturbation determinant D(z) = det(I + V R₀(z)) is an analytic function whose zeroes correspond to eigenvalues (bound states) and whose phase on the continuous spectrum gives ξ(λ).
Resonances are poles of the S-matrix in the complex plane; their widths give decay rates. The spectral shift function bridges the spectral and scattering properties via the Birman-Krein and trace formulas.
Part 4

🔥 Inverse Scattering and the Lax-Phillips Theory

Inverse
The Marchenko and Gel'fand-Levitan Equations, and the Lax-Phillips Framework
Inverse scattering recovers the potential V from scattering data (the S-matrix or reflection coefficients). The Gel'fand-Levitan equation: K(x,y) + F(x,y) + ∫_x^∞ K(x,t)F(t,y) dt = 0 for y > x, where F is constructed from the spectral function. The Marchenko equation: A(x+y) + B(x,y) + ∫_x^∞ A(y+t)B(x,t) dt = 0. Both are Fredholm integral equations that uniquely determine the potential. The Lax-Phillips scattering theory for hyperbolic PDEs studies the wave equation on exterior domains via the Lax-Phillips semigroup Z(t) = P_+ U(t) P_- acting on the outgoing translation representation. The poles of the scattering matrix correspond to resonances of the obstacle — they govern the decay of energy in the exterior domain. Applications include Selberg scattering theory for automorphic functions on hyperbolic surfaces, the Mazzeo-Melrose theory of scattering on asymptotically hyperbolic manifolds, and the theory of resonances for the Laplacian on Riemannian manifolds with cusps. The Poisson formula for resonances relates the trace of the wave group to a sum over resonances.
Inverse scattering uses integral equations (Gel'fand-Levitan, Marchenko) to reconstruct the potential. The Lax-Phillips theory provides a translation representation for wave equations, linking resonance poles to the geometry of the obstacle.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

Multichannel Scattering and Coupled-Channel Equations

In many physical systems the asymptotic Hilbert space splits into several orthogonal channels, each labelled by a set of quantum numbers such as total angular momentum, spin, or internal excitation. The free Hamiltonian H₀ then acts as a direct sum H₀ = ⊕ₙ H₀⁽ⁿ⁾ on the channel subspaces ℋₙ. The interacting Hamiltonian H = H₀ + V contains off‑diagonal couplings Vₘₙ that transfer probability between channels. The multichannel Møller operators are defined by the strong limits Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e-iH₀t Pac(H₀), where Pac(H₀) projects onto the absolutely continuous subspace of H₀. Existence and completeness of these operators require that the interchannel couplings satisfy suitable short‑range conditions, typically ∫₁^∞ ‖Vₘₙ(r)‖ dr < ∞ for all channel pairs.

The stationary formulation leads to a system of coupled Lippmann–Schwinger equations for the channel components ψ⁺ₙ(k) of an outgoing scattering state:

ψ⁺ₙ(k) = φₙ(k) + Σₘ ∫ G₀ₙ⁺(E) Vₙₘ ψ⁺ₘ(k) dE,

where φₙ(k) is the incoming plane wave in channel n and G₀ₙ⁺(E) = (E - H₀ₙ + i0)-1 is the free resolvent in that channel. The multichannel S‑matrix S(E) is a unitary matrix on the direct sum of channel spaces; its elements Sₘₙ(E) give the transition amplitudes from incoming channel n to outgoing channel m. Unitarity S†S = I encodes flux conservation across all open channels. When a channel is closed at a given energy (its threshold lies above E), the corresponding column of S(E) vanishes, but analytic continuation in the complex energy plane reveals virtual transitions that manifest as resonance poles.

Coupled‑channel equations are the workhorse of nuclear, atomic, and molecular scattering. In the close‑coupling approximation one truncates the infinite channel set to a finite basis, solves the resulting finite system of radial equations, and checks convergence by enlarging the basis. The R‑matrix method provides a numerically stable way to match interior solutions (where the potential is strong) to exterior asymptotic forms, yielding the S‑matrix at each energy. The eigenphase sum Σⱼ δⱼ(E), obtained from the diagonalisation of S(E), obeys a multichannel Levinson theorem: the total phase shift at zero energy equals π times the total number of bound states across all channels, provided no half‑bound states occur at thresholds.

Long-Range Potentials and the Dollard Modification

Standard time‑dependent scattering theory assumes that the interaction potential V(x) decays faster than |x|-1 (short‑range condition). For Coulomb‑type potentials V(x) ∼ γ/|x| the free evolution e-iH₀t no longer provides a correct asymptotic reference because the phase accumulated along a classical trajectory diverges logarithmically with time. Dollard (1964) introduced a modified free dynamics U_D(t) = e-iH₀t e-iγ log|t| (in one dimension; in three dimensions the phase factor becomes e-iγ log(t) with an angular‑dependent coefficient). The Dollard wave operators are then defined as Ω_±^D = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt U_D(t) Pac(H₀). Existence of these modified operators restores asymptotic completeness for a large class of long‑range potentials, including the pure Coulomb potential and potentials with a Coulomb tail plus a short‑range remainder.

In the stationary picture the Dollard modification corresponds to replacing the free Green’s function G₀⁺(x,y;k) by the Coulomb Green’s function G_C⁺(x,y;k), which incorporates the exact Coulomb phase shifts. The Lippmann–Schwinger equation becomes ψ⁺_C(k,x) = φ_C(k,x) - ∫ G_C⁺(x,y;k) Vsr(y) ψ⁺_C(k,y) dy, where Vsr is the short‑range part of the potential. The scattering amplitude then splits into a known Coulomb amplitude f_C(θ) (the Rutherford formula) plus a correction fsr(θ) that is analytic in the momentum transfer. This separation is essential for extracting nuclear phase shifts from proton‑proton scattering data, where the Coulomb interaction dominates at small angles.

For potentials decaying as |x|-α with 0 < α ≤ 1 the Dollard phase must be generalised to e-i c t1-α (or logarithmic for α = 1). The Enss method provides a geometric proof of asymptotic completeness for such potentials by constructing propagation observables that separate particles into clusters moving apart with distinct velocities. The key estimate is a minimal escape velocity bound: if a state has energy E, its position operator satisfies ‖x ψ(t)‖ ≥ vmin |t| for large |t|, where vmin depends on the long‑range tail. This geometric approach avoids explicit phase modifications and underlies modern proofs of asymptotic completeness for N‑body systems with Coulomb interactions.

Worked Example: Dollard Phase for a 1/r Potential
Consider the one‑dimensional Hamiltonian H = -½ d²/dx² + γ/|x| with γ > 0. Show that the standard Møller operator Ω_+ = s‑limt→∞ eiHt e-iH₀t does not exist, but the Dollard‑modified operator Ω_+^D = s‑limt→∞ eiHt e-iH₀t e-iγ log t does exist on the absolutely continuous subspace.
The free propagator in momentum representation is (e-iH₀t φ)(p) = e-i p² t/2 φ(p). For a wave packet concentrated near momentum p₀ > 0, the classical trajectory is x(t) ≈ p₀ t. The phase accumulated from the potential along this trajectory is ∫₀^t γ/(p₀ s) ds = (γ/p₀) log t. In the interacting evolution the state acquires an extra factor e-i (γ/p₀) log t. Since p₀ varies across the packet, the phase is not a simple multiple of the identity, and the strong limit of eiHt e-iH₀t fails to converge. Multiplying the free evolution by e-iγ log t (which corresponds to the average phase for p₀ = 1 in suitable units) removes the leading logarithmic divergence. A rigorous proof uses the Cook method: one shows that ‖(V - γ/|x|) e-iH₀t e-iγ log t ψ‖ is integrable in t for ψ ∈ ℋac(H₀), which implies existence of the modified wave operator by the Cook–Kuroda theorem.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mentions a Coulomb or 1/r tail, immediately recall the Dollard modification. Write the modified free propagator U_D(t) = e-iH₀t e-iγ log t and state that standard wave operators fail because the phase diverges logarithmically. For multichannel problems, note that each open channel may require its own Dollard phase if the long‑range part is channel‑dependent. This distinction often separates a passing answer from a top‑mark answer.

Trace Class Conditions and the Existence of Wave Operators

The abstract existence theory for Møller operators relies on operator‑ideal criteria for the perturbation V. Kato’s smooth perturbation theory shows that if V is H₀-smooth on an interval I, i.e. ∫_ℝ ‖V e-iH₀t ψ‖² dt ≤ C ‖ψ‖² for all ψ, then the wave operators exist and are complete on the spectral subspace EH₀(I)ℋ. A sufficient condition for H₀-smoothness is that V factorises as V = G* J G with G bounded from the domain of H₀ to a Hilbert space 𝒦 and J a bounded self‑adjoint operator on 𝒦. In the concrete setting H₀ = -Δ on L²(ℝ³), the Rollnik condition ∫∫ |V(x) V(y)| / |x-y|² dx dy < ∞ guarantees that V is H₀-smooth on (0,∞).

A more refined criterion uses trace‑class and Hilbert‑Schmidt ideals. If V is relatively trace class with respect to H₀, i.e. V (H₀ + i)-1 ∈ 𝔖₁ (the trace class), then the wave operators exist and the scattering operator S = Ω_+* Ω_- differs from the identity by a trace‑class operator. The Birman–Krein formula det S(λ) = e-2π i ξ(λ) then holds with a well‑defined spectral shift function ξ(λ). If V (H₀ + i)-1 ∈ 𝔖₂ (Hilbert‑Schmidt) but not trace class, the wave operators still exist, but S - I is only Hilbert‑Schmidt; the determinant must be interpreted as a regularised (Fredholm) determinant. The Kato–Rosenblum theorem states that if V is trace class, the absolutely continuous parts of H and H₀ are unitarily equivalent, so singular continuous spectrum cannot be created by a trace‑class perturbation.

For potentials decaying as |x|-α with α > 3 in three dimensions, V (H₀ + i)-1 is trace class. For 2 < α ≤ 3 it is Hilbert‑Schmidt but not trace class. The borderline α = 2 corresponds to the critical Rollnik class. These thresholds are sharp: there exist potentials with α = 2 for which the wave operators fail to exist on a set of energies of positive measure. In the N‑body setting, the HVZ theorem describes the essential spectrum, and the Mourre estimate provides a positive commutator method to prove absence of singular continuous spectrum and asymptotic completeness for a wide class of long‑range pair potentials.

Analytic Continuation of the S‑Matrix and Regge Poles

The scattering matrix S(k) for a short‑range, spherically symmetric potential is a meromorphic function of the complex momentum k in the cut plane ℂ i[0,∞). Its poles in the upper half‑plane correspond to bound states (purely imaginary k = iκ, κ > 0), while poles in the lower half‑plane correspond to resonances. For non‑central or multichannel problems, S becomes a matrix‑valued function of the energy E = k². Analytic continuation in the complex angular momentum ℓ plane, introduced by Regge (1959), reveals Regge poles ℓ_n(E) — complex values of ℓ at which the partial‑wave S‑matrix element S_ℓ(E) has a pole. The Regge trajectory ℓ(E) describes how the pole moves with energy; its real part gives the effective angular momentum of a resonance, while its imaginary part is related to the resonance width.

The Sommerfeld–Watson transform rewrites the partial‑wave expansion of the scattering amplitude as a contour integral in the complex ℓ plane: f(θ) = (1/2ik) Σℓ=0^∞ (2ℓ+1) (S_ℓ - 1) P_ℓ(cos θ) = (1/2ik) ∫_C (ℓ+½) (S_ℓ - 1) P_ℓ(cos θ) dℓ / sin πℓ. Deforming the contour picks up contributions from Regge poles, yielding a representation of the amplitude as a sum over Regge poles plus a background integral. At high energies, the leading Regge pole (the Pomeron in hadronic physics) dominates the forward scattering amplitude, giving a power‑law behaviour f(θ) ∼ sα(0) where α(0) is the intercept of the leading trajectory. This Regge theory underpins the analytic structure of scattering amplitudes in quantum field theory and string theory.

In potential scattering, the Jost function F_ℓ(k) is an entire function of ℓ for fixed k, and its zeros give the Regge poles. The Mandelstam representation expresses the scattering amplitude as a double spectral integral over the Mandelstam variables s and t, assuming analyticity and crossing symmetry. The Froissart bound σtot(s) ≤ C log² s follows from the assumption that the amplitude is analytic in a Lehmann ellipse in the cos θ plane. These analytic properties are not merely mathematical curiosities; they constrain dispersion relations that connect the real and imaginary parts of the forward amplitude, enabling precise predictions for low‑energy parameters from high‑energy data.

  • Assuming standard Møller operators exist for Coulomb‑type potentials — they do not; the Dollard modification is mandatory.
  • Confusing the Rollnik condition with the Kato smoothness condition — Rollnik is a sufficient condition for smoothness, not equivalent.
  • Treating the multichannel S‑matrix as a scalar — it is a unitary matrix; unitarity must be checked channel by channel.
  • Neglecting threshold effects in Levinson’s theorem — half‑bound states at zero energy contribute an extra π/2 to the phase shift.
  • Using the Breit‑Wigner formula far from the resonance peak — it is a local approximation valid only when |E - E_r| ≪ Γ.
  • Assuming all Regge poles lie on linear trajectories — only in specific models (e.g., string theory) are trajectories exactly linear; in potential scattering they are curved.
  • Forgetting that the spectral shift function is defined only up to an additive integer — the Birman–Krein formula fixes it modulo integers.

These advanced topics complete the rigorous toolkit needed for research‑level scattering problems and for the most demanding examination questions.

Time‑Delay and the Eisenbud–Wigner–Smith Formula

The concept of time‑delay provides a physically intuitive measure of how long a scattering particle spends in the interaction region compared with free propagation. For a one‑channel problem with Hamiltonian H = H₀ + V and unitary scattering matrix S(E) = e2iδ(E), the Eisenbud–Wigner–Smith time‑delay is defined as τ(E) = 2ℏ dδ/dE. In the multichannel case the scattering matrix is a unitary matrix S(E) on the space of open channels; the time‑delay operator is the Hermitian matrix Q(E) = −iℏ S†(E) dS(E)/dE. Its eigenvalues are the proper time‑delays for the asymptotic channel states, and the trace Tr Q(E) equals the sum of eigen‑time‑delays, which can be expressed as the energy derivative of the total scattering phase shift. The trace formula connects the time‑delay to the density of states: ρ(E) − ρ₀(E) = (1/2πℏ) Tr Q(E), where ρ and ρ₀ are the interacting and free densities of states respectively. This identity is a direct consequence of the Krein spectral shift function ξ(E) because ξ(E) = (1/π) arg det S(E) and τ(E) = 2ℏ dξ/dE. Rigorous proofs require that V be trace class relative to H₀, or at least that the wave operators exist and are complete. For long‑range potentials the Dollard modification must be used, and the time‑delay operator acquires an additional contribution from the asymptotic phase. In the semiclassical limit ℏ → 0 the eigenvalues of Q(E) converge to the classical sojourn times of trajectories with energy E, providing a bridge between quantum scattering and classical mechanics. The time‑delay formalism also underlies the analysis of resonance lifetimes: a Breit‑Wigner resonance at E = Er − iΓ/2 produces a Lorentzian peak in τ(E) of height 2ℏ/Γ and width Γ, so the resonance width is the inverse of the maximal time‑delay. Experimental applications include electron scattering from molecules, where time‑delay spectroscopy reveals temporary anion states, and nuclear physics, where the Smith matrix is used to analyse overlapping resonances in compound‑nucleus reactions.

The Birman–Schwinger Principle and Bound State Counting

The Birman–Schwinger principle transforms the eigenvalue problem for a Schrödinger operator H = −Δ + V on L²(ℝⁿ) into a spectral problem for a compact integral operator. For a negative energy E = −κ² (κ > 0) the resolvent of the free Hamiltonian is R₀(−κ²) = (−Δ + κ²)−1, whose integral kernel in three dimensions is Gκ(x,y) = e−κ|x−y|/(4π|x−y|). Define the Birman–Schwinger operator Kκ = |V|1/2 R₀(−κ²) |V|1/2 (with appropriate sign factors for non‑positive V). Then −κ² is an eigenvalue of H if and only if Kκ has eigenvalue 1. Moreover, the number N(V) of negative eigenvalues of H (counting multiplicities) equals the number of eigenvalues of K0 = |V|1/2 (−Δ)−1 |V|1/2 that exceed 1. This principle yields sharp bounds on N(V) such as the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum inequality N(V) ≤ Cn ∫ |V−(x)|n/2 dx for n ≥ 3, where V− = max(−V,0). In dimension two the bound involves the Orlicz space L log L, and in dimension one the Bargmann bound N(V) ≤ 1 + ∫ |x| |V−(x)| dx holds. The Birman–Schwinger operator is also the starting point for the proof of the Efimov effect: for three‑body systems with pairwise zero‑range interactions the operator Kκ develops an infinite sequence of eigenvalues accumulating at κ = 0, producing infinitely many three‑body bound states. In scattering theory the same kernel appears in the Lippmann–Schwinger equation for the T‑matrix, and the condition det(I − Kκ) = 0 is equivalent to the vanishing of the Jost function. The principle extends to matrix‑valued potentials and to Pauli operators with magnetic fields, where the kernel involves the magnetic Green function. Numerical implementations use the Birman–Schwinger formulation to compute bound states by solving a nonlinear eigenvalue problem for κ, which is often more stable than direct discretisation of the Schrödinger operator.

Worked Example: Birman–Schwinger Bound for a Spherical Well
Consider the three‑dimensional spherical well V(r) = −V₀ for r < a and 0 otherwise, with V₀ > 0. Use the Birman–Schwinger principle to obtain an upper bound on the number of bound states N(V).
The Birman–Schwinger operator at κ = 0 is K₀ = V₀1/2 (−Δ)−1 V₀1/2 supported on the ball of radius a. Its kernel is V₀/(4π|x−y|) for |x|,|y| < a. By the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum inequality N(V) ≤ C₃ ∫ |V₋|3/2 dx = C₃ V₀3/2 (4π a³/3). The optimal constant C₃ is known to be (4/3π)2/3 ≈ 0.115. Hence N(V) ≤ 0.115 × V₀3/2 × (4π a³/3). For a given V₀ a this gives a concrete numerical upper bound. The exact number of bound states is obtained by solving the transcendental equation κ a = √(V₀ a² − κ² a²) tan(√(V₀ a² − κ² a²)) for κ > 0, but the Birman–Schwinger estimate already shows that N(V) grows like (V₀ a²)3/2 for deep wells.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a bound on the number of bound states, first identify the dimension and the sign of the potential. Write down the appropriate CLR or Bargmann inequality, insert the given potential, and simplify the integral. Do not attempt to solve the full spectral problem unless explicitly required; the inequality is usually the intended answer.

Complex Scaling and the Computation of Resonances

Complex scaling (also called dilation analyticity) is a powerful technique that turns resonances — poles of the analytically continued scattering matrix on the second Riemann sheet — into discrete eigenvalues of a non‑selfadjoint, dilated Hamiltonian. For a dilation angle θ ∈ (0, π/2) define the unitary dilation operator U(θ) on L²(ℝⁿ) by (U(θ)ψ)(x) = enθ/2 ψ(eθx). The dilated Hamiltonian is H(θ) = U(θ) H U(θ)−1 = e−2θ (−Δ) + V(eθx). If the potential V is dilation analytic (i.e. extends to an analytic function in a sector containing the positive real axis and satisfies suitable decay), then the essential spectrum of H(θ) is rotated by angle −2θ into the lower half‑plane: σess(H(θ)) = e−2iθ [0, ∞). Resonances of H with complex energy E = Er − iΓ/2 (Γ > 0) become genuine eigenvalues of H(θ) provided 2θ > arg(E). The eigenvalues are independent of θ in the region where they are exposed, a consequence of the Aguilar–Balslev–Combes theorem. Numerically, one discretises H(θ) using a basis of square‑integrable functions (e.g. Gaussian or B‑spline bases) and diagonalises the resulting complex symmetric matrix. The computed eigenvalues that are stable with respect to variations of θ and basis size approximate the true resonances. Complex scaling also yields the resonance wave functions (Gamow vectors) as the corresponding eigenvectors, which are square‑integrable after dilation but grow exponentially in the original coordinates. The method extends to many‑body systems via the HVZ theorem: each cluster threshold generates a rotated branch of essential spectrum, and resonances associated with a given cluster appear as eigenvalues below the corresponding rotated threshold. For long‑range potentials such as Coulomb, exterior complex scaling (scaling only outside a ball) or smooth exterior scaling is used to preserve the asymptotic form of the potential. The technique underlies modern computational packages for atomic and molecular photoionisation, electron‑molecule scattering, and nuclear reaction theory.

Scattering Theory for Dirac Operators and Relativistic Effects

The Dirac operator HD = −iα·∇ + βm + V(x) on L²(ℝ³; ℂ⁴) describes relativistic spin‑½ particles. Its free part H0 has spectrum (−∞, −m] ∪ [m, ∞) with a gap (−m, m). Scattering theory for Dirac operators must handle the two continua (positive and negative energy) and the possibility of bound states embedded in the gap. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eitHD e−itH0 exist and are asymptotically complete for a large class of potentials decaying faster than |x|−1 (short‑range) and for Coulomb‑type potentials with the Dollard modification. The scattering matrix S(E) is a unitary operator on the direct sum of the positive‑ and negative‑energy spectral subspaces at energy E. Because of the gap, the S‑matrix has a block structure: S = diag(S++, S−−) plus off‑diagonal blocks S+−, S−+ describing pair creation/annihilation processes. For purely electrostatic potentials V(x) = V(x)I₄ the off‑diagonal blocks vanish, and the problem reduces to two independent scalar Schrödinger‑type equations after a Foldy–Wouthuysen transformation. The phase shifts for the upper and lower components satisfy a coupled system of first‑order radial equations (the Dirac radial equations). Levinson’s theorem for the Dirac operator relates the total phase shift at zero energy to the number of bound states in the gap, with a correction of π/2 for each half‑bound state at the threshold ±m. Resonances appear as poles of the analytically continued S‑matrix in the lower half‑plane of the complex energy plane, and complex scaling can be applied to the Dirac operator after a suitable regularisation of the essential spectrum rotation. The relativistic optical theorem reads Im T(E) = π T†(E) δ(E − H0) T(E), where T = S − I. In the non‑relativistic limit c → ∞ the Dirac scattering amplitude reduces to the Schrödinger amplitude plus spin‑orbit and Darwin corrections, which are observable in electron‑atom scattering at intermediate energies.

Integrable Systems and the Inverse Scattering Transform

The inverse scattering transform (IST) solves certain nonlinear evolution equations by mapping them to a linear scattering problem whose scattering data evolve trivially in time. The prototypical example is the Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) equation ut − 6u ux + uxxx = 0. Its Lax pair consists of the Schrödinger operator L = −∂x² + u(x,t) and the auxiliary operator A = −4∂x³ + 6u∂x + 3ux. The compatibility condition Lt = [A, L] reproduces KdV. For a given initial potential u(x,0) the direct scattering problem yields the reflection coefficient R(k), discrete eigenvalues −κj² (bound states), and norming constants cj. The time evolution of the scattering data is R(k,t) = R(k,0) e8ik³t, κj(t) = κj(0), cj(t) = cj(0) e4κj³t. The inverse problem reconstructs u(x,t) from the time‑dependent scattering data by solving a Gel'fand–Levitan–Marchenko integral equation. The solution decomposes into a sum of solitons (one for each bound state) plus a dispersive radiation tail determined by the reflection coefficient. The IST extends to the nonlinear Schrödinger equation, the sine‑Gordon equation, and the Kadomtsev–Petviashvili equation, each associated with a different Lax operator (Zakharov–Shabat, AKNS, etc.). In the matrix‑valued case the scattering data become matrix reflection coefficients, and the inverse problem involves a Riemann–Hilbert factorisation problem. The IST provides a rigorous framework for the asymptotic analysis of solutions: as t → ∞ the solution splits into a finite number of solitons moving with constant velocities and a decaying radiation part. The method also yields exact multi‑soliton solutions via the dressing method or the τ‑function formalism. Inverse scattering for the Schrödinger operator on the line is the foundation; for the half‑line or with non‑trivial boundary conditions the spectral data include a boundary matrix, and the IST adapts accordingly. The connection with the spectral shift function appears because the trace of the scattering matrix determines the conserved quantities of the integrable hierarchy.

  • Confusing the Eisenbud–Wigner time‑delay with the classical sojourn time — they coincide only in the semiclassical limit and for non‑trapping potentials.
  • Applying the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum bound in two dimensions without the logarithmic correction — the correct bound involves the Orlicz space L log L.
  • Assuming complex scaling works for any potential — dilation analyticity is essential; for Coulomb potentials one must use exterior complex scaling.
  • Neglecting the off‑diagonal blocks of the Dirac S‑matrix — they describe pair production and are non‑zero for general Lorentz‑scalar or vector potentials.
  • Treating the inverse scattering transform as a purely algebraic procedure — the Gel'fand–Levitan–Marchenko equation is an integral equation that must be solved analytically or numerically.
  • Forgetting that the spectral shift function is defined modulo an integer — the Birman–Krein formula fixes it only up to an additive constant.
  • Using the Breit‑Wigner formula far from the resonance peak — it is a local approximation valid only when |E − Er| ≪ Γ.

These advanced topics complete the rigorous toolkit needed for research‑level scattering problems and for the most demanding examination questions.

Scattering Theory for Periodic Potentials and Band Structure

When the interaction potential V(x) is periodic with lattice vector a, the free Hamiltonian H₀ = −Δ + V₀(x) acquires a band spectrum described by Bloch functions ψₖ(x) = eik·x uₖ(x) with uₖ(x+a)=uₖ(x). The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e-iH₀t exist on the absolutely continuous subspace of H₀ provided the perturbation W = V−V₀ is short‑range relative to the periodic background. The S‑matrix then decomposes into a direct integral over the Brillouin zone: S = ∫BZ⊕ S(k) dk, where each fibre S(k) acts on the finite‑dimensional space of open channels at quasi‑momentum k. Band edges correspond to thresholds where new channels open; near a non‑degenerate edge the dispersion relation behaves as E(k) ≈ E₀ + α|k−k₀|², and the density of states exhibits a square‑root singularity. The spectral shift function ξ(E) inherits logarithmic divergences at band edges, a fact that enters the Birman–Krein formula det S(E) = e-2πi ξ(E). For a compactly supported perturbation of a periodic potential, the wave operators are complete and the absolutely continuous spectrum of H fills the same bands as H₀, possibly with embedded eigenvalues that can be removed by a generic perturbation (Kato’s theorem). The Floquet–Bloch transform reduces the stationary scattering problem to a family of elliptic boundary value problems on the unit cell with quasi‑periodic boundary conditions. The resulting Jost solutions satisfy a Lippmann–Schwinger equation with the periodic Green’s function G₀(k; x,y) = ∑n (E−H₀(k))-1(x,y+na). Analytic continuation of G₀ in the complex quasi‑momentum plane reveals resonances as poles on non‑physical sheets, a technique used in the study of photonic crystals and semiconductor superlattices. The Lax–Phillips framework adapts by taking the incoming/outgoing subspaces as the spans of Bloch waves with group velocity directed toward/away from the interaction region; the translation representation then lives on a direct integral of L² spaces over the Brillouin zone. This structure underlies the rigorous derivation of the Boltzmann equation for electrons in a weakly disordered periodic lattice.

Scattering Theory on Quantum Graphs and Networks

A quantum graph consists of a metric graph Γ with edges e of length Lₑ (finite or infinite) and a self‑adjoint Hamiltonian H = −d²/dx² + Vₑ(x) on each edge, coupled at vertices by boundary conditions that ensure probability current conservation. The most common vertex conditions are the Kirchhoff (continuity plus sum of outgoing derivatives zero) and δ‑type (continuity with a jump in derivative proportional to the wave function value). For graphs with semi‑infinite leads, the scattering matrix S(k) maps incoming amplitudes on the leads to outgoing amplitudes. It can be computed by solving the stationary Schrödinger equation on each edge with the ansatz ψₑ(x) = aₑ eikx + bₑ e-ikx and imposing the vertex conditions, which yields a linear system (I − M(k)) a = b where M(k) encodes the internal structure of the compact part of the graph. The poles of S(k) in the lower half‑plane correspond to resonances; for graphs with rationally related edge lengths the resonance set is periodic in k. The trace formula for quantum graphs relates the density of states to a sum over periodic orbits (the Gutzwiller‑type trace formula), and the spectral shift function can be expressed as ξ(E) = (1/π) Im log det S(√E). The Lax–Phillips construction on graphs identifies the incoming subspace with functions supported on the leads that are purely incoming plane waves, and the outgoing subspace analogously; the translation representation is then a direct sum of L²(ℝ₊) spaces over the leads. Completeness of the wave operators holds if the compact core is non‑trapping (no bound states in the continuum) and the vertex conditions are non‑magnetic. For magnetic fields, a gauge‑invariant phase factor ei∫ A·dx appears in the edge propagation, leading to an Aharonov–Bohm effect in the scattering matrix. The inverse scattering problem on graphs — recovering edge lengths and vertex couplings from S(k) — is solvable for trees and for graphs with a single cycle using the method of spectral data and the Borg–Marchenko theorem adapted to the graph setting. Applications include modeling quantum wires, photonic networks, and the spectral analysis of periodic structures via the Floquet–Bloch reduction to a compact graph with quasi‑periodic vertex conditions.

Worked Example: Scattering Matrix for a Star Graph with Three Leads
Consider a star graph consisting of three semi‑infinite leads (edges e₁, e₂, e₃) meeting at a single vertex v. On each lead the Hamiltonian is −d²/dx². Impose Kirchhoff vertex conditions at v: continuity ψ₁(0)=ψ₂(0)=ψ₃(0) and current conservation ψ₁'(0)+ψ₂'(0)+ψ₃'(0)=0. An incoming plane wave of amplitude 1 on lead 1 with wave number k>0 is scattered into outgoing waves on all three leads. Compute the 3×3 scattering matrix S(k).
On lead j write ψⱼ(x)=aⱼ eikx+bⱼ e-ikx with x≥0 measured from the vertex. Incoming amplitudes are aⱼ, outgoing are bⱼ. For the given excitation a₁=1, a₂=a₃=0. Continuity gives b₁+1 = b₂ = b₃. Current conservation: ik(b₁−1) + ik b₂ + ik b₃ = 0 ⇒ (b₁−1)+b₂+b₃=0. Substituting b₂=b₃=b₁+1 yields (b₁−1)+2(b₁+1)=0 ⇒ 3b₁+1=0 ⇒ b₁=−1/3. Then b₂=b₃=2/3. The first column of S is (b₁,b₂,b₃)ᵀ = (−1/3, 2/3, 2/3)ᵀ. By symmetry the other columns are obtained by permuting indices. Hence S(k) = (1/3) [ [−1, 2, 2]; [2, −1, 2]; [2, 2, −1] ]. This matrix is unitary and independent of k, reflecting the scale invariance of the free Laplacian on a star graph. The eigenvalues are 1 (once) and −1 (twice); the eigenvalue 1 corresponds to the totally symmetric incoming combination which is perfectly transmitted (no reflection), while the two degenerate eigenvalues −1 correspond to antisymmetric combinations that are totally reflected with a phase shift of π.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for the scattering matrix on a graph, first write the general plane‑wave ansatz on each lead, then impose the vertex conditions to obtain a linear system for the outgoing amplitudes in terms of the incoming ones. Exploit symmetry to reduce the number of unknowns; the resulting S‑matrix is often k‑independent for free Laplacians. Check unitarity S†S=I as a quick verification. For resonance questions, remember that poles of S(k) in the lower half‑plane correspond to complex k with Im k<0; on a graph with rational edge lengths these poles form a lattice in the complex plane.

Scattering Theory for Non‑Hermitian PT‑Symmetric Hamiltonians

PT‑symmetric quantum mechanics considers Hamiltonians H that are not Hermitian but invariant under the combined action of parity (P: x→−x) and time reversal (T: i→−i). A typical example is H = −d²/dx² + x²(ix)^ε with ε≥0. When the PT symmetry is unbroken, the spectrum is real and the eigenfunctions are simultaneous eigenstates of PT. The scattering theory for such operators requires a modified inner product, the CPT inner product, under which the time evolution is unitary. The wave operators are defined as Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e-iH₀t where H₀ is a suitable reference Hamiltonian (often the free Laplacian) and the limit is taken in the CPT‑norm. The S‑matrix S = Ω₊* Ω₋ is then pseudo‑unitary: S† η S = η with η the metric operator implementing the CPT inner product. For one‑dimensional PT‑symmetric potentials that are reflectionless in the Hermitian limit, the transmission coefficient can exceed unity in magnitude while the reflection coefficients satisfy |R_L|² + |R_R|² = 1 + (|T|²−1) due to the non‑conservation of the standard probability current. The spectral shift function is defined via the Birman–Krein formula with the pseudo‑determinant det_η S = e-2πi ξ(E). Resonances appear as complex eigenvalues of H on the second sheet of the analytically continued S‑matrix; they occur in conjugate pairs because PT symmetry maps k→−k*. The Lax–Phillips framework can be transplanted by choosing incoming/outgoing subspaces that are orthogonal with respect to the η‑inner product. A key result is that for a large class of PT‑symmetric potentials the wave operators exist and are complete on the absolutely continuous subspace, provided the potential decays faster than |x|-1 and the PT symmetry remains unbroken. If the symmetry is spontaneously broken, the spectrum acquires complex conjugate pairs and the scattering matrix develops poles on the physical sheet, signalling the breakdown of the standard scattering formalism. Applications include optical systems with balanced gain and loss, where the PT‑symmetric scattering matrix describes the amplification and attenuation of modes in coupled waveguides.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Magnetic Fields

When a magnetic vector potential A(x) is present, the free Hamiltonian becomes H₀ = (−i∇ − A₀(x))² on ℝⁿ (n≥2). The magnetic field B = ∇×A₀ influences scattering through the Aharonov–Bohm effect: even if B is confined to a solenoid, the vector potential outside is non‑zero and produces a measurable phase shift. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e-iH₀t exist for short‑range electric perturbations V and magnetic perturbations A−A₀ that decay faster than |x|-1 in a suitable gauge. Gauge invariance of the S‑matrix is ensured by the intertwining property Ω±(A+∇χ) = eiχ Ω±(A) e-iχ. The stationary scattering amplitude f(θ, φ; k) for a magnetic potential with compact support can be expressed via the Lippmann–Schwinger equation with the magnetic Green’s function G₀^A(x,y; E). For the Aharonov–Bohm solenoid of flux Φ, the partial‑wave expansion yields phase shifts δ_m = π(|m−α|−|m|) where α = Φ/Φ₀ (Φ₀ = 2π/e is the flux quantum). The differential cross section exhibits a characteristic 1/sin²(θ/2) singularity in the forward direction, reflecting the long‑range nature of the vector potential. The spectral shift function for magnetic perturbations satisfies the Birman–Krein formula with the magnetic S‑matrix; its high‑energy asymptotics involve the magnetic flux through the support of B. The Lax–Phillips construction uses the magnetic translation representation: the incoming subspace consists of states that, when evolved backward with H₀, concentrate on the incoming spherical waves with the magnetic phase factor. For non‑compact magnetic fields (e.g., constant B), the spectrum of H₀ is pure point (Landau levels) and the standard scattering theory must be replaced by a theory of edge states and quantum Hall conductance, where the S‑matrix relates incoming and outgoing edge channels. In the semiclassical limit ℏ→0, the magnetic time‑delay is given by the Eisenbud–Wigner formula with the classical action including the line integral of A along the trajectory. The inverse scattering problem for magnetic fields — determining B from the scattering amplitude — is uniquely solvable in two dimensions for sufficiently regular, compactly supported B, using the nonlinear Fourier transform associated with the Pauli operator.

Scattering Theory for the Wave Equation on Asymptotically Euclidean Manifolds

Let (M,g) be a complete Riemannian manifold that coincides with ℝⁿ outside a compact set, and consider the wave equation (∂ₜ² + Δ_g) u = 0. The free dynamics is given by the Euclidean wave group on ℝⁿ. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ U(t) U₀(t)⁻¹ map incoming/outgoing free waves to solutions that behave like free waves in the Euclidean ends. The S‑matrix acts on the space of Cauchy data on the unit sphere Sn-1 at infinity and can be identified with the scattering matrix of the associated stationary Helmholtz equation (Δ_g + λ) u = 0 via the Fourier transform in time. The Lax–Phillips framework is particularly natural here: the incoming subspace D₋ consists of solutions supported in the past light cone of a large ball, and the outgoing subspace D₊ consists of solutions supported in the future light cone. The translation representation is then an isometry from the orthogonal complement K = (D₋ ⊕ D₊)^⊥ onto L²(ℝ; H) where H is the energy space of the compact part. The generator of the translation group is the Lax–Phillips semigroup Z(t), whose spectrum in the lower half‑plane corresponds to resonances (poles of the meromorphically continued resolvent (Δ_g + λ)-1). For non‑trapping geometries (no trapped geodesics), the resolvent continues analytically to a strip Im λ > −C and the local energy decays exponentially. For trapping geometries, resonances accumulate near the real axis and the decay is only polynomial. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) for the wave equation is related to the scattering phase by ξ(λ) = (1/π) arg det S(√λ). The trace formula of Duistermaat–Guillemin expresses the singularities of the wave trace at lengths of closed geodesics in terms of the Poincaré map. Inverse scattering on asymptotically Euclidean manifolds asks whether the scattering matrix at all energies determines the metric g up to isometry. For n≥3, generic uniqueness holds (Stefanov–Uhlmann), while in two dimensions the problem is equivalent to the Calderón problem for the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map. The connection with the Lax–Phillips theory is that the scattering matrix is the boundary value of the Lax–Phillips semigroup’s Fourier–Laplace transform, providing a time‑domain characterization of the inverse problem.

  • Assuming the standard probability current is conserved for PT‑symmetric Hamiltonians — it is not; the CPT inner product must be used.
  • Neglecting the gauge dependence of the magnetic scattering amplitude — physical observables are gauge invariant, but intermediate expressions depend on the chosen gauge.
  • Treating the Aharonov–Bohm phase shift as a short‑range effect — the vector potential decays only as 1/|x|, so the Dollard modification is required for the wave operators.
  • Confusing the Lax–Phillips incoming subspace for the wave equation with the stationary incoming spherical waves — the former is defined by support properties in space‑time, the latter by asymptotic angular momentum decomposition.
  • Applying the standard Birman–Krein formula to non‑Hermitian S‑matrices without the metric operator η — the correct formula uses the pseudo‑determinant det_η.
  • Assuming completeness of wave operators on quantum graphs with magnetic fluxes without checking the non‑trapping condition — bound states in the continuum can destroy completeness.
  • Overlooking that the spectral shift function on a periodic background is defined modulo an integer at each band edge — the Birman–Krein formula fixes it only up to an additive constant per band.

Scattering Theory for Many‑Body Systems and the N‑Body Problem

The extension of two‑body scattering theory to an arbitrary number of particles requires a careful treatment of cluster decompositions and the asymptotic separation of subsystems. The fundamental object is the N‑body Hamiltonian H = H₀ + V where H₀ = Σᵢ (−ℏ²/2mᵢ)Δᵢ and V = Σi<j</sub> Vij(xᵢ−xⱼ). The free dynamics splits into a direct sum over all cluster partitions a of the configuration space, each partition describing a grouping of particles into clusters that move freely relative to each other. The channel Hamiltonian H_a = H₀ + V_a contains only the intra‑cluster interactions V_a; its spectrum consists of bound‑state eigenvalues of the clusters plus the continuous spectrum of the relative motion.

Wave operators are defined for each channel a as Ω±_a = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e-iH_a t Pac(H_a), where Pac(H_a) projects onto the absolutely continuous subspace of H_a. The central result, proved by Enss, Sigal‑Soffer and others, is asymptotic completeness: the ranges of all Ω±_a are mutually orthogonal and their direct sum equals the absolutely continuous subspace of the full Hamiltonian H. This implies that every scattering state eventually separates into a collection of non‑interacting clusters, each internally in a bound state. The proof uses propagation estimates (Enss method) that control the probability of finding particles in regions where inter‑cluster distances are bounded.

The multichannel S‑matrix is the collection of operators Sab = Ω+_a* Ω−_b mapping incoming channel b to outgoing channel a. It is unitary on the direct sum of channel spaces and satisfies the cluster decomposition property: if a and b share a common refinement, the corresponding block factorises into a product of lower‑order S‑matrices. The Faddeev equations provide a practical computational scheme for the three‑body case by splitting the transition operator T = V + V G₀ T into components Tij that satisfy a coupled system of integral equations. For N≥4 the Yakubovsky equations generalise this idea, but the number of components grows rapidly.

Bound states embedded in the continuum (BIC) and resonances appear as poles of the meromorphically continued resolvent (H−z)-1 on unphysical sheets. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) for the N‑body problem satisfies a generalised Birman‑Krein formula det S(λ) = e-2πi ξ(λ) where the determinant is taken over the direct sum of channel Hilbert spaces. Trace‑class conditions on the inter‑cluster potentials guarantee the existence of the wave operators; for Coulomb‑type long‑range interactions the Dollard modification must be applied to each pair potential, leading to a phase‑corrected free evolution.

Scattering Theory for Nonlinear Schrödinger Equations and Soliton Interaction

Integrable nonlinear dispersive equations such as the focusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂ₜψ + ∂ₓ²ψ + 2|ψ|²ψ = 0 admit a complete description of scattering via the inverse scattering transform (IST). The Lax pair formulation associates to the potential ψ(x,0) a Zakharov‑Shabat spectral problem L(λ)φ = λφ where L(λ) = i∂ₓ + (0 ψ; −ψ* 0). The continuous spectrum lies on the real λ‑axis; the scattering data consist of a reflection coefficient r(λ) and a discrete set of eigenvalues λ_k in the upper half‑plane with norming constants c_k. Each discrete eigenvalue corresponds to a soliton of amplitude η_k = Im λ_k and velocity ξ_k = Re λ_k.

Time evolution of the scattering data is linear: r(λ,t) = r(λ,0) e4iλ²t, c_k(t) = c_k(0) e4iλ_k²t. The solution at any later time is reconstructed by solving a Riemann‑Hilbert problem or a Gel'fand‑Levitan‑Marchenko integral equation. Multi‑soliton solutions are obtained by pure discrete data (r≡0); their asymptotic behaviour as t→±∞ is a sum of single solitons with shifted positions. The position shift Δx_k after a pairwise collision of solitons k and j is given by Δx_k = (1/η_k) ln| (λ_k−λ_j)/(λ_k−λ_j*) |², a hallmark of elastic soliton scattering.

For non‑integrable perturbations (e.g., higher‑order dispersion, external potentials) the IST framework no longer applies directly. One then uses perturbative scattering theory: the linearised operator around a solitary wave has a neutral mode due to translation invariance and possibly internal modes. The continuous spectrum is absolutely continuous under suitable decay assumptions, and the wave operators for the linearised flow exist. Nonlinear scattering theory (e.g., the work of Buslaev‑Perelman, Cuccagna, Mizumachi) proves asymptotic stability of solitary waves in energy space: any solution with small initial data decomposes into a modulated solitary wave plus a dispersive radiation term that scatters to zero in the Strichartz norm.

The S‑matrix for the nonlinear problem is not a linear operator on a fixed Hilbert space; instead one defines a nonlinear scattering map that takes the asymptotic radiation profile as t→−∞ to the profile as t→+∞. For integrable equations this map is trivial on the discrete data (solitons pass through each other unchanged except for phase shifts) and acts as a nonlinear Fourier transform on the continuous data. In the non‑integrable case the map exhibits radiation generation and possible energy transfer between discrete and continuous modes, a phenomenon absent in linear scattering.

Worked Example: Two‑Soliton Phase Shift
Consider the focusing NLS with two solitons having spectral parameters λ₁ = 1 + 2i and λ₂ = 3 + i. Compute the position shift of the first soliton after the collision.
The shift formula is Δx₁ = (1/η₁) ln| (λ₁−λ₂)/(λ₁−λ₂*) |². Here η₁ = Im λ₁ = 2. λ₁−λ₂ = (1−3) + i(2−1) = −2 + i. λ₁−λ₂* = (1−3) + i(2+1) = −2 + 3i. Their squared moduli: |−2+i|² = 4+1 = 5; |−2+3i|² = 4+9 = 13. Ratio = 5/13. ln(5/13) = ln5 − ln13 ≈ 1.609 − 2.565 = −0.956. Δx₁ = (−0.956)/2 = −0.478. The negative sign means the first soliton is delayed by approximately 0.48 length units relative to free propagation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the asymptotic behaviour of a multi‑soliton solution, first identify the discrete eigenvalues and their ordering by velocity (Re λ). The faster soliton overtakes the slower one; the phase shift depends only on the pair of eigenvalues involved. Memorise the shift formula and practice evaluating complex moduli quickly. For non‑integrable perturbations, recall that the linearised operator’s neutral mode leads to a modulation parameter; the radiation term satisfies a linear dispersive estimate, so Strichartz norms are the standard tool to prove scattering.

Scattering Theory on Manifolds with Conical Singularities

Let (M,g) be a complete Riemannian manifold that is Euclidean outside a compact set except for a finite number of conical points where the metric takes the form dr² + r² h(θ) near r=0 with h a metric on the link manifold Y. The Laplace‑Beltrami operator Δ_g on such a space is essentially self‑adjoint on C_c^∞(M{cones}) and its domain can be described using the b‑calculus of Melrose. The continuous spectrum is [0,∞) with constant multiplicity equal to the dimension of the space of incoming spherical waves on the regular part; the conical singularities contribute additional channels corresponding to the eigenvalues of the model cone operator on the infinite cone C(Y).

The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ e^{it√{Δ_g}} e^{-it√{Δ₀}} exist and are complete provided the metric perturbation is short‑range in the sense of b‑Sobolev spaces. The S‑matrix acts on the direct sum of the regular spherical harmonics and the cone channels. Its matrix elements can be expressed via the scattering matrix of the model cone, which is computed by separation of variables: on the cone the Helmholtz equation (Δ_g + λ)u = 0 reduces to a Bessel equation in r and an eigenvalue problem on Y. The scattering phase for each cone channel is determined by the indicial roots of the Bessel functions, leading to a meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (Δ_g + λ)-1 to the complex plane with poles at the resonances.

Resonances on conic manifolds are known to lie in a logarithmic neighbourhood of the real axis; their distribution is governed by the geometry of the link Y. The Lax‑Phillips framework adapts naturally: the incoming subspace D₋ consists of solutions supported in the past light cone of a large ball that also satisfy the Friedrichs boundary condition at the cone tips. The translation representation maps the orthogonal complement K onto L²(ℝ; H_b) where H_b is the b‑energy space. The generator Z(t) is a semigroup whose spectrum in the lower half‑plane coincides with the resonances. For non‑trapping conic geometries (no geodesics trapped away from the cones) the local energy decays exponentially; if there are trapped geodesics that graze the cones, resonances accumulate near the real axis and only polynomial decay holds.

Inverse scattering on conic manifolds asks whether the full S‑matrix at all energies determines the metric g up to isometry. Results of Hassell‑Vasy and others show generic uniqueness for dimensions n≥3 when the link Y is known. In two dimensions the problem reduces to the Calderón problem on a surface with conical singularities, where the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map on the boundary of a compact exhaustion determines the conformal factor. The Lax‑Phillips semigroup provides a time‑domain characterisation: the scattering matrix is the boundary value of the Fourier‑Laplace transform of Z(t), so knowledge of the time‑domain response operator (the analogue of the response operator in boundary control) is equivalent to knowledge of the S‑matrix.

Scattering Theory for Time‑Dependent Periodic Potentials and Floquet Scattering

Consider the Schrödinger equation i∂ₜψ = H(t)ψ with H(t) = H₀ + V(t) where V(t+T) = V(t) is periodic in time with period T = 2π/ω. The Floquet theorem states that solutions can be written as ψ(t) = e-iεt φ(t) where φ(t+T) = φ(t) and ε is the quasi‑energy defined modulo ω. The monodromy operator U(T,0) = T exp(−i∫₀^T H(s) ds) is unitary on the Hilbert space; its eigenvalues are e-iεT. The Floquet Hamiltonian K = H(t) − i∂ₜ acts on the extended Hilbert space ℋ = L²([0,T]; L²(ℝⁿ)) and has pure point spectrum consisting of the quasi‑energies εα = εα,0 + mω (m∈ℤ).

Scattering theory for such systems compares the full evolution U(t,0) with the free evolution U₀(t) = e-iH₀t. Because H₀ is time‑independent, the natural free Floquet Hamiltonian is K₀ = H₀ − i∂ₜ. Wave operators are defined as Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ U(t,0) U₀(t)-1 Pac(K₀) where Pac(K₀) projects onto the absolutely continuous subspace of K₀. Existence and completeness hold under short‑range conditions on V(t) in both space and time (e.g., ∫ |V(t,x)| (1+|x|) dx dt < ∞). The S‑matrix S = Ω+* Ω− acts on the direct sum of Floquet channels labelled by the free quasi‑energy bands.

A distinctive feature is photon‑assisted scattering: an incoming particle with energy E can emerge in a channel with energy E + mω by absorbing (m>0) or emitting (m<0) photons from the periodic field. The S‑matrix elements Sαβ(E) are non‑zero only when the quasi‑energy conservation ε_α = ε_β (mod ω) holds. In the high‑frequency limit ω→∞ the effective Hamiltonian is given by the Magnus expansion; the leading term is the time‑average of V(t) and the S‑matrix approaches that of a static potential. In the low‑frequency (adiabatic) limit the system follows instantaneous eigenstates and Landau‑Zener transitions between Floquet bands become relevant.

The spectral shift function for the Floquet problem satisfies a generalised Birman‑Krein formula det S(E) = e-2πi ξ(E) where the determinant is over the Floquet channel space. Resonances appear as poles of the meromorphically continued Floquet resolvent (K−z)-1 on the second Riemann sheet; they correspond to quasi‑bound states that decay by photon emission. Complex scaling (dilation analyticity) can be applied to the extended Hamiltonian K to compute these resonances numerically. The Lax‑Phillips construction for time‑periodic systems uses the incoming subspace of states that, when evolved backward with K₀, concentrate on incoming Floquet waves; the translation representation yields a semigroup whose generator has spectrum at the resonances.

Scattering Theory for the Dirac Equation with Strong External Fields

The Dirac Hamiltonian H_D = c α·p + β mc² + V(x) + e φ(x) + e c α·A(x) describes relativistic spin‑½ particles in scalar, electrostatic and magnetostatic potentials. The free Hamiltonian H₀ has spectrum (−∞,−mc²] ∪ [mc²,∞) with a gap of width 2mc². Scattering theory must treat the two continua separately; the wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiH_D t e-iH₀ t P± where P± project onto the positive/negative energy subspaces of H₀. Existence of Ω± requires that the potentials decay faster than |x|-1 in the electrostatic case and faster than |x|-2 for the vector potential (to avoid the long‑range Aharonov‑Bohm effect). For Coulomb potentials V(x) = −Ze²/|x| the Dollard phase modification is needed for each continuum.

The S‑matrix is a 2×2 block operator mapping incoming positive/negative energy states to outgoing positive/negative energy states. The off‑diagonal blocks describe pair creation (positive→negative) and pair annihilation (negative→positive) processes. In the presence of a supercritical electrostatic field (Z > 137 for point nuclei) the Dirac Hamiltonian ceases to be essentially self‑adjoint; one must impose a boundary condition at the origin (e.g., the MIT bag condition) which introduces a self‑adjoint extension parameter. The scattering matrix then depends on this parameter and exhibits resonant behaviour associated with quasi‑bound states diving into the negative continuum.

For strong magnetic fields the Landau level structure of H₀ changes the density of states dramatically. The wave operators exist if the magnetic field is short‑range or if one works in the gauge‑invariant formulation using the magnetic translation representation. The S‑matrix for a constant magnetic field is diagonal in the Landau level index and the scattering amplitude acquires the Aharonov‑Bohm phase factor exp(i e/ℏ ∫ A·dx). In the semiclassical limit ℏ→0 the scattering trajectories are solutions of the Lorentz force equation; the Eisenbud‑Wigner time‑delay formula generalises to include the magnetic contribution to the classical action.

Inverse scattering for the Dirac operator asks whether the scattering matrix at all energies determines the potentials V, φ, A uniquely. For spherically symmetric potentials the problem reduces to a system of ordinary differential equations for the radial components; the Jost functions can be reconstructed from the phase shifts. In the general non‑spherical case, results of Weder and others show that the high‑energy limit of the scattering amplitude determines the Fourier transform of the potentials, yielding uniqueness for sufficiently regular, compactly supported fields. The Lax‑Phillips framework for the Dirac equation uses the incoming subspace of solutions that are supported in the past light cone and have positive energy; the translation representation maps the orthogonal complement onto L²(ℝ; ℋ) where ℋ is the energy space of the interaction region. The generator of the translation semigroup has spectrum at the resonances of the Dirac operator, which are poles of the meromorphically continued resolvent (H_D−z)-1.

  • Assuming the N‑body wave operators exist without verifying the Enss propagation estimates for the specific inter‑cluster potentials.
  • Treating the nonlinear S‑matrix as a linear operator on a fixed Hilbert space; it is a nonlinear map on scattering data.
  • Ignoring the additional cone channels in the S‑matrix on conic manifolds; they arise from the indicial roots of the model cone operator.
  • Forgetting that Floquet quasi‑energies are defined modulo ω, so channel indices must be taken modulo the photon number.
  • Using the standard Birman‑Krein formula for the Dirac S‑matrix without separating positive and negative energy blocks; the determinant must be taken on each continuum separately.
  • Overlooking the need for a self‑adjoint extension parameter in supercritical Coulomb fields; the scattering matrix is not unique without it.
  • Confusing the magnetic Dollard phase for the Dirac equation with the non‑relativistic phase; the relativistic phase involves the line integral of the four‑potential along the classical trajectory.
The advanced sections above complete the required extension of the scattering theory lesson.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Point Interactions and Delta Potentials

Point interactions are idealised potentials supported on a set of zero measure, most commonly a single point in ℝ³ or a finite set of points. In three dimensions the formal Hamiltonian H = −Δ + λδ(x) is not self‑adjoint on C₀^∞(ℝ³{0}); one must specify a self‑adjoint extension of the symmetric operator −Δ|_{C₀^∞(ℝ³{0})}. The family of extensions is parameterised by a single real number α (or ∞ for the Dirichlet condition) via the boundary condition

limr→0 (u(r) − (q/4πr)) = α q,

where u is a function in the domain, q is the coefficient of the 1/r singularity, and α has dimension of inverse length. The scattering matrix for this model can be computed explicitly: the s‑wave phase shift δ₀(k) satisfies tan δ₀(k) = −k/(4πα). For α = 0 one recovers the free Hamiltonian; for α → ∞ the point interaction becomes a hard sphere of zero radius, producing a phase shift δ₀(k) = −π/2. Bound states appear when α < 0, with binding energy E_b = −(4πα)².

In one and two dimensions the situation differs because the free Green function has a logarithmic singularity in 2D and a linear one in 1D. The self‑adjoint extensions are again described by a single parameter, but the renormalisation of the coupling constant is required to obtain a non‑trivial limit. The 1D δ‑potential λδ(x) yields the transmission coefficient T(k) = 1/(1 + iλ/(2k)) and reflection coefficient R(k) = −iλ/(2k + iλ). In 2D the scattering amplitude has a logarithmic energy dependence, f(k) = 1/(ln(k/κ) + iπ/2), where κ is a renormalisation scale. These models are exactly solvable and serve as benchmarks for numerical methods and for the theory of quantum graphs.

The rigorous construction uses the theory of self‑adjoint extensions of symmetric operators (von Neumann’s deficiency indices) and the Kreĭn resolvent formula. The wave operators Ω± exist and are complete for all α ∈ ℝ ∪ {∞}; the absolutely continuous spectrum remains [0,∞) and there is at most one bound state. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) is a step function jumping by 1 at the bound‑state energy (if present) and otherwise given by ξ(λ) = (1/π)δ₀(√λ). This example illustrates how singular perturbations can be treated without ultraviolet divergences, a feature that carries over to more complicated models such as point interactions on manifolds or in the presence of magnetic fields.

Scattering Theory for the Pauli Equation with Spin-Orbit Coupling

The Pauli Hamiltonian for a non‑relativistic spin‑½ particle in an external electromagnetic field reads

H_P = (1/2m)(σ·(p − eA))² + eφ − (eħ/2m)σ·B + V_SO(r) L·S,

where σ are the Pauli matrices, L = r×p, S = (ħ/2)σ, and V_SO(r) is the spin‑orbit potential derived from the scalar potential φ (e.g. V_SO = (1/2m²c²)(1/r)dφ/dr for a central field). The term −(eħ/2m)σ·B is the Zeeman interaction. The free Hamiltonian H₀ = p²/2m has a twofold spin degeneracy. Scattering theory for H_P proceeds by constructing the wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiH_P t e-iH₀ t on the Hilbert space L²(ℝ³; ℂ²). Existence and completeness hold under the usual short‑range conditions on A, φ, and V_SO (e.g. |V_SO(r)| ≤ C(1+|r|)-1-ε).

Because the spin‑orbit term couples orbital and spin degrees of freedom, the partial‑wave expansion must be performed in the basis of total angular momentum J = L + S. The scattering matrix becomes a 2×2 matrix in the spin space for each partial wave ℓ, with elements Sℓj j' where j = ℓ ± ½. The off‑diagonal elements describe spin‑flip transitions. In the first Born approximation the spin‑flip amplitude for a central spin‑orbit potential is

fsf(θ) = −(m/2πħ²) ∫ e-iq·r V_SO(r) ⟨χ_f| L·S |χ_i⟩ d³r,

where q = k_f − k_i and χ_i, χ_f are initial and final spinors. For a pure spin‑orbit potential the differential cross section exhibits a characteristic sin²θ dependence, reflecting the axial symmetry of L·S. The analysing power (Sherman function) A(θ) = (|f+|² − |f−|²)/(|f+|² + |f−|²) is a measurable quantity that directly probes the spin‑orbit strength.

At higher energies the Foldy‑Wouthuysen transformation block‑diagonalises the Dirac Hamiltonian, yielding the Pauli Hamiltonian with relativistic corrections (Darwin term, mass‑velocity term). The scattering matrix for the full Dirac equation reduces to the Pauli S‑matrix plus corrections of order (v/c)². In the low‑energy limit the effective range expansion for each total‑angular‑momentum channel contains a spin‑orbit contribution to the scattering length and effective range. These parameters can be extracted from phase‑shift analyses of electron‑atom or nucleon‑nucleon scattering data.

Technique: First Born spin‑flip amplitude for a central spin‑orbit potential
Compute the leading order spin‑flip scattering amplitude fsf(θ) for a weak spin‑orbit potential VSO(r) = V₀ e-r/a with V₀ small, assuming an unpolarised incident beam and detection of the final spin projection along the quantisation axis.

In the first Born approximation the scattering amplitude is the Fourier transform of the potential matrix element between initial and final spinors. Choose the quantisation axis along the incident momentum k_i. The initial spinor is χ_i = (1,0)^T (spin up). The final spinor for spin‑flip is χ_f = (0,1)^T (spin down). The matrix element ⟨χ_f| L·S |χ_i⟩ = (ħ/2) ⟨χ_f| L_+ |χ_i⟩ where L_+ = L_x + iL_y. In the coordinate representation L_+ = ħ eiφ (∂_θ + i cot θ ∂_φ). Acting on the plane wave eik_i·r and integrating over angles yields a factor proportional to sin θ eiφ. The radial integral is ∫0^∞ r² V₀ e-r/a j₁(qr) dr, where j₁ is the spherical Bessel function of order 1 and q = 2k sin(θ/2). Evaluating the integral gives (2V₀ a³ q a)/(1+q² a²)². Collecting constants, the spin‑flip amplitude is

fsf(θ) = −(m V₀ a³ ħ k / 2π) (sin θ) / (1 + 4k² a² sin²(θ/2))².

The differential cross section for spin‑flip is dσsf/dΩ = |fsf(θ)|². The analysing power A(θ) = (|f+|² − |f−|²)/(|f+|² + |f−|²) vanishes in the first Born approximation because the non‑flip amplitude is real while the flip amplitude is purely imaginary; a non‑zero A(θ) appears at second order.

Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the spin‑flip amplitude, first identify the total‑angular‑momentum basis (j = ℓ ± ½). Write the S‑matrix as a 2×2 block for each ℓ. The off‑diagonal blocks are the spin‑flip elements. In the Born approximation only the Fourier transform of the spin‑orbit potential times the matrix element of L·S contributes. Remember that the analysing power requires interference between flip and non‑flip amplitudes, so it is zero at first order. Check whether the potential is central; if not, the partial‑wave expansion must include mixing of different ℓ.

Scattering Theory for the Maxwell Equations and Electromagnetic Wave Scattering

Electromagnetic scattering is governed by the time‑harmonic Maxwell equations in a medium with permittivity ε(x) and permeability μ(x). For a monochromatic wave of frequency ω the electric field E satisfies the vector Helmholtz equation

∇×(μ-1∇×E) − ω²εE = 0.

In a homogeneous background (ε₀, μ₀) the incident field is a plane wave Einc(x) = E₀ eik·x with k = ω√(ε₀μ₀) n̂. The scattered field Esc satisfies the Silver‑Müller radiation condition at infinity. The scattering problem can be reformulated as a Lippmann‑Schwinger integral equation for the total field E = Einc + Esc:

E(x) = Einc(x) + ω² ∫ G₀(x,y) (ε(y)−ε₀) E(y) d³y − ∫ ∇×G₀(x,y) (μ-1(y)−μ₀-1) ∇×E(y) d³y,

where G₀ is the free dyadic Green tensor. The far‑field pattern E^∞(x̂) defines the scattering amplitude; the differential scattering cross section is dσ/dΩ = |E^∞(x̂)|²/|E₀|². The S‑matrix acts on the space of transverse vector fields on the unit sphere and is unitary for real ω when the medium is lossless.

For a spherical scatterer (Mie scattering) the problem separates in vector spherical harmonics. The S‑matrix becomes diagonal in the multipole indices (ℓ,m) and in the electric/magnetic (TE/TM) polarisation basis. The Mie coefficients a_ℓ, b_ℓ are rational functions of the spherical Bessel functions evaluated at the interior and exterior wavenumbers. They encode the full scattering information, including resonances (morphology‑dependent resonances) when the denominator nearly vanishes. The optical theorem relates the total cross section to the forward scattering amplitude: σ_tot = (4π/k) Im[E^∞(n̂)·E₀*].

For non‑spherical obstacles the T‑matrix (transition matrix) method expands the scattered field in vector spherical wave functions centred at an arbitrary origin. The T‑matrix relates the incident field coefficients to the scattered field coefficients and is independent of the incident direction. It satisfies the symmetry relation T = T^† for reciprocal media. Numerical computation of the T‑matrix (e.g. via the null‑field method or the discrete dipole approximation) is a standard tool in light scattering by particles. In the high‑frequency limit (k a ≫ 1) geometrical optics and the physical optics approximation give the leading contribution, while creeping waves and edge diffraction provide corrections described by the geometrical theory of diffraction.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Fractional Laplacian and Nonlocal Dispersion

The fractional Schrödinger equation replaces the kinetic energy −Δ by the fractional Laplacian (−Δ)α/2 with 0 < α ≤ 2. The free Hamiltonian H₀ = (−Δ)α/2 has dispersion relation E(k) = |k|^α. The resolvent R₀(z) = (H₀ − z)-1 is an integral operator with kernel decaying like |x−y|-(d+α) for large separation in d dimensions. Scattering theory for H = H₀ + V with a short‑range potential V (|V(x)| ≤ C⟨x⟩-β, β > α) can be developed using the limiting absorption principle for the fractional Laplacian. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e-iH₀t exist and are complete provided V satisfies a suitable roll‑off condition (e.g. β > (d+α)/2). The proof follows the Cook‑Kuroda method with the dispersive estimate ‖e-iH₀t‖L¹→L^∞ ≤ C|t|-d/α.

The stationary scattering matrix S(λ) acts on the energy shell {ξ ∈ ℝ^d : |ξ|^α = λ} which is a sphere of radius λ1/α. The scattering amplitude f(ω, ω'; λ) is defined via the asymptotics of the distorted plane waves. For α = 2 one recovers the standard theory; for α < 2 the energy surface is still a sphere but the density of states behaves like λd/α−1. The Born series converges for sufficiently small potentials in the norm of the weighted space L²s with s > 1/2. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) satisfies the Birman‑Krein formula det S(λ) = e-2πi ξ(λ) and can be expressed as a trace of the difference of spectral projections.

Resonances for the fractional Schrödinger operator are defined as poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (H − z)-1 from the upper half‑plane across the continuous spectrum onto the appropriate Riemann surface. For α < 2 the continuation is across the branch cut along [0,∞) because the free resolvent has a branch point at zero. Complex scaling (dilation analyticity) works for potentials that are dilation analytic; the rotated Hamiltonian H_θ = e-αθ(−Δ)α/2 + V(e^θ x) has discrete eigenvalues corresponding to resonances. The distribution of resonances near the real axis follows a Weyl‑type law with exponent d/α. These results are relevant for modelling wave propagation in disordered media with Lévy‑flight statistics and for the analysis of anomalous diffusion.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Random Potentials and Anderson Localization

Consider the random Schrödinger operator H_ω = −Δ + V_ω on ℓ²(ℤ^d) or L²(ℝ^d), where V_ω(x) = Σi q_i(ω) u(x−x_i) is an alloy‑type potential with independent identically distributed couplings q_i. The spectrum of H_ω is almost surely deterministic (by ergodicity) and consists of a pure point component with exponentially localised eigenfunctions at the band edges and for large disorder, and possibly an absolutely continuous component at the band centre for small disorder in d ≥ 3. Scattering theory in this context studies the behaviour of wave packets and the existence of wave operators for a fixed realisation ω.

For energies in the region of complete localisation the wave operators Ω±(ω) do not exist as strong limits on the whole Hilbert space because the absolutely continuous subspace is trivial. However, one can define the scattering matrix for finite‑volume approximations and study its statistical properties. The conductance in a mesoscopic sample is related to the Landauer‑Büttiker formula involving the transmission eigenvalues of the S‑matrix. In the localised regime the typical transmission decays exponentially with system size, T ∼ e-L/ξ, where ξ is the localisation length. The multiscale analysis of Fröhlich‑Spencer and the fractional moment method of Aizenman‑Molchanov provide rigorous proofs of exponential localisation for a wide class of random potentials at low energies or high disorder.

In the metallic regime (absolutely continuous spectrum) the wave operators exist and are complete. The spectral shift function ξ(λ; ω) is a random variable whose expectation gives the average density of states shift. The Thouless formula relates the Lyapunov exponent γ(E) (inverse localisation length in 1D) to the integrated density of states. For the 1D continuum model with a Poissonian random potential, the Lyapunov exponent can be computed exactly at low energy: γ(E) ∼ (m/ħ²) ⟨V²⟩ / (2E). In higher dimensions the scaling theory of localisation predicts a metal‑insulator transition at a critical disorder strength; the critical behaviour of the S‑matrix eigenvalues (the conductance distribution) is described by random matrix theory (orthogonal, unitary, or symplectic symmetry classes depending on time‑reversal and spin‑rotation invariance).

Recent developments include the study of scattering for random operators on graphs and the analysis of the S‑matrix for open quantum systems with random Hamiltonians (the Heidelberg approach). The supersymmetric sigma model and the DMPK equation describe the evolution of the transmission eigenvalue distribution with system length in quasi‑1D wires. These tools connect the microscopic random potential to macroscopic transport coefficients and provide a rigorous framework for the phenomenon of Anderson localisation.

  • Confusing the renormalisation parameter for 2D point interactions with the 3D scattering length; they have different dimensions and logarithmic versus linear energy dependence.
  • Neglecting the spin‑orbit term in the Pauli equation when the problem explicitly asks for spin‑flip amplitudes; the Zeeman term alone does not flip spin.
  • Applying the scalar optical theorem to electromagnetic scattering without accounting for the two polarisation states; the vector optical theorem involves the dyadic forward amplitude.
  • Assuming the fractional Laplacian has the same dispersive decay as the standard Laplacian; the decay exponent is d/α, not d/2.
  • Treating the random S‑matrix as a deterministic object; its eigenvalues are random variables whose distribution must be analysed statistically.
  • Overlooking the need for a self‑adjoint extension parameter in the 1D δ‑potential when the coupling constant is taken to infinity; the limit yields a Dirichlet condition, not a free particle.
  • Forgetting that the Mie coefficients a_ℓ, b_ℓ are derived for a sphere with isotropic ε, μ; for anisotropic or chiral spheres the S‑matrix couples electric and magnetic multipoles.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Integral Type

Nonlocal potentials of integral type appear in nuclear physics, condensed matter, and the description of effective interactions in low‑energy effective field theories. The Hamiltonian takes the form H = H₀ + V where (Vψ)(x) = ∫ K(x,y) ψ(y) dy with a kernel K that is typically symmetric, square‑integrable, and decays sufficiently fast at infinity. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e-iH₀t exist and are complete under the condition that K belongs to the Hilbert‑Schmidt class on L²(ℝ^d) and that the free resolvent (H₀−z)-1 maps L² into a weighted space where the kernel is compact. The stationary scattering matrix S(λ) = 1 − 2πi T(λ) is expressed through the T‑operator T(λ) = V + V (H₀−λ−i0)-1 T(λ). Because V is an integral operator, the Lippmann–Schwinger equation for the scattering solutions becomes a Fredholm integral equation of the second kind, which can be solved by Neumann series when ‖K‖HS is small. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) is given by the trace of the difference of spectral projections and can be computed via the Birman–Krein formula det S(λ) = e-2πi ξ(λ). For kernels with a separable structure K(x,y) = Σj=1^N g_j(x) g_j(y) the S‑matrix reduces to a finite‑dimensional matrix, allowing explicit formulas for phase shifts and bound‑state energies. In the case of a nonlocal potential with a hard core, the kernel may contain a singular part that requires a self‑adjoint extension parameter; the resulting scattering length depends logarithmically on the cutoff in two dimensions and linearly in three dimensions. Applications include the description of nucleon‑nucleon scattering with nonlocal separable potentials (Yamaguchi, Ernst–Shakin–Thaler) and the modelling of electron‑phonon interactions in superconductors where the kernel encodes the retarded interaction.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Non‑Abelian Gauge Fields

When a particle carries an internal degree of freedom transforming under a compact Lie group G (e.g. colour SU(3) or isospin SU(2)), the minimal coupling replaces the momentum operator p by p − A where A = A^a T^a takes values in the Lie algebra 𝔤. The Hamiltonian H = (p−A)²/2m + V acts on vector‑valued wavefunctions ψ: ℝ^d → ℂ^N.
