The gauge covariance of the theory implies that the S‑matrix intertwines the free and interacting representations of the asymptotic symmetry group. Wave operators are defined on the tensor product ℋ = L²(ℝ^d) ⊗ ℂ^N and satisfy the intertwining property Ω± H₀ = H Ω±. The stationary scattering matrix S(λ) becomes a matrix‑valued function on the energy shell, acting on the internal space. For a pure gauge field A = U-1 dU with U: ℝ^d → G, the Hamiltonian is unitarily equivalent to the free one, so the S‑matrix is trivial up to a gauge transformation at infinity; non‑trivial scattering arises from gauge‑invariant field strengths Fμν = ∂_μ A_ν − ∂_ν A_μ + [A_μ, A_ν]. In the high‑energy (eikonal) limit the scattering amplitude factorises into a path‑ordered exponential (Wilson line) along the classical trajectory: Sab(b) = P exp(i ∫ A_μ dx^μ) where b is the impact parameter. This Wilson line encodes the non‑Abelian Aharonov–Bohm effect and leads to colour‑exchange amplitudes. The optical theorem generalises to Im Taa(θ=0) = (k/4π) Σ_b |Tab(θ=0)|², summing over final internal states. For asymptotically free gauge theories the S‑matrix exhibits Regge poles associated with gluon exchange; the leading Regge trajectory is linear in the momentum transfer squared, J(t) = α₀ + α' t, with α' determined by the string tension. In lattice gauge theory the scattering matrix is extracted from the asymptotic behaviour of Euclidean correlation functions via the Lüscher formula, which relates finite‑volume energy levels to infinite‑volume phase shifts. The non‑Abelian nature also modifies the Birman–Schwinger principle: the kernel becomes matrix‑valued and the bound‑state condition involves the determinant of 1 + V1/2 (H₀−E)-1 V1/2 in the combined space.

Worked Example: Non‑Abelian Eikonal Phase
Consider a high‑energy quark scattering off a static non‑Abelian gauge field A_i^a(x) = δi3 δa3 f(x_⊥) where x_⊥ = (x¹,x²) and f is a smooth profile. Compute the eikonal S‑matrix element for a quark in the fundamental representation of SU(3) with impact parameter b = (b¹,b²).
The eikonal phase is the path‑ordered exponential along the straight line x³ = vt, x_⊥ = b. Since A_i points only in the colour 3 direction, the colour matrices commute and path ordering is trivial. The Wilson line reduces to exp(i g T3 ∫-∞∞ f(b) dx³) = exp(i g T3 L f(b)) where L is the longitudinal extent (formally infinite, but the phase is defined modulo 2π). For a quark in the fundamental representation T3 = diag(1/2, -1/2, 0). Thus the S‑matrix is diagonal in colour space with entries exp(i g L f(b)/2), exp(-i g L f(b)/2), 1. The scattering amplitude is the Fourier transform of S(b)−1 with respect to b, yielding colour‑dependent diffraction peaks. The optical theorem is satisfied because Im Taa(0) = (k/4π) Σ_b |Tab(0)|² holds for each colour component separately.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the S‑matrix in a non‑Abelian gauge background, first identify whether the gauge field is pure gauge (then S is a gauge transformation) or has non‑zero field strength (then use the Wilson line). Remember that the colour matrices in the fundamental representation satisfy Tr(T^a T^b) = ½ δab. For high‑energy eikonal scattering, the amplitude factorises into a product of a colour matrix and a scalar impact‑parameter integral. Write the optical theorem in matrix form and check that the sum over final internal states is explicit.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time‑Dependent Potentials and the Kato–Yajima Theory

Time‑dependent potentials V(t,x) that decay sufficiently fast as |t|→∞ allow a scattering theory parallel to the stationary case. The Kato–Yajima approach constructs wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ U(t,0) U₀(0,t) where U(t,s) is the propagator for H(t) = H₀ + V(t) and U₀(t,s) = e-iH₀(t-s). Existence and completeness hold if ∫-∞∞ ‖V(t) (H₀+1)-1/2‖ dt < ∞ (the Kato smoothness condition) and V(t) is strongly continuous in t. The scattering operator S = Ω+* Ω- is unitary on the absolutely continuous subspace of H₀. For periodic V(t+T)=V(t) the Floquet theory replaces the energy λ by the quasi‑energy ε ∈ [0, 2π/T) and the S‑matrix becomes a block‑diagonal operator on the extended Hilbert space L²(𝕋_T) ⊗ L²(ℝ^d). The stationary representation uses the Floquet Hamiltonian K = H(t) − i∂_t acting on the Sambe space; its spectrum consists of bands ε_n(k) and the scattering matrix relates incoming and outgoing Floquet channels. In the adiabatic limit where V(t) varies slowly, the S‑matrix acquires a geometric phase (Berry connection) in addition to the dynamical phase. For potentials with a Coulomb tail V(t,x) ∼ Z(t)/|x| the Dollard modification must be combined with the time‑dependent phase factor exp(−i ∫^t Z(s) log|s| ds). The spectral shift function for time‑dependent Hamiltonians is defined via the trace of the difference of the spectral measures of the unitary groups U(t) and U₀(t); it satisfies a Birman–Krein type formula det S = exp(−2πi ξ) where ξ is now a function on the quasi‑energy torus. Applications include laser‑assisted scattering (above‑threshold ionisation) where the S‑matrix elements are expressed in terms of Bessel functions of the field amplitude, and quantum control problems where the goal is to shape V(t) to achieve a target S‑matrix.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Manifolds with Boundary and the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann Map

Let (M,g) be a complete non‑compact Riemannian manifold with a compact boundary ∂M. The Hamiltonian H = −Δ_g acts on L²(M) with self‑adjoint boundary conditions (Dirichlet, Neumann, or Robin). The scattering matrix relates incoming and outgoing waves on the cylindrical ends of M. A powerful tool is the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann (DtN) map Λ(λ): H1/2(∂M) → H-1/2(∂M) defined by Λ(λ) f = ∂_ν u|∂M where u solves (−Δ_g − λ) u = 0 in M with u|∂M = f and the outgoing radiation condition at infinity. The poles of Λ(λ) in the lower half‑plane correspond to resonances of the interior problem. The scattering matrix S(λ) on the continuous spectrum can be expressed as S(λ) = (Λ(λ) − i√λ) (Λ(λ) + i√λ)-1 after a suitable identification of the boundary data with the asymptotic spherical harmonics on the ends. For manifolds with asymptotically Euclidean ends, the DtN map is a pseudodifferential operator of order 1 on ∂M, and its principal symbol is √{λ − |ξ|_g²}. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) equals (1/π) Im Tr log(Λ(λ) + i√λ) and satisfies a trace formula involving the length spectrum of trapped geodesics. In the presence of a boundary, the Lax‑Phillips framework is realised by taking the incoming/outgoing subspaces as the images of the wave group restricted to the boundary. The scattering operator becomes a unitary operator on L²(ℝ_+ × ∂M) and its translation representation yields the Lax‑Phillips semigroup Z(t) = P_+ U(t) P_- where P_± are the orthogonal projections onto the outgoing/incoming subspaces. The generator of Z(t) has spectrum contained in the lower half‑plane, and its eigenvalues are precisely the resonances. This construction extends to manifolds with corners and to the wave equation with variable coefficients, providing a geometric interpretation of the S‑matrix as the boundary value of the resolvent.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Singular Magnetic Fields and the Aharonov–Bohm Effect

A singular magnetic field is a vector potential A with a distributional curl, e.g. A = (Φ/2πr) e_θ in two dimensions, producing a magnetic flux Φ confined to a line (the Aharonov–Bohm solenoid). The Hamiltonian H = (p−A)²/2m on ℝ²{0} is essentially self‑adjoint on C_c^∞(ℝ²{0}) only after a choice of self‑adjoint extension parameterised by a phase α ∈ [0,2π) at the origin. The scattering solutions are partial waves ψ_m(r,θ) = eimθ R_m(r) with effective angular momentum m − Φ/Φ₀ where Φ₀ = 2π/e is the flux quantum. The S‑matrix is diagonal in the angular momentum basis with elements S_m = e2iδ_m, where the phase shift δ_m = (π/2)(|m−Φ/Φ₀| − |m|) for the ideal solenoid. The differential cross section dσ/dθ = (1/2πk) |Σ_m (e2iδ_m−1) eimθ|² exhibits a characteristic 1/ sin²(θ/2) singularity in the forward direction, reflecting the long‑range nature of the vector potential. For a finite‑radius solenoid with a regularised field, the phase shifts acquire an additional dependence on the interior boundary condition, and the S‑matrix approaches the ideal limit as the radius shrinks. The Aharonov–Bohm effect demonstrates that the S‑matrix depends on the gauge‑invariant flux even though the magnetic field vanishes on the particle’s path; this is a topological scattering phenomenon. In three dimensions a magnetic flux tube produces a similar effect with partial waves labelled by the projection of angular momentum on the tube axis. The spectral shift function for the Aharonov–Bohm Hamiltonian is ξ(λ) = (1/π) Σ_m (δ_m(λ) − δ_m(0)) and can be expressed in terms of the digamma function. The Lax‑Phillips semigroup for this system has a generator whose eigenvalues are the complex resonances associated with the self‑adjoint extension parameter; these resonances lie on a line parallel to the real axis and their spacing is determined by the flux. Experimental realisations include electron interferometry around a superconducting vortex and scattering of cold atoms by a synthetic gauge field.

  • Confusing the Kato smoothness condition for time‑dependent potentials with the stationary Kato condition; the former involves an integral over time of the operator norm, the latter a supremum over energy.
  • Assuming the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map is always a pseudodifferential operator of order 1; on manifolds with trapped geodesics it may have a more complicated singularity structure.
  • Neglecting the self‑adjoint extension parameter in the Aharonov–Bohm problem; the scattering phase shifts depend explicitly on this parameter and the ideal solenoid corresponds to a specific choice.
  • Treating the non‑Abelian Wilson line as an ordinary exponential when the gauge field does not commute at different points; path ordering is essential and cannot be dropped.
  • Applying the standard optical theorem to a multichannel S‑matrix without summing over all open internal channels; the imaginary part of the forward amplitude equals the sum of squared moduli over all final states.
  • Overlooking the need for a Dollard‑type phase factor when the time‑dependent potential has a long‑range Coulomb tail; the asymptotic dynamics is not free but includes a logarithmic phase.
  • Assuming the spectral shift function for a time‑dependent Hamiltonian is a function of energy alone; it lives on the quasi‑energy torus and must be integrated over the period to obtain a scalar invariant.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Relativistic Corrections and the Foldy‑Wouthuysen Transformation

The non‑relativistic Schrödinger Hamiltonian H = p²/2m + V(x) receives systematic corrections when the particle’s kinetic energy approaches its rest mass energy mc². The Foldy‑Wouthuysen (FW) transformation provides a unitary block‑diagonalisation of the Dirac Hamiltonian HD = c α·p + βmc² + V(x) into even and odd operators with respect to the Dirac matrices. Expanding the transformed Hamiltonian in powers of 1/c yields the Pauli‑FW Hamiltonian

HFW = mc² + p²/2m + V − p⁴/8m³c² + (ℏ/4m²c²) σ·(∇V × p) + (ℏ²/8m²c²) ∇²V + O(1/c⁴).

The term −p⁴/8m³c² is the relativistic kinetic‑energy correction; the spin‑orbit term (ℏ/4m²c²) σ·(∇V × p) couples the particle’s spin to the orbital motion; the Darwin term (ℏ²/8m²c²) ∇²V accounts for Zitterbewegung smearing. In scattering theory these corrections modify the free resolvent R₀(z) = (H₀ − z)⁻¹ and the T‑matrix T(z) = V + VR₀(z)T(z). The first‑order Born amplitude acquires an extra factor (1 − p²/2m²c²) from the kinetic correction, while the spin‑orbit term generates a spin‑dependent amplitude proportional to σ·(k′ × k) where k, k′ are incoming and outgoing momenta. The differential cross section for unpolarised beams becomes

dσ/dΩ = |fC(θ)|² + (ℏ²/4m²c²) |∇V(q)|² sin²θ + O(1/c⁴),

with q = k′ − k the momentum transfer. For a Coulomb potential V(r) = −Ze²/r the spin‑orbit contribution reproduces the Mott formula, and the Darwin term yields a contact interaction that regularises the forward singularity. The FW framework also clarifies the domain of the perturbed Hamiltonian: the relativistic corrections are relatively bounded with respect to p²/2m with bound < 1, guaranteeing self‑adjointness on the Sobolev space H²(ℝ³). Wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e−iH₀t exist and are complete for short‑range potentials satisfying ∫ (1+|x|) |V(x)| dx < ∞, and the S‑matrix S = Ω+⁎ Ω− inherits the spin‑dependent structure. In the partial‑wave expansion the phase shifts δl(k) receive additive corrections δl(1)(k) = −(k⁴/8m³c²) ∫₀^∞ r² jl(kr)² dr + spin‑orbit terms that split the degeneracy between total angular momentum j = l ± ½. These results underpin precision electron‑atom scattering calculations and the theoretical description of fine‑structure effects in low‑energy nuclear physics.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with External Time‑Dependent Fields and the Keldysh Formalism

When the Hamiltonian contains an explicitly time‑dependent external field V(x,t) = V₀(x) + V₁(x) cos(ωt), the standard stationary scattering theory must be replaced by a Floquet‑Keldysh approach. The total Hamiltonian H(t) = H₀ + V(t) generates a unitary propagator U(t,t₀) satisfying i∂tU = H(t)U. The Floquet theorem asserts that solutions can be written as ψα(x,t) = e−iεαt φα(x,t) with φα periodic in t of period T = 2π/ω. The quasienergies εα are defined modulo ω and form the spectrum of the Floquet Hamiltonian HF = H(t) − i∂t acting on the extended Hilbert space ℋ ⊗ L²([0,T]). The scattering operator is defined on this space as S = Ω+⁎ Ω− where the wave operators now involve the full propagator: Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ U(0,t) U₀(t,0). The Keldysh contour formalism provides a systematic perturbation theory for the non‑equilibrium Green’s functions GR/A/K(x,t;x′,t′) which encode the retarded, advanced and Keldysh components. The Dyson equation on the contour reads G = G₀ + G₀ Σ G, where the self‑energy Σ contains the interaction with the time‑dependent field. For a monochromatic drive the self‑energy acquires side‑band structure Σ(ε) = Σ₀(ε) + Σ₁(ε−ω) + Σ₁(ε+ω) + …, leading to photon‑assisted scattering amplitudes. The transition probability from an initial asymptotic state |k⟩ to a final state |k′⟩ with absorption/emission of n photons is given by the squared modulus of the Floquet T‑matrix element Tk′k(n) = ⟨k′| Vn + Vn G₀(ε+nω) V₀ + … |k⟩, where Vn are the Fourier components of V₁(x) cos(ωt). The differential cross section summed over all open photon channels reads

dσ/dΩ = Σn |fn(θ)|² δ(εf − εi − nω).

In the high‑frequency limit ω ≫ |V₁| the effective Hamiltonian can be obtained via a Magnus expansion, yielding a time‑averaged potential plus a micromotion correction that modifies the scattering length. For strong low‑frequency fields the Keldysh parameter γ = √(2mIp)/(|e|E₀/ω) distinguishes the multiphoton regime (γ ≫ 1) from the tunnelling regime (γ ≪ 1). The S‑matrix remains unitary on the extended space, and its analytic continuation in the complex quasienergy plane reveals resonances associated with field‑induced bound states (dressed states). The formalism has been rigorously justified for short‑range potentials satisfying the Yafaev condition ∫ (1+|x|) |V(x,t)| dx < ∞ uniformly in t, and for periodic drives the existence of wave operators follows from the Mourre estimate applied to the Floquet Hamiltonian. Applications include laser‑assisted electron‑atom collisions, transport in driven quantum dots, and Floquet topological insulators where the scattering matrix acquires topological invariants.

Deriving the first‑order relativistic correction to the scattering amplitude
Consider a spin‑½ particle of mass m scattering off a central potential V(r) = −Ze²/r. Using the Foldy‑Wouthuysen Hamiltonian up to order 1/c², compute the correction to the Born amplitude fB(θ) = −(2m/ℏ²) ∫ e−iq·x V(r) d³x arising from the kinetic‑energy term −p⁴/8m³c². Express the corrected amplitude f(θ) = fB(θ) + δf(θ) in terms of the momentum transfer q = 2k sin(θ/2).
The kinetic correction contributes a perturbation W = −p⁴/8m³c². In the first Born approximation the correction to the amplitude is δf(θ) = −(2m/ℏ²) ⟨k′| W |k⟩. Since p²|k⟩ = ℏ²k²|k⟩, we have ⟨k′| p⁴ |k⟩ = ℏ⁴ k⁴ δ(k′−k). However the matrix element between different momenta is obtained by inserting a complete set of plane waves: ⟨k′| p⁴ |k⟩ = ∫ d³p ⟨k′| p⁴ |p⟩ ⟨p|k⟩ = ℏ⁴ ∫ d³p p⁴ δ(k′−p) δ(p−k) = ℏ⁴ k⁴ δ(k′−k). For k′ ≠ k the diagonal matrix element vanishes, so the kinetic correction does not contribute to the off‑diagonal Born amplitude at this order. The leading relativistic modification of the Coulomb scattering amplitude therefore comes from the spin‑orbit and Darwin terms, not from the kinetic term. The corrected amplitude is f(θ) = fB(θ) + (ℏ²/4m²c²) (Ze²) (k²/q²) sin²θ + O(1/c⁴), which reproduces the Mott formula.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the first relativistic correction to a scattering amplitude, identify which term in the Foldy‑Wouthuysen Hamiltonian contributes at the required order. The kinetic correction is diagonal in momentum space and often vanishes for off‑diagonal matrix elements; the spin‑orbit and Darwin terms generate the observable angular dependence. Write the perturbation explicitly, evaluate the matrix element using plane‑wave states, and keep track of factors of ℏ, c and m. A quick dimensional check (amplitude has dimension of length) catches missing constants.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Asymptotically Hyperbolic Manifolds

Let (M,g) be a complete non‑compact Riemannian manifold of dimension n ≥ 2 whose metric is asymptotically hyperbolic: near the conformal boundary ∂M there exists a defining function x such that g = x⁻² (dx² + h(x)) with h(x) a smooth family of metrics on ∂M. The Laplace‑Beltrami operator Δg has essential spectrum [ (n−1)²/4 , ∞ ) and no embedded eigenvalues for generic metrics. The resolvent R(λ) = (Δg − λ)⁻¹, initially defined for Im λ > 0, admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane as an operator between weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,δ(M). Its poles are the resonances of the manifold. The scattering matrix S(λ) is defined via the asymptotic expansion of generalized eigenfunctions: for λ = s(n−1−s) with Re s > (n−1)/2, any solution of (Δg − λ)u = 0 behaves as u ∼ xn−1−s f− + xs f+ near ∂M, where f± ∈ C∞(∂M). The Poisson operator P(λ) maps boundary data f− to the unique solution with prescribed leading term, and the scattering operator is S(λ) = P(λ)⁎ P(λ) acting on L²(∂M). It is a unitary pseudodifferential operator of order 0 whose principal symbol is the identity. The scattering phase s(λ) = (1/2i) log det S(λ) satisfies a trace formula involving the length spectrum of closed geodesics (the Selberg trace formula for hyperbolic quotients). Resonances correspond to poles of the meromorphically continued scattering matrix; their distribution obeys a Weyl law N(r) ∼ C rn for convex co‑compact hyperbolic manifolds. The Lax‑Phillips framework is realised by taking the incoming/outgoing subspaces as the images of the wave group restricted to the boundary, yielding a translation representation on L²(ℝ × ∂M) and a semigroup Z(t) = P+ U(t) P− whose generator has spectrum exactly the resonance set. This geometric scattering theory underpins the study of quantum chaos on negatively curved spaces, the AdS/CFT correspondence where the boundary CFT correlators are given by the scattering matrix, and the analysis of decay of waves on black‑hole spacetimes via the conformal compactification of Kerr‑de Sitter metrics.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Non‑Local Nonlinearity and the Gross‑Pitaevskii Interaction

The Gross‑Pitaevskii equation i∂tψ = (−½Δ + Vext + g|ψ|²) ψ describes a dilute Bose‑Einstein condensate at zero temperature. The non‑local nonlinearity g|ψ|²ψ is a Hartree‑type interaction with a contact potential. Scattering theory for this nonlinear equation seeks asymptotic states ψ±(t) = e−iH₀t φ± such that ‖ψ(t) − ψ±(t)‖H¹ → 0 as t → ±∞. For small initial data in H¹(ℝ³) with ‖ψ₀‖H¹ < ε, the wave operators Ω± : φ± ↦ ψ₀ exist and are homeomorphisms onto a neighbourhood of the origin. The proof uses Strichartz estimates for the linear propagator e−iH₀t and a contraction mapping in the space X = C(ℝ; H¹) ∩ L⁴t L⁸x. The nonlinear term is treated as a perturbation: the Duhamel formula ψ(t) = e−iH₀t φ± − i ∫±∞t e−iH₀(t−s) (g|ψ|²ψ)(s) ds defines a fixed‑point problem. The scattering operator S = Ω+⁻¹ Ω− maps asymptotic profiles φ− to φ+ and is a nonlinear unitary map on the energy space. For large data the dynamics exhibits soliton resolution: any solution with energy below the threshold for blow‑up decomposes into a sum of solitary waves (ground states of the stationary equation −½ΔQ + gQ³ = ωQ) plus a dispersive radiation term that scatters to a linear solution. The linearised operator around a solitary wave L+ = −½Δ + ω − 3gQ², L− = −½Δ + ω − gQ² has a simple negative eigenvalue, a kernel generated by translation invariance, and the rest of the spectrum positive. This spectral structure yields orbital stability of the solitary wave and allows the construction of wave operators on the centre‑stable manifold. The inverse scattering transform for the integrable cubic NLS in one dimension provides an explicit nonlinear Fourier transform: the scattering data consist of a reflection coefficient r(k) and discrete eigenvalues κj with norming constants. The time evolution of the scattering data is trivial (r(k,t) = r(k,0) e2ik²t, κj constant), and the solution is reconstructed via a Riemann‑Hilbert problem. In higher dimensions the problem is non‑integrable, but the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Kenig‑Merle yields global well‑posedness and scattering for energy‑critical NLS (power 1+4/(n−2)). The scattering amplitude for the nonlinear problem can be defined via the asymptotic expansion of the solution in the interaction picture, leading to a nonlinear Born series where each term involves multiple integrals of the nonlinearity against the free propagator. This framework connects to the theory of wave turbulence, where the kinetic equation for the wave action spectrum is derived from the nonlinear scattering operator under random phase assumptions.

  • Confusing the Foldy‑Wouthuysen kinetic correction with the spin‑orbit term; the former is diagonal in momentum space and often gives no off‑diagonal Born contribution.
  • Neglecting the side‑band structure of the Keldysh self‑energy when computing photon‑assisted cross sections; each side band corresponds to a distinct photon number channel.
  • Assuming the scattering matrix on an asymptotically hyperbolic manifold is a standard pseudodifferential operator of order 0 without checking the conformal compactness of the metric.
  • Treating the Gross‑Pitaevskii nonlinearity as a perturbation for arbitrarily large data; the contraction argument only works for small H¹ norms.
  • Overlooking the spectral condition on the linearised operator L+ when proving orbital stability of solitary waves; a simple negative eigenvalue is essential.
  • These traps highlight the most frequent errors in advanced scattering theory examinations.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Hardy‑Type Singular Potentials and Critical Coupling

The Hardy inequality ∫ |∇u|² dx ≥ ((n−2)/2)² ∫ |u|²/|x|² dx for n ≥ 3 sets a sharp threshold for the inverse‑square potential V(x)=λ/|x|². When the coupling constant λ satisfies λ ≤ (n−2)²/4 the quadratic form is bounded below and the Hamiltonian H = −½Δ + λ/|x|² is essentially self‑adjoint on C₀^∞(ℝⁿ{0}). In this subcritical regime the wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiHt e−iH₀t exist and are complete; the absolutely continuous spectrum of H coincides with [0,∞) and the scattering matrix S(λ) is a unitary operator on the sphere Sn−1 that differs from the identity by a phase factor depending on λ and the angular momentum quantum number ℓ. At the critical coupling λ = (n−2)²/4 the Hardy constant is saturated; the Hamiltonian ceases to be essentially self‑adjoint and a one‑parameter family of self‑adjoint extensions appears, each characterised by a boundary condition at the origin. The scattering matrix for these extensions acquires an additional energy‑dependent phase shift that can be computed via the Birman–Schwinger principle: the resolvent difference (H−z)−1−(H₀−z)−1 is trace class for Im z ≠ 0, and its determinant yields the Jost function whose zeros correspond to bound states. For λ > (n−2)²/4 the potential is too singular; the Hamiltonian is not semi‑bounded and the standard time‑dependent scattering theory breaks down unless a renormalisation (e.g. a three‑dimensional delta‑shell regularisation) is introduced. These results, due to Kalf, Walter, and later to Gesztesy–Simon, illustrate how the Hardy threshold governs both the existence of wave operators and the analytic structure of the S‑matrix.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Manifolds with Cylindrical Ends and the Poisson Operator

Let (M,g) be a complete non‑compact Riemannian manifold that outside a compact set is isometric to a cylinder ℝ₊ × Y with product metric dt² + h, where (Y,h) is a closed (n−1)‑dimensional manifold. The Laplacian Δ_g on such a manifold has absolutely continuous spectrum [0,∞) with possible embedded eigenvalues. The Poisson operator P(λ) maps boundary data f ∈ C^∞(Y) to the unique solution u of (−Δ_g − λ)u = 0 that behaves like ei√λ t f(y) as t → +∞. Its Schwartz kernel is a Legendrian distribution associated to the geodesic flow on the cylinder, and the scattering matrix S(λ) = P(−λ)* P(λ) is a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 on Y. Melrose’s b‑calculus provides a systematic framework: the resolvent (Δ_g − λ)−1 extends meromorphically to the complex plane as a family of b‑pseudodifferential operators, and its poles are the resonances. The scattering matrix admits an asymptotic expansion S(λ) ∼ I + iλ−1/2 A₁ + λ−1 A₂ + … where the coefficients A_j are pseudodifferential operators determined by the geometry of Y. In quantum waveguide applications, Y is a cross‑section of a thin tube; the low‑energy scattering matrix reduces to a finite‑dimensional unitary matrix acting on the propagating modes, and the resonance poles correspond to quasi‑bound states trapped by bends or constrictions. The Poisson operator also enters the inverse scattering problem: knowledge of S(λ) for all λ > 0 determines the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map on Y, which in turn determines the metric g on the cylindrical end up to a gauge transformation.

Worked Example: Low‑Energy Scattering on a Cylindrical Waveguide
Consider the Laplacian on ℝ × (0,π) with Dirichlet boundary conditions. Compute the leading term of the scattering matrix S(k) as k → 0 for the first transverse mode.
The transverse eigenfunctions are φ_m(y)=√(2/π) sin(my) with eigenvalues m². For energy λ=k², only modes with m ≤ k propagate. As k→0 only m=1 propagates. The Poisson kernel for mode 1 is P₁(k; t,y)=e^{i√{k²−1} t} φ₁(y). The scattering matrix is a 1×1 unitary scalar S₁(k)=e2iδ₁(k). Matching the incoming and outgoing waves at t=0 gives the phase shift δ₁(k)=−arctan(√{1−k²}/k). Expanding for small k yields δ₁(k)∼−π/2 + k + O(k³). Hence S₁(k)∼−1 + 2ik + O(k²). The leading term −1 reflects the Dirichlet boundary condition at the waveguide entrance.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for the low‑energy asymptotics of a scattering matrix on a cylindrical manifold, first identify the propagating transverse modes. Write the Poisson operator for each mode, impose the boundary condition at the junction, and extract the phase shift. The leading term is always a diagonal unitary matrix whose entries are e2iδ_m(0); compute δ_m(0) from the transverse eigenvalue and the geometry of the junction.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Derivative Nonlinearities and the Gauge Transformation

The derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation (DNLS) i∂_t ψ = −½Δ ψ + i α (ψ̄ ∇ψ − ψ ∇ψ̄)·∇ψ appears in plasma physics and optical fibres. The derivative nonlinearity is long‑range in the sense that the standard wave operators fail to exist for generic small data. The gauge transformation ψ = u exp(i α ∫−∞^x |u|² dx') (in one dimension) or its higher‑dimensional analogues converts DNLS into a nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a nonlocal cubic term and a magnetic potential. After the transformation the equation reads i∂_t u = −½(∇ − i α |u|²)² u + lower‑order terms. The new nonlinearity is of Hartree type with a Coulomb‑like kernel, for which the scattering theory is better understood. Hayashi and Ozawa proved that for small initial data in H1,1(ℝ) the modified wave operators Ω±^mod = s‑limt→±∞ eiH₀t G(t) e−iHt exist, where G(t) is a time‑dependent phase correction G(t) = exp(i α ∫−∞^x |u(t)|² dx'). The asymptotic profile u_± satisfies u(t) = e−iH₀t u_± + o(1) in H¹. The scattering operator S = (Ω+^mod)−1 Ω−^mod is unitary on the energy space. In dimensions n ≥ 2 the gauge transformation involves a nonlocal vector potential A = α ∇(−Δ)−1 |u|², and the modified wave operators require a second‑order phase correction to cancel the long‑range magnetic tail. The method extends to the Chen–Lee–Liu equation and to the derivative NLS with a gauge‑invariant nonlinearity i ψ̄ ∇ψ·∇ψ, where the gauge transformation is replaced by a pseudo‑differential conjugation.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Hartree Type and the Boson Star Dynamics

The Hartree equation i∂_t ψ = −½Δ ψ + (V * |ψ|²) ψ with V(x)=±|x|−γ (0 < γ < n) models the mean‑field limit of many‑body bosonic systems. For repulsive potentials (V ≥ 0) and small data in H¹(ℝⁿ), the wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eiH₀t e−iHt exist and are asymptotically complete; the proof uses Strichartz estimates and the fact that the Hartree nonlinearity is energy‑subcritical for γ < 2. For the attractive Coulomb case V(x)=−|x|−1 in three dimensions, the equation admits standing wave solutions ψ(t,x)=eiωt φ_ω(x) known as boson stars. The linearised operator around φ_ω has a simple negative eigenvalue, a kernel generated by the gauge symmetry, and the rest of the spectrum positive. This spectral structure yields orbital stability of the boson star for ω in a certain interval (Weinstein’s criterion). Scattering for data with energy below the ground state threshold follows from the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Kenig–Merle: any solution either scatters to a linear solution or converges to a modulated boson star. The scattering operator S maps the asymptotic profile φ_− to φ_+ and is a nonlinear unitary map on the energy space. In the many‑body context, the Hartree dynamics approximates the N‑body Schrödinger evolution with an error of order 1/N in trace norm, and the scattering matrix of the Hartree equation coincides with the limit of the N‑body S‑matrix as N→∞.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time‑Dependent Metrics and Geometric Optics Approximation

Consider the Schrödinger equation i∂_t ψ = −½Δg(t) ψ on a manifold (M,g(t)) where the metric g(t) varies slowly in time, e.g. g(t)=a(t)² g₀ with a scale factor a(t) satisfying |ȧ/a| ≪ 1. In the adiabatic regime the instantaneous eigenfunctions of −½Δg(t) form a time‑dependent basis. The geometric optics (WKB) ansatz ψ(t,x)=A(t,x) ei S(t,x)/ε with ε→0 leads to the Hamilton–Jacobi equation ∂_t S + ½|∇g(t) S|g(t)² = 0 and a transport equation for the amplitude A along the Hamiltonian flow. The scattering matrix S_ε(λ) relating incoming and outgoing WKB states is a Fourier integral operator associated to the canonical relation generated by the classical scattering map on the cotangent bundle T*M. As ε→0 the principal symbol of S_ε is the classical scattering amplitude, while subprincipal symbols encode the Maslov index and the Berry phase accumulated along the trajectory. For cosmological particle production, the time‑dependent metric is a Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker spacetime; the Bogoliubov coefficients β_k between in‑ and out‑modes give the particle number density n_k = |β_k|². The scattering matrix in this setting is diagonal in the comoving momentum basis, and its modulus squared yields the spectrum of created particles. Rigorous results by Dimock and Kay show that for metrics with sufficiently regular time dependence the wave operators exist and the S‑matrix is a unitary operator on the one‑particle Hilbert space, with the geometric optics approximation providing the leading semiclassical asymptotics.

  • Confusing the critical Hardy constant with the subcritical regime; the Hamiltonian is not essentially self‑adjoint at the critical value and requires a choice of self‑adjoint extension.
  • Assuming the Poisson operator on a cylindrical end is a standard pseudodifferential operator; it is a Legendrian distribution associated to the geodesic flow.
  • Neglecting the gauge‑dependent phase correction when constructing modified wave operators for derivative NLS; the standard wave operators do not exist without it.
  • Treating the Hartree nonlinearity as a short‑range perturbation for all γ; the Coulomb case γ=1 is long‑range and demands modified wave operators.
  • Applying the geometric optics approximation without verifying the adiabatic condition |ȧ/a| ≪ 1; otherwise the WKB expansion breaks down and the scattering matrix is not a Fourier integral operator.
  • Overlooking the Maslov index contribution in the semiclassical scattering amplitude; it determines the correct phase of the Bogoliubov coefficients.
  • Each of these traps highlights a subtle but decisive distinction that examiners frequently test in advanced scattering theory questions.

Scattering Theory for the Energy‑Critical Quintic Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation

The energy‑critical nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) in three spatial dimensions reads

i∂tu + Δu = ±|u|4u, u(0)=u0 ∈ Ḣ1(ℝ3).

The scaling symmetry uλ(t,x)=λ1/2u(λ2t,λx) leaves the homogeneous Ḣ1 norm invariant, which makes the quintic power the borderline case for the conservation of energy. The defocusing sign (+) admits global well‑posedness and scattering for all finite‑energy data, a result proved by Colliander, Keel, Staffilani, Takaoka and Tao (2008) using the induction‑on‑energy method combined with the concentration‑compactness/rigidity scheme of Kenig and Merle. For the focusing sign (−) the situation is more delicate: solutions with energy below the ground state threshold and with ‖∇u0‖L2 < ‖∇Q‖L2 (where Q is the unique positive radial solution of −ΔQ+Q=Q5) are global and scatter (Duyckaerts, Holmer and Roudenko, 2008). Above this threshold one can have finite‑time blow‑up or soliton‑like dynamics. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ e−itΔ eitH exist on the subspace of scattering states and are asymptotically complete in the energy space. The scattering operator S = (Ω+)*Ω− is a nonlinear unitary map on Ḣ1. Recent work by Dodson (2015) extended global well‑posedness and scattering to the non‑radial, defocusing case in dimensions d≥4, while the three‑dimensional non‑radial case was settled by Killip and Visan (2010) using a refined interaction Morawetz estimate. In the focusing case, the classification of minimal‑mass blow‑up solutions by Merle and Raphaël (2004) shows that any non‑scattering solution must concentrate at least the mass of the ground state. These results are standard material in advanced scattering theory courses and are frequently tested through questions on the threshold energy, the role of the virial identity, and the construction of the nonlinear profile decomposition.

Scattering Theory for Power‑Type Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Strauss Exponent

Consider the Hartree‑type equation with a power‑law kernel

i∂tu + Δu = (|x|−γ * |u|2)u, 0 < γ < min(2,d),

where * denotes convolution in ℝd. The nonlinearity is nonlocal but retains the scaling invariance uλ(t,x)=λ(d−γ)/2u(λ2t,λx). The critical Sobolev exponent is sc = d/2 − (d−γ)/2 = γ/2. For γ = 1 (Coulomb) in d = 3 we have sc = 1/2, i.e. the equation is Ḣ1/2‑critical. The small‑data scattering theory in the energy space Ḣ1 was developed by Ginibre and Velo (1985) and later refined by Hayashi and Naumkin (1998) using the weighted Strichartz estimates. The large‑data problem is governed by the Strauss exponent pS(d,γ) = 1 + (2+γ)/(d−1), which marks the threshold between global existence and possible blow‑up for the corresponding local power nonlinearity |u|p−1u. For the nonlocal case, the same exponent appears in the analysis of the decay of the nonlinear term via the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality. When p > pS the nonlinearity is short‑range in the sense of scattering theory and standard wave operators exist; when p ≤ pS one must introduce modified wave operators with a long‑range phase correction of the form exp(±i c ∫0t ‖u(s)‖L2p/(p+1)2p/(p+1) ds). The existence and asymptotic completeness of these modified wave operators for the Hartree equation with γ ∈ (0,1] were proved by Wada (2001) and by Cazenave and Vilela (2009) for the combined power‑Hartree nonlinearity. The scattering operator S maps the asymptotic profile in the weighted space L2,σ to the outgoing profile and is a homeomorphism on the energy space. Examiners often ask for the derivation of the long‑range phase, the verification of the Kato‑smoothing estimate for the nonlocal term, and the distinction between the short‑range regime (γ > 1) and the long‑range regime (γ ≤ 1).

Worked Example: Long‑Range Phase for the 3D Coulomb Hartree Equation
Show that the modified wave operator Ω+mod = s‑limt→∞ e−itΔ e−iΦ(t) eitH exists for the equation i∂tu + Δu = (|x|−1 * |u|2)u, where Φ(t) = ∫0t (|x|−1 * |e−isΔφ|2) ds and φ ∈ 𝒮(ℝ3).
1. Write the Duhamel formula for the solution u(t) = eitΔφ − i∫0t ei(t−s)Δ (|x|−1 * |u(s)|2)u(s) ds. 2. Replace u(s) by the linear evolution eisΔφ in the phase integral; the error is controlled by the decay estimate ‖eisΔφ‖L6 ≲ |s|−1/2‖φ‖Ḣ1. 3. Compute Φ(t) = ∫0t (|x|−1 * |eisΔφ|2) ds. Using the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality, ‖|x|−1 * |f|2‖L∞ ≤ C‖f‖L62 ≲ |s|−1. Hence Φ(t) converges as t→∞ to a finite limit Φ(∞). 4. Define the modified free evolution Umod(t) = eitΔ eiΦ(t). The difference Umod(t)φ − u(t) satisfies an integral equation with a kernel that decays like |t|−1−ε. 5. Apply the Cook‑Kuroda method: the strong limit of Umod(−t)u(t) exists because the time derivative of the difference is integrable on [1,∞). 6. Conclude that Ω+mod exists and is isometric on the dense set 𝒮(ℝ3), extending to a unitary operator on L2.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the existence of modified wave operators, first identify the long‑range exponent (γ ≤ 1 for Hartree). Write the phase correction explicitly as an integral of the nonlinear term evaluated on the linear flow. Then verify the decay of that phase using the appropriate dispersive estimate (Strichartz or weighted L2). Finally, invoke the Cook‑Kuroda criterion: the time derivative of the modified evolution must be integrable. This three‑step template works for all long‑range NLS and Hartree models.

Scattering Theory for Combined Power and Hartree Nonlinearities

Many physical models lead to a Schrödinger equation with both a local power nonlinearity and a nonlocal Hartree term:

i∂tu + Δu = λ|u|p−1u + (|x|−γ * |u|2)u, λ ∈ ℝ, p > 1, 0 < γ < min(2,d).

The scaling analysis shows that the local term is Ḣsc,loc‑critical with sc,loc = d/2 − 2/(p−1), while the Hartree term is Ḣγ/2‑critical. The equation is energy‑subcritical if max(sc,loc, γ/2) < 1, energy‑critical if equality holds, and energy‑supercritical otherwise. For the energy‑subcritical regime (e.g. d = 3, p = 3, γ = 1) global well‑posedness and scattering for small data in H1 follow from a standard contraction argument in the Strichartz space LqtLrx with (q,r) = (4,12/5). Large‑data scattering in the defocusing case (λ > 0, Hartree repulsive) was proved by Miao, Xu and Zhao (2011) using the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method adapted to the mixed nonlinearity. The key new ingredient is a profile decomposition that separates the local and nonlocal components; the nonlocal part requires a refined Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev estimate to control the interaction between profiles. In the focusing case (λ < 0) the ground state of the combined nonlinearity solves −ΔQ + Q = λ|Q|p−1Q + (|x|−γ * |Q|2)Q. The sharp threshold for scattering versus blow‑up is given by the variational characterization of the ground state mass/energy (Holmer and Roudenko, 2007). Modified wave operators are needed when γ ≤ 1 because the Hartree tail is long‑range; the phase correction now contains both the local contribution ∫|u|p+1 and the nonlocal contribution ∫(|x|−γ * |u|2)|u|2. The existence of these modified wave operators was established by Nakanishi (2002) for p > 1 + 2/d and γ ∈ (0,1]. The scattering operator S is a nonlinear unitary map on H1 and its Fréchet derivative at zero coincides with the linear S‑matrix of the free Hamiltonian. Typical exam questions ask to derive the virial identity for the mixed nonlinearity, to identify the critical exponent pc = 1 + 4/(d−γ) that separates short‑range from long‑range behaviour of the Hartree part, and to explain why the standard wave operators fail when γ ≤ 1.

Scattering Theory on Manifolds with Asymptotically Conical Ends

Let (M,g) be a complete non‑compact Riemannian manifold of dimension d ≥ 2 that is asymptotically conical: outside a compact set M is diffeomorphic to (R+ × Y, dr2 + r2h) where (Y,h) is a compact (d−1)‑dimensional manifold without boundary. The Laplace‑Beltrami operator Δg on such manifolds has purely absolutely continuous spectrum [0,∞) and no embedded eigenvalues under a non‑trapping condition on the geodesic flow. The resolvent R(λ±i0) = (Δg − λ ∓ i0)−1 admits a meromorphic continuation to the complex plane as a family of operators between weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,α → Hs−2,−α for α > 1/2. The poles of this continuation are the resonances; their distribution reflects the geometry of the cone cross‑section Y. The Poisson operator P(λ) maps boundary data on Y to generalized eigenfunctions of Δg and is a Legendrian distribution associated to the geodesic flow on the cone. The scattering matrix S(λ) = P(λ)*P(λ) is a Fourier integral operator on C∞(Y) whose canonical relation is the scattering relation of the geodesic flow. Guillarmou and Hassell (2008) proved that S(λ) is a classical pseudodifferential operator of order 0 when the cone is non‑trapping, and its principal symbol is the classical scattering amplitude. Vasy (2013) extended the construction to manifolds with conic singularities and obtained a high‑energy asymptotics for the scattering matrix in terms of the sojourn time of geodesics. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limt→±∞ eitΔg e−itΔ0 exist and are asymptotically complete, where Δ0 is the model Laplacian on the exact cone. The proof uses the Mourre estimate with a conjugate operator adapted to the radial vector field r∂r. In the presence of trapped geodesics, the resolvent satisfies a polynomial bound ‖R(λ±i0)‖ ≤ Cλ−1/2 and the scattering matrix acquires a non‑trivial singular support. These results are central to modern geometric scattering theory and appear in advanced problem sets on the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent, the structure of the Poisson operator, and the relation between resonances and the poles of the scattering matrix.

  • Confusing the critical Hardy constant with the subcritical regime; the Hamiltonian is not essentially self‑adjoint at the critical value and requires a choice of self‑adjoint extension.
  • Assuming the Poisson operator on a conical end is a standard pseudodifferential operator; it is a Legendrian distribution associated to the geodesic flow.
  • Neglecting the gauge‑dependent phase correction when constructing modified wave operators for combined power‑Hartree nonlinearities; the standard wave operators do not exist without it.
  • Treating the Hartree nonlinearity as a short‑range perturbation for all γ; the Coulomb case γ = 1 is long‑range and demands modified wave operators.
  • Applying the geometric optics approximation without verifying the non‑trapping condition; otherwise the scattering matrix is not a Fourier integral operator of order zero.
  • Overlooking the Maslov index contribution in the semiclassical scattering amplitude on conical manifolds; it determines the correct phase of the scattering matrix eigenvalues.
  • Misidentifying the energy‑critical exponent for the mixed nonlinearity; the correct threshold is max(d/2 − 2/(p−1), γ/2) = 1, not the individual critical exponents.

These advanced topics complete the core material required for a comprehensive understanding of modern scattering theory at the research level.

Modified Wave Operators for Long‑Range Magnetic Potentials

When a charged particle moves in a static magnetic field described by a vector potential A(x) that decays like |x|‑α with 0 < α ≤ 1, the standard Cook’s method for constructing wave operators fails because the phase accumulated along classical trajectories diverges logarithmically. The remedy, introduced by Dollard and later refined by Enss and Weder, is to incorporate a time‑dependent phase correction that cancels the long‑range magnetic contribution.

Let H₀ = (−i∇)²/2m be the free Hamiltonian and H = (−i∇ − e A(x))²/2m the magnetic Hamiltonian. Define the Dollard‑type modifier

U_D(t) = exp { −i ∫₀ᵗ V_D(s) ds }, V_D(s) = e A(s v̂)·v̂,

where v̂ is the unit vector in the direction of the asymptotic momentum. The modified wave operators are then

Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ ei t H U_D(t) e-i t H₀.

Under the decay condition α > ½ and assuming A is C¹ with bounded derivatives, one can prove strong convergence of Ω_± and asymptotic completeness. The proof hinges on the stationary phase analysis of the phase factor exp(i e∫₀ᵗ A(s v̂)·v̂ ds) and on establishing a Mourre estimate with the conjugate operator A = ½(x·p + p·x), where p = −i∇.

Physically, the modified operator accounts for the Aharonov‑Bohm effect: even if the magnetic field vanishes outside a compact region, the vector potential can produce a non‑trivial scattering phase. In two dimensions with a solenoid of flux Φ, the scattering matrix acquires the factor exp(i eΦ/ħ) for each partial wave, leading to observable interference patterns.

Exam‑level expectations include: (i) stating the decay condition for which standard wave operators do not exist, (ii) writing the explicit Dollard modifier for a magnetic potential of Coulomb type, (iii) explaining why the phase correction restores the existence of Ω_±, and (iv) describing the physical interpretation in terms of the Aharonov‑Bohm phase shift.

Trace Formulas and the Spectral Shift Function for Multi‑Channel Scattering

The spectral shift function ξ(λ) quantifies how the spectrum of a reference Hamiltonian H₀ is displaced by a perturbation V. In the multi‑channel setting, where the Hilbert space decomposes as ℋ = ⊕j=1^N ℋ_j and each channel has its own free Hamiltonian H₀(j), the trace formula takes the form

Tr [f(H) − f(H₀)] = ∫ℝ f′(λ) ξ(λ) dλ, f∈C_c^∞(ℝ),

with H = H₀ + V acting on the full space. The Birman‑Krein formula links ξ(λ) to the determinant of the scattering matrix S(λ):

det S(λ) = exp(−2πi ξ(λ)).

For N ≥ 2 channels, S(λ) is an N × N unitary matrix whose entries Sij(λ) describe transmission from channel j to i and reflection when i = j. The trace of the logarithmic derivative of S(λ) yields the total shift:

ξ(λ) = (1/2πi) Tr [ S(λ)* dS(λ)/dλ ].

Key technical steps involve proving that V is trace class relative to (H₀ − i)-1, which guarantees the existence of the Fredholm determinant det(I + V R₀(λ ± i0)). The Krein spectral shift function is then obtained by differentiating the argument of this determinant. In practice, one often works with rank‑one or finite‑rank couplings, such as a point interaction that connects two otherwise independent half‑lines. In that case the scattering matrix reduces to

S(λ) = begin{pmatrix} r(λ) & t(λ) t(λ) & r(λ) end{pmatrix}, |r|²+|t|² = 1,

and the spectral shift simplifies to ξ(λ) = (1/π) arg t(λ).

Typical exam questions ask candidates to derive the Birman‑Krein relation for a two‑channel model, compute ξ(λ) explicitly for a delta‑coupling V = γ δ(x) P where P projects onto a fixed channel, and discuss how the sign of γ influences the number of bound states via the Levinson theorem:

ξ(0) − ξ(∞) = π N_b, N_b = number of bound states.

Understanding the trace formula also prepares students for advanced topics such as the Friedel sum rule in condensed matter, where ξ(λ_F) relates to the excess charge induced by an impurity at the Fermi energy λ_F.

Worked Example – Two‑Channel Delta Coupling
Consider two one‑dimensional half‑lines ℝ⁺1 and ℝ⁺2 with free Hamiltonians H₀(j) = −d²/dx² on each. A point interaction at the common origin couples the channels via V = γ δ(x) P, where P projects onto the symmetric combination (ψ₁+ψ₂)/√2. Compute the scattering matrix S(λ) and the spectral shift function ξ(λ). Show that the total number of bound states satisfies Levinson’s theorem.

1. Solve the stationary Schrödinger equation (−d²/dx² − λ)ψ = 0 on each half‑line. The general solution for x>0 is ψ_j(x)=a_j ei k x+b_j e-i k x, with k=√λ.

2. Impose continuity at x=0: ψ₁(0)=ψ₂(0)=ψ(0). The delta condition gives the jump in derivative:

ψ′1(0)+ψ′2(0)=γ ψ(0).

3. For an incoming wave from channel 1, set a₁=1, b₂=0. Solving the linear system yields transmission and reflection amplitudes:

t(λ)=(2 i k)/(2 i k‑γ), r(λ)=(‑γ)/(2 i k‑γ).

4. The scattering matrix is

S(λ)=begin{pmatrix} r(λ) & t(λ) t(λ) & r(λ) end{pmatrix}.

5. The determinant det S(λ)=r²‑t²= (‑γ‑2 i k)/(‑γ+2 i k)=e‑2 i δ(λ) where δ(λ)=arctan(γ/(2k)).

6. Using the Birman‑Krein formula, ξ(λ)= (1/2π) arg det S(λ)=‑δ(λ)/π.

7. Bound states correspond to poles of S(λ) on the positive imaginary axis: 2 i k‑γ=0 ⇒ k=iγ/2 ⇒ λ=‑γ²/4. Thus a bound state exists iff γ>0.

8. Levinson’s theorem: ξ(0)‑ξ(∞)= (‑δ(0)/π)‑(‑δ(∞)/π)= (‑π/2)/π‑0=‑½. The negative sign indicates one bound state (N_b=1) because the convention for ξ includes a factor of –π N_b. Hence the theorem is satisfied.

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the scattering matrix in a multi‑channel model, first write down the matching conditions at the interaction region, then solve the linear system for the amplitudes. Keep the symmetry of the matrix in mind – unitarity forces |r|²+|t|²=1 and S†S=I, which can be used to check algebra quickly. For the spectral shift, remember the Birman‑Krein relation; you only need the determinant of S, not each entry separately.

Complex Scaling and the Computation of Resonances in Non‑Self‑Adjoint Settings

Complex scaling (or dilation analyticity) is a powerful tool for exposing resonances as discrete eigenvalues of a non‑self‑adjoint operator. For a Schrödinger operator H = −Δ + V(x) on ℝ^d with V analytic in a sector |arg x| < θ₀, one defines the unitary dilation U_θ ψ(x)=edθ/2ψ(eθx) for real θ. Extending θ to a complex value θ = i ϕ with 0 < ϕ < θ₀ yields the scaled operator

H(ϕ) = Uiϕ H Uiϕ‑1 = e‑2iϕ (−Δ) + V(eiϕx).

For ϕ large enough, the continuous spectrum rotates downwards by an angle 2ϕ, opening a wedge in the lower half‑plane where the resolvent (H(ϕ)‑z)-1 is analytic. Resonances of the original operator correspond to eigenvalues of H(ϕ) that lie below the rotated continuous spectrum, i.e. with Im z < 0 and |arg z| < 2ϕ.

Key technical requirements:

  • V must be dilation‑analytic: it admits an analytic continuation V(eiϕx) for |ϕ| < θ₀.
  • The scaled operator H(ϕ) remains closed with domain H²(ℝ^d), and its spectrum consists of the rotated essential part plus isolated eigenvalues of finite algebraic multiplicity.
  • The Aguilar‑Balslev‑Combes theorem guarantees that the set of eigenvalues of H(ϕ) is independent of ϕ as long as the eigenvalue stays away from the rotated continuous spectrum.

In practice, one computes resonances by discretising H(ϕ) on a finite basis (e.g., a complex‑scaled Gaussian basis) and solving the resulting non‑Hermitian matrix eigenvalue problem. The imaginary part of an eigenvalue E = E_R − iΓ/2 yields the resonance width Γ, which measures the decay rate of the metastable state.

For a concrete example, consider the one‑dimensional potential V(x)=‑V₀ e‑x²/a². After scaling with ϕ = π/4, the potential becomes V(eiπ/4x)=‑V₀ e‑i x²/a², a complex Gaussian. Numerical diagonalisation of the resulting matrix reveals a sequence of resonances whose widths increase with energy, reflecting the tunnelling probability through the Gaussian barrier.

Exam questions often ask to (i) state the conditions under which complex scaling is valid, (ii) derive the rotated continuous spectrum for a given scaling angle, (iii) explain why resonances appear as eigenvalues of the non‑self‑adjoint operator, and (iv) outline a numerical scheme (e.g., complex‑scaled finite‑difference or basis‑set method) to compute a specific resonance.

Semiclassical Scattering Amplitudes and the Role of the Maslov Index

In the high‑energy limit (ħ → 0) the scattering amplitude can be expressed as a sum over classical trajectories that connect the incoming direction ω′ to the outgoing direction ω. The stationary phase approximation yields

a(ω, ω′; k) ≈ ∑γ∈Γ(ω,ω′) A_γ(k) ei k L_γ − i π μ_γ/2,

where k=√(2mE)/ħ, L_γ is the length of the trajectory γ, A_γ(k) is an amplitude involving the determinant of the Jacobian of the scattering map, and μ_γ is the Maslov index – an integer counting the number of caustic encounters (phase‑space focal points) along γ.

For a short‑range central potential V(r) with compact support, the classical scattering map is a diffeomorphism on the sphere Sd‑1. The Jacobian determinant J_γ equals |∂θ_out/∂θ_in|, where θ denotes the polar angle. The amplitude takes the explicit form

A_γ(k) = (2π i k)‑(d‑1)/2 |J_γ|‑1/2.

The Maslov index for a single‑bounce trajectory in three dimensions is μ_γ = 2, reflecting one focal point when the trajectory passes through the turning point of the radial motion. In multi‑bounce orbits around a convex obstacle, each reflection contributes an additional +1 to μ_γ.

These semiclassical formulas underpin the derivation of the differential cross‑section σ(θ) = |a(ω, ω′; k)|². The interference between different classical paths leads to diffraction patterns, such as the glory effect observed in scattering by a hard sphere, where the phase difference between the direct and reflected rays is governed by the Maslov index.

In exam settings, students are frequently asked to:

  1. Derive the stationary phase expression for a given central potential, identifying L_γ and computing J_γ.
  2. Explain the origin of the Maslov phase and compute μ_γ for a two‑bounce orbit around a circular obstacle.
  3. Show how the semiclassical amplitude reproduces the Rutherford scattering formula in the Coulomb case, noting that the long‑range nature modifies the phase by an additional logarithmic term.
  4. Discuss the limitations of the semiclassical approximation when the potential has a singularity (e.g., inverse‑square) that creates additional caustics.

Understanding the Maslov index is crucial for correctly assigning the sign of the interference term; a common mistake is to omit the factor (‑iπμ/2), which leads to an incorrect prediction of constructive versus destructive interference in the differential cross‑section.

Quantum Graphs with Magnetic Flux: Scattering Matrix and Spectral Statistics

A quantum graph consists of edges (one‑dimensional wires) connected at vertices, with wave functions satisfying the Schrödinger equation on each edge and matching conditions at the vertices. When a magnetic flux Φ threads a loop of the graph, the edgewise wave functions acquire Aharonov‑Bohm phases. For an edge e of length ℓ_e, the solution can be written as

ψ_e(x) = a_e ei k x + b_e e‑i k x, 0 ≤ x ≤ ℓ_e,

and the magnetic effect enters through the boundary condition at the vertices: the continuity of ψ and the Kirchhoff‑type condition for the derivative are modified to

∑e∈v (dψ_e/dx)(v) = i α_v ψ(v), α_v = (2π/Φ₀) Φ_v,

where Φ₀ = h/e is the flux quantum and Φ_v is the net flux through cycles incident at vertex v.

The scattering matrix S(k) of the whole graph is obtained by eliminating the internal amplitudes a_e, b_e in favour of the incoming amplitudes on the external leads. For a simple ring of total length L pierced by flux Φ, the scattering matrix reduces to

S(k) = (ei k L − ei θ)/(1 − ei (k L + θ)), θ = 2π Φ/Φ₀.

This expression shows the periodic dependence on Φ, leading to conductance oscillations (Aharonov‑Bohm effect) observable in mesoscopic experiments.

Beyond single‑loop graphs, the spectral statistics of large random quantum graphs exhibit universal behaviour described by random matrix theory (RMT). When the magnetic flux breaks time‑reversal symmetry, the appropriate ensemble is the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE); without flux, the Gaussian Orthogonal Ensemble (GOE) applies. The level spacing distribution P(s) transitions from the GOE Wigner surmise P(s)= (π/2) s e‑π s²/4 to the GUE form P(s)= (32/π²) s² e‑4 s²/π as Φ increases from 0 to Φ₀/2.

Exam questions may require students to (i) write down the matching conditions for a graph with a given magnetic flux configuration, (ii) derive the scattering matrix for a multi‑loop graph, (iii) explain how the flux modifies the symmetry class of the spectral statistics, and (iv) compute the conductance using the Landauer formula G = (2e²/h) Tr [t†t], where t is the transmission block of S(k).

  • Confusing the decay exponent α for magnetic potentials with that for scalar potentials; the long‑range condition for wave‑operator existence is α > ½, not merely α > 0.
  • Omitting the phase factor exp(‑iπμ/2) in semiclassical amplitudes, which leads to wrong interference predictions.
  • Assuming the Birman‑Krein formula holds without verifying that the perturbation is trace class; for rank‑infinite couplings the determinant may not exist.
  • Applying complex scaling to potentials that are not dilation‑analytic, which invalidates the Aguilar‑Balslev‑Combes theorem.
  • Treating the magnetic flux in quantum graphs as a simple additive phase on each edge, ignoring the vertex‑level Kirchhoff modification that carries the flux dependence.
  • Using the standard Levinson theorem for long‑range Coulomb potentials without adding the extra π/2 term required for the phase shift at zero energy.
  • Neglecting the Mourre estimate when proving asymptotic completeness for magnetic long‑range scattering, leading to an incomplete justification of wave‑operator existence.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Singular Potentials and the Renormalization Group Approach

Singular potentials such as the inverse‑square potential V(r) = λ/r² appear in many physical contexts, from quantum mechanics near a hard core to effective field theories. The Hamiltonian H = −Δ + λ/r² on L²(ℝ³) is not essentially self‑adjoint for λ < 3/4; it admits a one‑parameter family of self‑adjoint extensions labelled by a boundary condition at the origin. The renormalization group (RG) provides a systematic way to relate the bare coupling λ to the physical scattering length a. In the RG language, the flow of the dimensionless coupling g = λ/ℏ² under a change of the short‑distance cutoff Λ yields a limit cycle when λ is in the critical regime, leading to the Efimov effect in three‑body systems. For two‑body scattering, the S‑matrix for a given extension can be expressed in terms of the scattering phase shift δ(k) = −arctan( (k a) / (1 − (k a)²/2 + …) ) where the effective range expansion is modified by the anomalous dimension induced by the singularity. The existence and completeness of wave operators for these extensions were proved by using the Mourre estimate with a conjugate operator adapted to the scaling symmetry. A key subtlety is that the standard trace‑class condition for the perturbation fails; instead one uses a weighted trace‑class condition with weights that compensate the singularity. In examinations, candidates are often asked to derive the RG equation for the running coupling, to identify the fixed points and limit cycles, and to compute the modification of Levinson’s theorem due to the singular potential. A common mistake is to apply the usual Born series without renormalization, which diverges for λ ≤ −1/4.

The limit‑circle/limit‑point classification at the origin determines the number of boundary conditions required. For λ ≥ 3/4 the potential is in the limit‑point case and the Hamiltonian is essentially self‑adjoint; for λ < 3/4 it is limit‑circle and a one‑parameter family of self‑adjoint extensions exists, each characterised by a phase θ ∈ [0, π) in the asymptotic behaviour ψ(r) ∼ r1/2+ν + eiθ r1/2−ν with ν = √(1/4−λ). The scattering matrix S(k) = e2iδ(k) then depends on θ, and the physical scattering length a is related to θ by a = tan(θ/2) Λ2ν up to a scheme‑dependent constant. The RG flow equation dg/d ln Λ = β(g) with β(g) = −2ν g + O(g²) exhibits a non‑trivial fixed point at g* = 0 for ν > 0 and a limit cycle for ν imaginary (λ > 1/4). This cycle underlies the discrete scaling symmetry of Efimov trimers. When computing the spectral shift function ξ(λ) for such potentials, the Birman–Krein formula det S(λ) = e−2πi ξ(λ) still holds, but ξ(λ) acquires an extra contribution from the boundary condition at the origin. Examiners frequently test the ability to match the asymptotic solutions, to extract the phase shift from the Jost function, and to verify that the wave operators are complete by checking the Mourre estimate with the conjugate operator A = (r·p + p·r)/2. Another trap is to assume that the standard Kato–Birman theory applies directly; the singular nature forces the use of weighted spaces L²(ℝ³, ⟨r⟩±s) with s > 1/2.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Derivative Type and the Method of Normal Forms

Derivative nonlinearities such as i∂ₜu + Δu = ∂ₓ(|u|²u) or the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation (DNLS) i∂ₜu + ∂ₓ²u = i∂ₓ(|u|²u) arise in plasma physics and optical fibres. The presence of derivatives in the nonlinearity destroys the standard gauge invariance and complicates the scattering theory because the nonlinearity is not a perturbation in the usual Strichartz spaces. The method of normal forms, introduced by Shatah and refined by Guo, Kwon, and others, transforms the equation by a near‑identity change of variables u = v + Φ(v) where Φ is a multilinear operator chosen to eliminate the resonant derivative interactions. After the transformation, the new equation for v contains only non‑resonant terms that are smoother and can be treated by standard dispersive estimates. For the DNLS, the normal form transformation removes the derivative loss and yields a cubic nonlinearity without derivatives, allowing the use of the L²‑critical well‑posedness theory. The scattering operator is then constructed by proving asymptotic completeness in the energy space H¹(ℝ). A crucial step is to establish a priori bounds on the transformed solution v using the interaction Morawetz estimate and the virial identity adapted to the derivative structure. In the periodic setting, the normal form method must be combined with the Fourier restriction norm (Bourgain) spaces to handle the small divisors. Examiners often ask to perform the normal form transformation explicitly for a given derivative nonlinearity, to identify the resonant set, and to prove that the remainder term is perturbative in the Strichartz norm. A frequent error is to ignore the non‑local terms generated by the transformation, which can reintroduce derivative losses if not estimated carefully. Another trap is to assume that the standard wave operators exist without verifying the modified asymptotic profile; for derivative nonlinearities the free asymptotic state is not a solution of the linear equation but of a modified linear equation with a phase correction.

The normal form transformation is constructed by solving the homological equation LΦ = N_res where L is the linear dispersion operator and N_res is the resonant part of the nonlinearity. For DNLS, the resonant set consists of frequencies satisfying ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃−ξ₄ = 0 and ξ₁²−ξ₂²+ξ₃²−ξ₄² = 0, which forces ξ₁ = ξ₂ or ξ₁ = ξ₄. The transformation Φ is then a trilinear Fourier multiplier with symbol supported away from the resonant set, gaining a factor of |ξ|⁻¹. This gain compensates the derivative loss. The transformed equation takes the form i∂ₜv + ∂ₓ²v = N_nonres(v) + higher order terms, where N_nonres is a quintic (or higher) nonlinearity without derivatives. The scattering theory then proceeds by proving that the solution v(t) approaches a free solution v₊ as t → ∞ in H¹, using the long‑time Strichartz estimates and the fact that the nonlinearity is energy‑subcritical. In the case of the derivative NLS on the line, the modified wave operators are constructed by solving the integral equation v(t) = U(t)v₊ − i∫ₜ^∞ U(t−s) N_nonres(v(s)) ds, where U(t) = eit∂ₓ². The convergence of the integral relies on the decay of the Strichartz norm of v, which follows from the interaction Morawetz estimate ∫∫ |v|⁴ dx dt ≤ C ‖v₀‖H¹⁴. A typical exam question asks to derive the interaction Morawetz estimate for the transformed equation, to explain why the resonant set is excluded, and to compute the leading order phase correction in the asymptotic expansion of the solution. Misidentifying the resonant set or forgetting the Jacobian factor in the Fourier multiplier are common pitfalls.

Normal Form Transformation for DNLS
Consider the derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂ₜu + ∂ₓ²u = i∂ₓ(|u|²u) on ℝ. Perform the normal form transformation u = v + Φ(v) with Φ(v) = ∫ eix(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃) m(ξ₁,ξ₂,ξ₃) v̂(ξ₁) v̂(ξ₂) v̂(ξ₃) dξ₁ dξ₂ dξ₃. Determine the multiplier m that eliminates the resonant cubic derivative term, and write the resulting equation for v up to quintic order.
The cubic nonlinearity in Fourier space is i∂ₓ(|u|²u) → −(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃) v̂(ξ₁) v̂(ξ₂) v̂(ξ₃). The linear dispersion relation is ω(ξ) = ξ². The homological equation for the multiplier m is (ω(ξ₁)−ω(ξ₂)+ω(ξ₃)−ω(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃)) m = −(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃) on the non‑resonant set. The resonance condition ω(ξ₁)−ω(ξ₂)+ω(ξ₃)−ω(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃) = 0 simplifies to (ξ₁−ξ₂)(ξ₁−ξ₃)(ξ₂−ξ₃) = 0. Hence the resonant set is ξ₁=ξ₂ or ξ₁=ξ₃ or ξ₂=ξ₃. On the complement we can set m = −(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃) / (ω(ξ₁)−ω(ξ₂)+ω(ξ₃)−ω(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃)). The denominator factorises as 2(ξ₁−ξ₂)(ξ₁−ξ₃)(ξ₂−ξ₃). Thus m = −(ξ₁−ξ₂+ξ₃) / [2(ξ₁−ξ₂)(ξ₁−ξ₃)(ξ₂−ξ₃)]. The transformed equation for v is i∂ₜv + ∂ₓ²v = N₅(v) + O(v⁷) where N₅ is a quintic nonlinearity without derivatives, obtained by substituting u = v + Φ(v) into the original equation and collecting terms of order five. The explicit form of N₅ involves convolutions of m with the original cubic symbol and is non‑local but derivative‑free.
Exam Strategy for Normal Forms
When asked to construct a normal form, first write the nonlinearity in Fourier variables, identify the linear dispersion relation, and compute the resonance condition. The multiplier is the ratio of the nonlinearity symbol to the linear phase factor, defined away from the resonant set. Always check that the multiplier is bounded on the non‑resonant set; if it has singularities, a further decomposition (e.g., using a smooth cutoff) is needed. In the final answer, state clearly the order of the remaining nonlinearity and why it is perturbative in the chosen function space.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Manifolds with Asymptotically Cylindrical Ends and the Relative Index Theorem

Manifolds with asymptotically cylindrical ends, also called manifolds with cylindrical ends or periodic ends, are non‑compact Riemannian manifolds (M,g) that outside a compact set are isometric to a product [0,∞) × Y where Y is a closed Riemannian manifold. The Laplacian Δ_g on such manifolds has essential spectrum consisting of half‑lines [λ_j, ∞) where λ_j are the eigenvalues of the cross‑sectional Laplacian Δ_Y. Scattering theory on these spaces was developed by Melrose, Mazzeo, and Guillarmou using the b‑calculus and the Poisson operator. The scattering matrix S(λ) is a meromorphic family of operators on the space of boundary data on Y, and its poles correspond to resonances. The relative index theorem of Bunke and others relates the spectral flow of a family of Dirac‑type operators on the cylindrical end to the index of a Toeplitz operator on the boundary. In the context of the Schrödinger equation, the wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitΔ_g e−itΔ₀ exist and are complete provided the perturbation is short‑range relative to the cylindrical metric. The proof uses the Mourre estimate with a conjugate operator that is the generator of translations along the cylindrical direction. A key technical point is that the threshold eigenvalues λ_j embedded in the continuous spectrum can produce singularities in the scattering matrix; the limiting absorption principle holds in weighted b‑Sobolev spaces with weights that avoid the thresholds. Examiners often ask to describe the structure of the essential spectrum, to construct the Poisson operator mapping boundary data to generalized eigenfunctions, and to state the relative index formula ind(D_+) − ind(D_−) = sf({D_t}) where sf denotes spectral flow. A common mistake is to treat the cylindrical end as a Euclidean end and apply the standard Agmon–Hörmander estimates, which fail because the volume growth is linear rather than polynomial.

The b‑calculus provides a pseudodifferential calculus adapted to the cylindrical geometry. The resolvent R(λ) = (Δ_g − λ)−1 is a b‑pseudodifferential operator of order −2, and its Schwartz kernel has a polyhomogeneous expansion at the boundary faces of the b‑double space. The scattering matrix S(λ) is obtained by comparing the asymptotics of the generalized eigenfunctions at the two ends of the cylinder. For a manifold with two cylindrical ends, the scattering matrix is a 2×2 block operator on L²(Y₁) ⊕ L²(Y₂). The poles of S(λ) in the complex plane correspond to resonances, and their distribution is governed by the geometry of the trapped set. In the case of a single cylindrical end, the scattering matrix reduces to a reflection operator on L²(Y). The relative index theorem enters when one considers a family of Hamiltonians H(t) = Δ_g + V_t where V_t is a compactly supported potential varying with t. The spectral flow of the family of Dirac operators on the cylinder equals the index of the scattering matrix considered as a Toeplitz operator on the Hardy space of the boundary. This connection is used to prove the stability of the number of bound states under perturbations. A typical exam problem asks to compute the leading term of the scattering matrix for a metric perturbation g = dt² + h(t,y) where h(t,y) → h_∞(y) exponentially fast, and to show that the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator associated to the geodesic flow on Y. Another trap is to confuse the b‑calculus with the scattering calculus of Melrose for asymptotically Euclidean manifolds; the two calculi have different boundary defining functions and different symbol spaces.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time-Dependent Hamiltonians and the Adiabatic Theorem

When the Hamiltonian H(t) = H₀ + V(t) depends explicitly on time, the standard stationary scattering theory does not apply directly. The adiabatic theorem provides a framework for scattering when V(t) varies slowly compared to the internal dynamics. In the adiabatic limit ε → 0 with V(t) = W(εt), the evolution operator U_ε(t, s) can be approximated by the product of the instantaneous spectral projections of H(εt). The scattering operator S_ε = Ω_+ε* Ω_−^ε, where Ω_±^ε = limt→±∞ U_ε(0, t) ei∫₀^t H₀ ds, converges to a limit S₀ that is diagonal in the basis of the unperturbed Hamiltonian H₀. The off‑diagonal transitions are exponentially small in 1/ε, a phenomenon known as adiabatic decoupling. The rigorous proof uses the superadiabatic basis constructed by Nenciu and Sordoni, which diagonalises the Hamiltonian up to errors of order ε^N for any N. The scattering matrix elements between different instantaneous eigenstates are given by the Dykhne–Davis–Pechukas formula Smn ∼ exp(−1/ε ∫ Im E_m(t) dt) where the integral is taken along a complex time contour connecting the Stokes lines. In the context of the Schrödinger equation with a time‑dependent potential V(x, t) = V₀(x) + ε V₁(x, εt), the wave operators exist and are complete in the adiabatic regime provided the instantaneous Hamiltonians have no level crossings. If level crossings occur, the Landau–Zener formula gives the transition probability P = exp(−π Δ² / (2ε |dΔ/dt|)) where Δ is the gap at the crossing. Examiners frequently test the derivation of the adiabatic expansion, the construction of the superadiabatic projectors, and the computation of the geometric phase (Berry phase) accumulated during the evolution. A common error is to assume that the adiabatic theorem holds uniformly for all times without verifying the gap condition; the theorem breaks down near level crossings or when the perturbation is not sufficiently smooth.

The adiabatic expansion is obtained by seeking a unitary transformation U(t) = exp(iε S₁(t) + iε² S₂(t) + …) that block‑diagonalises the Hamiltonian in the instantaneous eigenbasis. The generators S_k are determined recursively by the condition that the transformed Hamiltonian H'(t) = U(t) H(t) U(t)* − iε U(t) ∂ₜ U(t)* has no off‑diagonal terms up to the desired order. The leading term S₁ satisfies [H₀(t), S₁(t)] = i ∂ₜ P(t) where P(t) is the spectral projection onto the relevant eigenband. The solution is S₁ = i ∑m≠n |m(t)⟩ ⟨m(t)| ∂ₜ P(t) |n(t)⟩ ⟨n(t)| / (E_m(t) − E_n(t)). The geometric phase appears as the diagonal part of the effective Hamiltonian: γ_n = i ∫ ⟨n(t)| ∂ₜ n(t)⟩ dt. In scattering problems, the asymptotic states are defined with respect to the free Hamiltonian H₀ = −Δ + V₀(x) at t → ±∞. The adiabatic wave operators Ω_±^ad = limt→±∞ U(t, 0) e−i∫₀^t H₀(s) ds exist if the potential V₁(x, τ) decays sufficiently fast as |τ| → ∞. The scattering matrix S_ad = Ω_+ad* Ω_−^ad is then computed by matching the adiabatic solutions across the interaction region. A typical exam question asks to derive the first‑order adiabatic correction to the scattering amplitude for a two‑level system with a slowly varying detuning, and to show that the transition probability is exponentially small. Another trap is to confuse the adiabatic limit with the semiclassical limit; the adiabatic parameter ε controls the time scale of the Hamiltonian variation, while the semiclassical parameter ℏ controls the wavelength. They are independent, and mixing them leads to incorrect scaling of the transition probabilities.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Strichartz Estimates

Nonlocal nonlinearities such as the Hartree nonlinearity (|x|−γ * |u|²) u with 0 < γ < 3, or the fractional nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂ₜu + (−Δ)^s u = |u|p−1u with s ∈ (0,1), require adapted dispersive estimates. The Strichartz estimates for the fractional propagator eit(−Δ)^s take the form ‖eit(−Δ)^s f‖L^q_t L^r_x ≤ C ‖f‖L² for admissible pairs (q,r) satisfying 2/q + d/r = d/2 − s and q > 2. These estimates are proved using the oscillatory integral representation of the kernel and the stationary phase method, with the phase function φ(ξ) = |ξ|2s. The dispersive decay is ‖eit(−Δ)^s f‖L^∞ ≤ C |t|−d/(2s) ‖f‖L¹, which is slower than the standard Schrödinger case (s=1) for s < 1. For the Hartree equation, the convolution kernel |x|−γ is bounded in the weak‑Ld/γ space, and the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality gives ‖(|x|−γ * |u|²) u‖Lq'_t Lr'_x ≤ C ‖u‖L^q_t L^r_x³ for suitable exponents. The scattering theory for these equations is developed in the energy space H^s (or H¹ for Hartree) by constructing the wave operators Ω_± = limt→±∞ U(−t) u(t) where U(t) = eit(−Δ)^s. The small‑data scattering follows from a contraction mapping argument in the Strichartz space, while large‑data scattering requires additional conservation laws (mass, energy) and the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Kenig and Merle. For the fractional NLS, the critical exponent is p_c = 1 + 4s/(d−2s) (energy‑critical) and pL² = 1 + 4s/d (mass‑critical). Examiners often ask to verify the admissibility condition for a given pair (q,r), to prove the dispersive estimate for the fractional propagator, and to set up the fixed‑point argument for the Hartree nonlinearity. A frequent mistake is to use the standard Strichartz estimates (s=1) for the fractional case, which leads to incorrect scaling and failure of the contraction. Another trap is to ignore the nonlocal nature of the Hartree term when estimating the Duhamel integral; the convolution must be handled with the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality, not with pointwise bounds.

The Strichartz estimates for the fractional propagator are derived by writing the kernel K_t(x) = ∫ ei(x·ξ + t|ξ|2s) dξ and applying the stationary phase method. The critical points satisfy ξ = 0 for s > 1/2, but for s < 1/2 the phase has no non‑degenerate critical points, and the decay is obtained by integration by parts using the vector field ∂_ξ. The resulting dispersive bound ‖K_t‖L^∞ ≤ C |t|−d/(2s) is sharp. The admissible pairs are then obtained by interpolating between the dispersive estimate and the energy estimate. For the Hartree equation, the nonlinearity is estimated in the dual Strichartz space using the fractional Leibniz rule and the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality: ‖(|x|−γ * |u|²) u‖Lq'_t Lr'_x ≤ C ‖u‖L^q_t L^r_x² ‖u‖L^∞_t L²_x. The scattering operator is constructed by showing that the solution u(t) approaches a free solution u_±(t) = eit(−Δ)^s φ_± as t → ±∞ in H^s. The asymptotic states φ_± are obtained as the limits of e−it(−Δ)^s u(t) in H^s. In the energy‑critical case, the concentration‑compactness argument requires a profile decomposition adapted to the fractional scaling symmetry u_λ(x) = λ(d−2s)/2 u(λx). The linear profile decomposition uses the fractional propagator and the orthogonality of the scaling parameters. A typical exam problem asks to prove the Strichartz estimate for the fractional propagator in dimension d=3, s=1/2, and to determine the range of γ for which the Hartree nonlinearity is energy‑subcritical. Another common error is to assume that the fractional Laplacian (−Δ)^s has the same domain as the standard Laplacian; the domain is the Bessel potential space H2s, which for s < 1/2 does not embed into L^∞, affecting the nonlinear estimates.

  • Confusing the renormalization group flow for singular potentials with the standard perturbative RG; the limit cycle and Efimov effect are specific to the inverse‑square singularity.
  • Neglecting the non‑resonant condition in the normal form transformation, which leads to a multiplier that is not bounded and fails to remove the derivative loss.
  • Applying Euclidean Agmon–Hörmander estimates to manifolds with cylindrical ends instead of the b‑calculus, resulting in incorrect threshold behaviour.
  • Assuming the adiabatic theorem holds uniformly through level crossings without using the Landau–Zener formula or superadiabatic bases.
