  • Using standard Strichartz estimates for the fractional Schrödinger propagator, ignoring the modified admissibility condition 2/q + d/r = d/2 − s.
  • Omitting the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality when estimating the Hartree nonlinearity, leading to false boundedness in the dual Strichartz space.
  • Forgetting that the scattering matrix on a cylindrical manifold is a Fourier integral operator associated to the geodesic flow on the cross‑section, not a pseudodifferential operator.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Fourier Restriction Norm Method

The Fourier restriction norm method, introduced by Bourgain, provides a powerful framework for proving local and global well‑posedness as well as scattering for dispersive equations with nonlocal nonlinearities. The central objects are the Bourgain spaces Xs,b associated with the linear propagator U(t) = eit(−Δ). For a function u(t,x) on ℝ×ℝd the norm is defined by

‖u‖Xs,b = ‖⟨ξ⟩s⟨τ+|ξ|2⟩b ℱt,xu(τ,ξ)‖L2τ,ξ.

Here ⟨·⟩ = (1+|·|2)1/2 and ℱt,x denotes the space‑time Fourier transform. The parameter b measures the amount of time regularity; the critical value for the Schrödinger equation is b = 1/2. The key linear estimate is the homogeneous Strichartz bound

‖U(t)f‖Xs,1/2 ≤ C‖f‖Hs.

For nonlocal nonlinearities of Hartree type (|x|−γ*|u|2)u with 0 < γ < d, the convolution is handled by the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality in Fourier space. The nonlinear estimate takes the form

‖(|x|−γ*|u|2)u‖Xs,b−1 ≤ C‖u‖Xs,b3

provided s satisfies the scaling condition s = d/2 − γ/2 (energy‑critical) or s > d/2 − γ/2 (energy‑subcritical). The proof uses the fractional Leibniz rule together with the bilinear estimate

‖uv‖X0,−1/2+ ≤ C‖u‖X0,1/2+‖v‖X0,1/2+.

Scattering follows by constructing the wave operators Ω± = limt→±∞U(−t)u(t) in Hs. The limit exists because the Duhamel term ∫0tU(t−τ)N(u(τ))dτ converges in Hs as t→±∞, a consequence of the time‑integrability encoded in the b index. Examiners frequently ask to verify the admissible range of (s,b) for a given γ and dimension d, and to prove the trilinear estimate using dyadic decomposition and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality in Fourier space. A common mistake is to apply the standard Strichartz estimates (which correspond to b = 1/2) without the additional b regularity, leading to a loss of derivatives for nonlocal terms.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the UpVp Spaces

The atomic function spaces Up and Vp, developed by Koch and Tataru, refine the Bourgain spaces by replacing the Fourier‑side weight ⟨τ+|ξ|2⟩b with a time‑frequency atomic decomposition. A function u belongs to Up if it can be written as a sum of p‑atoms ak supported on intervals Ik with ℱtak supported in a slab {τ+|ξ|2 ∈ Jk} and satisfying ‖ak‖L2t,x ≤ |Ik|1/2−1/p. The space Vp consists of functions of bounded p‑variation adapted to the same frequency localisation. The crucial embedding is Up ⊂ X0,1/2−1/p for p>2, which yields sharper bilinear estimates than the Xs,b scale.

For the Hartree nonlinearity the trilinear estimate in Up reads

‖(|x|−γ*|u|2)u‖Vp′ ≤ C‖u‖Up3,

where 1/p+1/p′=1. The proof exploits the orthogonality of the frequency slabs and the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality in the form

‖|x|−γ*f‖Lq ≤ C‖f‖Lr, 1/q = 1/r − γ/d.

Choosing p = 2+ε gives an endpoint estimate that is not available in Xs,b. This endpoint is essential for the energy‑critical case γ = 2, d = 3 where the scaling index is s = 1/2. The scattering operator is then constructed by showing that the solution map extends continuously to the asymptotic states in H1/2. Typical exam problems ask to derive the atomic representation of a given function, to prove the embedding Up ⊂ X0,1/2−1/p, and to set up the fixed‑point argument in the space Up∩Vp. A frequent error is to confuse the variation norm Vp with the Besov space B1/pp,∞; the former is adapted to the linear flow, the latter is not.

Worked Example: Verifying the Xs,b Estimate for Fractional NLS
Let d=3, s=1/2, and consider the fractional NLS i∂tu+(−Δ)1/2u = |u|2u. Show that the nonlinearity maps X1/2,1/2+ to X1/2,−1/2+.
Step 1: Write the Xs,b norm with the fractional dispersion relation τ+|ξ|. The linear estimate ‖eit(−Δ)1/2f‖X1/2,1/2 ≤ C‖f‖H1/2 follows from Plancherel. Step 2: For the trilinear term, use the fractional Leibniz rule ‖|u|2u‖H1/2 ≤ C‖u‖L∞2‖u‖H1/2. Step 3: Interpolate between the dispersive bound ‖eit(−Δ)1/2f‖L∞ ≤ C|t|−3‖f‖L1 and the energy bound to obtain the Strichartz estimate ‖u‖L4t,x ≤ C‖u‖X1/2,1/2+. Step 4: Combine to get ‖|u|2u‖X1/2,−1/2+ ≤ C‖u‖X1/2,1/2+3. The key is the admissibility condition 2/q+3/r = 3/2−s with s=1/2, giving (q,r)=(4,4).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for the range of (s,b) for which a nonlocal estimate holds, first write down the scaling relation s = d/2 − γ/2. Then recall the bilinear estimate ‖uv‖X0,−1/2+ ≤ C‖u‖X0,1/2+‖v‖X0,1/2+. The admissible b must satisfy b > 1/2 for the nonlinearity to be integrable in time; the endpoint b = 1/2 fails for nonlocal terms. Memorise the critical pairs (s,b) for γ = 1,2 in d = 3.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Modulation Space Estimates

Modulation spaces Msp,q, introduced by Feichtinger, measure the time‑frequency concentration of a function via the short‑time Fourier transform. For the Schrödinger equation with nonlocal nonlinearities, the modulation space framework yields estimates that are independent of the dispersion relation’s curvature, making them particularly useful for fractional and higher‑order dispersions. The norm is defined by

‖f‖Msp,q = ‖⟨ξ⟩sVgf(·,ξ)‖LqξLpx,

where Vgf(x,ξ) = ∫ f(t)g(t−x)e−iξtdt is the short‑time Fourier transform with a fixed window g ∈ 𝒮. The key linear estimate is the boundedness of the propagator on modulation spaces:

‖eit(−Δ)f‖Msp,q ≤ C‖f‖Msp,q, 1 ≤ p,q ≤ ∞.

For the Hartree nonlinearity, the convolution structure is handled by the algebra property of Ms1,1 (Feichtinger’s algebra) when s > d/2. In the energy‑subcritical regime one works in Ms2,1 which embeds into L∞ and allows the estimate

‖(|x|−γ*|u|2)u‖Ms2,1 ≤ C‖u‖Ms2,13.

The proof uses the pointwise bound |Vg(f*h)| ≤ |Vgf| * |Vgh| and Young’s inequality for mixed norms. Scattering is then obtained by constructing the wave operators in Ms2,1 and showing that the asymptotic states belong to the same space. Examiners often ask to prove the embedding Ms2,1 ⊂ L∞ for s > d/2, to verify the algebra property of Ms1,1, and to set up the contraction mapping in the Duhamel formulation using the modulation space norm. A typical trap is to assume that the standard Strichartz estimates imply modulation space bounds; the two scales are incomparable and the modulation space approach requires a separate time‑frequency analysis.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Profile Decomposition Method

The profile decomposition, pioneered by Bahouri and Gérard for the wave equation and adapted to the Schrödinger equation by Merle and Vega, provides a concentration‑compactness tool that replaces the Palais‑Smale condition in critical problems. For a bounded sequence {un} in Hs(ℝd) there exist profiles {φj} ⊂ Hs, parameters (λnj, xnj, tnj) with λnj > 0, and a remainder wnJ such that

un = Σj=1J (λnj)−(d−2s)/2 e−itnj(−Δ) φj((·−xnj)/λnj) + wnJ,

with the orthogonality condition

limn→∞ ( |log(λnj/λnk)| + |xnj−xnk|/λnj + |tnj−tnk|/(λnj)2 ) = ∞ for j ≠ k.

The remainder satisfies the vanishing Strichartz property limJ→∞ limsupn→∞ ‖eitΔwnJ‖LqtLrx = 0 for any admissible pair (q,r). For nonlocal nonlinearities the profile decomposition is combined with the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality to control the interaction terms between distinct profiles. The scattering proof proceeds by contradiction: assuming a minimal‑mass non‑scattering solution, one extracts a profile that yields a non‑trivial solution with strictly smaller mass, contradicting minimality. This rigidity argument is the core of the Kenig–Merle roadmap. Exam questions typically require stating the orthogonality conditions, proving the Pythagorean expansion of the Hs norm, and showing how the nonlocal term decouples across profiles. A common mistake is to omit the time‑translation parameter tnj in the decomposition for the Schrödinger equation; without it the linear profiles do not solve the free equation and the Strichartz estimates fail.

  • Confusing the Xs,b scale with the UpVp scale; the latter gives endpoint estimates but requires atomic decomposition.
  • Applying standard Strichartz estimates to fractional dispersions without adjusting the admissibility condition 2/q + d/r = d/2 − s.
  • Neglecting the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality when estimating the Hartree convolution in any function space framework.
  • Assuming modulation space bounds follow from Sobolev embeddings; Msp,q and Hs are not nested.
  • Omitting the time‑translation parameter in the profile decomposition for the Schrödinger equation, leading to incorrect linear profiles.
  • Using the bilinear estimate ‖uv‖X0,−1/2 ≤ C‖u‖X0,1/2‖v‖X0,1/2 at the endpoint b = 1/2, which is false for nonlocal nonlinearities.
  • Forgetting that the scattering operator in the energy‑critical case requires a concentration‑compactness/rigidity argument, not just a fixed‑point iteration.

These advanced frameworks illustrate how modern harmonic analysis tools refine the classical wave operator and S‑matrix theory for nonlocal and critical nonlinearities.

Scattering Theory for the Nonlocal Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation with Reverse‑Space Nonlocality

The reverse‑space nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) reads i u_t + uxx + 2 u2 u(-x) = 0, where the nonlinearity couples the field at x with its mirror at -x. This equation was introduced by Ablowitz and Musslimani (2013) and belongs to a new class of integrable nonlocal equations that are PT‑symmetric. The Lax pair for this model involves a nonlocal reduction of the AKNS spectral problem: the scattering data evolve linearly in time, and the inverse scattering transform (IST) reconstructs the solution from the reflection coefficient and discrete eigenvalues. The direct scattering problem is formulated on the real line with the symmetry condition r(-k) = -r(k) for the reflection coefficient, which follows from the PT symmetry of the potential. Soliton solutions are obtained from simple poles of the transmission coefficient; a one‑soliton solution has the form u(x,t) = η sech(η(x - 2ξ t)) ei(ξ x + (η2 - ξ2) t) with the constraint that the soliton centre satisfies x0 = 0 due to the nonlocal symmetry. Multi‑soliton interactions are elastic and can be expressed via determinant formulas analogous to the local NLS. The scattering operator S maps the initial reflection coefficient r0(k) to r(k,t) = r0(k) e4 i k2 t, showing that the continuous spectrum evolves by a pure phase factor. Rigorous results on the existence of wave operators for this equation were proved by using the IST in weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,σ with s ≥ 0, σ > 1/2. The wave operators Ω_± are shown to be asymptotically complete: for any initial data in the weighted space there exist unique asymptotic states u_± ∈ Hs,σ such that ‖u(t) - ei t ∂_x2 u_±‖Hs,σ → 0 as t → ±∞. The proof relies on the fact that the Jost solutions satisfy Volterra integral equations with kernels that decay sufficiently fast thanks to the weight. Examiners often ask to derive the symmetry condition on the scattering data from the PT‑symmetric reduction, to write the one‑soliton solution explicitly, and to explain why the wave operators are unitary on the continuous spectral subspace.

Scattering Theory for the Hartree Equation and the Interaction Morawetz Estimate

The Hartree equation i u_t + Δ u = (|x|-γ * |u|2) u, with 0 < γ < min(2, d), models the mean‑field limit of many‑body quantum systems. The interaction Morawetz estimate is a spacetime bound that controls the nonlinear potential energy and is crucial for proving scattering in the energy‑subcritical regime. For a solution u(t,x) with mass M = ∫ |u|2 dx and energy E = ½ ∫ |∇ u|2 dx + ¼ ∫ (|x|-γ * |u|2) |u|2 dx, the Morawetz action is defined as M(t) = 2 Im ∫ bar u ∇ u · a(x) dx, where a(x) = x is the standard multiplier. Differentiating and integrating by parts yields the identity ∂_t M(t) = 4 ∫ |∇ u|2 dx - γ ∫ (|x|-γ * |u|2) |u|2 dx + error terms involving the commutator [Δ, a·∇]. After time integration and using the conservation of energy, one obtains the estimate ∫_ℝ ∫ℝ^d (|x|-γ * |u|2) |u|2 dx dt ≤ C(E, M). This spacetime bound implies that the nonlinear potential belongs to L1_t L^∞_x, which in turn yields the asymptotic completeness of the wave operators in the energy space H1. The proof of scattering proceeds by constructing the wave operators Ω_± via the Cook method: one shows that the Duhamel term ∫0^t e-i s Δ (|x|-γ * |u|2) u ds converges in H1 as t → ±∞. The convergence follows from the Morawetz estimate combined with the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality and the Strichartz estimates for the free propagator. A key technical point is that the interaction Morawetz estimate is derived without assuming radial symmetry, unlike the classical Morawetz estimate for the local NLS. Examiners frequently test the derivation of the Morawetz identity, the use of the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality to bound the convolution term, and the final step that the Duhamel integral is Cauchy in H1.

Worked Example: Interaction Morawetz for Hartree
Let u be a global H1 solution of the Hartree equation in dimension d = 3 with γ = 1. Show that ∫_ℝ ∫ℝ3 (|x|-1 * |u|2) |u|2 dx dt ≤ C(E, M).
Define the Morawetz action M(t) = 2 Im ∫ bar u ∇ u · x dx. Compute ∂_t M(t) = 4 ∫ |∇ u|2 dx - ∫ (|x|-1 * |u|2) |u|2 dx + boundary terms that vanish for H1 solutions. Integrate from -T to T: M(T) - M(-T) = 4 ∫-T^T ∫ |∇ u|2 dx dt - ∫-T^T ∫ (|x|-1 * |u|2) |u|2 dx dt. The left side is bounded by 2 ‖x u‖L2 ‖∇ u‖L2 ≤ C(E, M) using the uncertainty principle. The kinetic term is controlled by the conserved energy. Rearranging gives the desired spacetime bound with a constant depending only on E and M.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for a Morawetz‑type estimate, first write the multiplier a(x) and the action M(t). Differentiate carefully, keep track of commutator terms, and integrate in time. Always state which conservation laws (mass, energy) you use to bound the boundary terms. The final inequality should be expressed solely in terms of the conserved quantities.

Scattering Theory for the Nonlocal Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation and the Virial‑Morawetz Identity

The virial identity for the Hartree equation provides a second‑order moment estimate that complements the interaction Morawetz bound. Define the variance V(t) = ∫ |x|2 |u(t,x)|2 dx. For a solution with sufficient decay, the second derivative is V''(t) = 8 ∫ |∇ u|2 dx - 2γ ∫ (|x|-γ * |u|2) |u|2 dx. This identity is derived by differentiating V(t) twice and using the equation of motion; the nonlocal term contributes a factor γ because of the homogeneity of the Riesz potential. When γ = 2 (the critical Hartree case), the virial identity becomes V''(t) = 16 E - 4 ∫ (|x|-2 * |u|2) |u|2 dx, which can be used to prove blow‑up for negative energy solutions via the Glassey argument. In the subcritical case γ < 2, the virial identity combined with the interaction Morawetz estimate yields a uniform bound on the spacetime norm of the nonlinearity, which is a key ingredient in the proof of global well‑posedness and scattering for large data in the energy space. The virial‑Morawetz functional M_V(t) = V'(t) - 2 M(t) (where M(t) is the Morawetz action) satisfies a differential inequality that can be integrated to give a bound on ∫0^T ∫ (|x|-γ * |u|2) |u|2 dx dt independent of T. This bound is then used to show that the Duhamel term converges in H1 as T → ∞, establishing the existence of wave operators. Examiners may ask to derive the virial identity for a general γ, to explain the role of the homogeneity degree, and to combine the virial and Morawetz estimates to obtain a spacetime bound that does not rely on radial symmetry.

Scattering Theory for the Nonlocal Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation and the Long‑Time Asymptotics via the Nonlinear Spectral Transform

For integrable nonlocal NLS equations, the long‑time asymptotics of solutions can be obtained by the nonlinear steepest descent method applied to the Riemann–Hilbert problem (RHP) associated with the inverse scattering transform. The RHP is formulated on the real axis with jump matrix J(k) = I - r(k) e2 i k2 t σ3 σ_+ - bar r(k) e-2 i k2 t σ3 σ_-, where r(k) is the reflection coefficient and σ_± are nilpotent matrices. The phase function θ(k) = k2 has a single stationary point at k = 0. The Deift–Zhou steepest descent analysis deforms the contour to pass through the stationary point along steepest descent directions, factorizes the jump matrix, and reduces the RHP to a model problem that can be solved explicitly in terms of parabolic cylinder functions. The leading asymptotic behavior of the solution is u(x,t) ∼ t-1/2 A(x/t) ei x2/(4t) where A is determined by the reflection coefficient at the origin. For the reverse‑space nonlocal NLS, the symmetry r(-k) = -r(k) forces the reflection coefficient to vanish at k = 0, which modifies the asymptotics: the leading term decays like t-3/2 instead of t-1/2. The rigorous justification requires that the reflection coefficient belongs to a weighted Sobolev space H1,1 and that there are no discrete eigenvalues (solitons). If solitons are present, the asymptotic decomposition contains a sum of soliton terms plus a dispersive remainder that decays as t-1/2 (or t-3/2 in the symmetric case). The nonlinear spectral transform thus provides a complete description of the scattering map: the asymptotic state is encoded in the scattering data, and the wave operators are explicitly given by the inverse scattering transform evaluated at the scattering data. Exam questions often focus on the contour deformation step, the factorization of the jump matrix, and the effect of the symmetry condition on the decay rate.

Scattering Theory for the Nonlocal Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation with PT‑Symmetric Nonlocality and the Inverse Scattering Transform

The PT‑symmetric nonlocal NLS i u_t + uxx + 2 u2 u^*(-x) = 0 (where * denotes complex conjugation) is another integrable nonlocal equation introduced by Ablowitz and Musslimani. Its Lax pair involves a nonlocal reduction of the AKNS system with the symmetry q(x) = u(x), r(x) = u^*(-x). The direct scattering problem yields scattering data {a(k), b(k)} satisfying the symmetry a(-k) = a^*(k), b(-k) = -b^*(k). The inverse scattering transform reconstructs the potential from the reflection coefficient ρ(k) = b(k)/a(k) and the discrete eigenvalues {k_j} in the upper half‑plane. The soliton solutions are obtained from simple poles of a(k); a one‑soliton solution has the form u(x,t) = 2 η e2 i ξ x + 4 i (ξ2 - η2) t / (cosh(2 η x + 8 ξ η t) + e2 i φ) with parameters η > 0, ξ ∈ ℝ, φ ∈ ℝ. The scattering operator acts on the reflection coefficient by ρ(k,t) = ρ(k,0) e4 i k2 t, exactly as in the local NLS. The existence and completeness of wave operators for this equation were proved by using the IST in the space of potentials with sufficient decay and by showing that the Jost solutions satisfy a Volterra integral equation with a kernel that is square integrable. The key difference from the local case is that the symmetry of the potential forces the reflection coefficient to be odd in k, which simplifies the analysis of the RHP at the origin. In the energy‑critical case (power nonlinearity |u|4 u with nonlocality), the IST is not available, but concentration‑compactness methods combined with the virial‑Morawetz estimates yield scattering for radial data. Examiners may ask to write the symmetry conditions for the scattering data, to derive the one‑soliton solution from the residue conditions, and to explain why the oddness of the reflection coefficient improves the decay of the dispersive part.

  • Confusing the reverse‑space nonlocal NLS with the PT‑symmetric nonlocal NLS; they have different symmetry reductions and different soliton formulas.
  • Forgetting that the interaction Morawetz estimate requires the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality to bound the convolution term; omitting this step loses marks.
  • Assuming the virial identity holds for all γ without checking the homogeneity of the Riesz potential; the factor γ appears explicitly.
  • Neglecting the symmetry condition r(-k) = -r(k) when performing the steepest descent analysis; it changes the decay rate from t-1/2 to t-3/2.
  • Using the standard Cook method for the Hartree equation without the Morawetz spacetime bound; the Duhamel integral does not converge in H1 otherwise.
  • Misstating the Lax pair for the PT‑symmetric nonlocal NLS; the reduction is q(x) = u(x), r(x) = u^*(-x), not r(x) = u(-x).
  • Overlooking that the scattering operator for integrable nonlocal equations is diagonal in the spectral variable and acts by a pure phase factor e4 i k2 t.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Critical Nonlinearities and the Concentration‑Compactness/Rigidity Method

The energy‑critical nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) in dimension d ≥ 3 with power nonlinearity |u|4/(d−2)u is invariant under the scaling uλ(t,x) = λ(d−2)/2u(λ2t,λx) which preserves the homogeneous Ḣ1 norm. The ground state Q solves −ΔQ + Q = |Q|4/(d−2)Q and minimizes the action among non‑trivial solutions. Kenig and Merle (2006) introduced the concentration‑compactness/rigidity scheme: assume a minimal‑mass blowup solution exists, extract a critical element via a profile decomposition (Bahouri‑Gérard), and prove rigidity using a virial/Morawetz identity localized to the light cone. The key steps are: (i) a linear profile decomposition in Ḣ1 with orthogonal parameters; (ii) a nonlinear profile decomposition approximating the solution by a sum of nonlinear profiles; (iii) a perturbation theory showing that if the error is small in the Strichartz norm, the solution scatters; (iv) a rigidity theorem excluding the existence of a minimal blowup solution by showing it must be compact modulo symmetries and then deriving a contradiction via the localized virial identity ∫ |x|2|u|2 ≤ Ct2. For the focusing case, scattering holds for data with mass‑energy below that of the ground state (M(u)E(u) < M(Q)E(Q)) and radial symmetry (or d ≥ 5). The defocusing case was settled by Colliander, Keel, Staffilani, Takaoka, and Tao (2008) using the interaction Morawetz estimate and the induction‑on‑energy method. Examiners often ask for the scaling‑critical Sobolev space, the role of the ground state, the structure of the profile decomposition, and the localized virial identity. A common trap is confusing the energy‑critical exponent 4/(d−2) with the mass‑critical exponent 4/d; the former preserves Ḣ1, the latter preserves L2.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Quadratic Derivative Type and the Null Condition

Quadratic derivative nonlinearities appear in wave maps, Yang‑Mills, and the Einstein equations. For the Schrödinger map equation ∂tu = u × Δu with values in S2, the nonlinearity is quadratic in ∇u and satisfies a null structure: the worst interactions occur when frequencies are parallel, but the geometric constraint u · ∂tu = 0 forces cancellation. The null condition for Schrödinger‑type systems was formulated by Klainerman and Machedon (1995) and adapted to the Schrödinger context by Tataru (2001) and others. A typical model is i∂tu + Δu = Q(∇u,∇u) where Q is a bilinear form satisfying Q(ξ,ξ) = 0 for all ξ ∈ ℝd. The null structure allows one to prove bilinear Strichartz estimates of the form ‖∇u ∇v‖L2t,x ≤ C‖u‖X‖v‖X with X a suitable function space (e.g., the U2/V2 spaces of Koch‑Tataru). The proof uses a dyadic decomposition and the observation that the resonance function |ξ|2 − |η|2 − |ξ−η|2 = −2ξ·η + 2|η|2 vanishes when ξ ∥ η, which is precisely where the null form vanishes. For small data in Ḣs with s > sc (the scaling‑critical regularity), global existence and scattering follow from a contraction mapping in the critical space. In the energy‑critical case (wave maps in 2+1 dimensions), the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Sterbenz‑Tataru (2010) and Krieger‑Schlag (2012) uses a caloric gauge to linearize the problem and a frequency‑localized Morawetz estimate. Examiners may ask to state the null condition for a given bilinear form, to explain why the resonance function vanishes on the null set, or to sketch the caloric gauge construction. A frequent error is to apply the standard Strichartz estimates without the null structure, which fail at the critical regularity because the derivative loss is not compensated.

Worked Example: Null Form Verification
Consider the quadratic nonlinearity N(u) = ∂xu ∂yu − ∂yu ∂xu in two spatial dimensions. Show that its symbol satisfies the null condition.
The symbol of N is σ(ξ,η) = iξ1 iη2 − iξ2 iη1 = −(ξ1η2 − ξ2η1) = −det(ξ,η). For the null condition we require σ(ξ,ξ) = 0 for all ξ. Indeed σ(ξ,ξ) = −det(ξ,ξ) = 0. Moreover, the resonance function for the Schrödinger equation is φ(ξ,η) = |ξ|2 − |η|2 − |ξ−η|2 = −2ξ·η + 2|η|2. The null set where φ = 0 is {ξ·η = |η|2}, i.e., ξ and η are parallel with |ξ| ≥ |η|. On this set, det(ξ,η) = 0, so the symbol vanishes. Hence the nonlinearity satisfies the null condition, and bilinear estimates gain a factor of the angle between ξ and η, allowing critical regularity results.
Exam Strategy: Null Structure Recognition
When a problem presents a quadratic derivative nonlinearity, immediately write its symbol σ(ξ,η) and check σ(ξ,ξ) = 0. If it holds, state the null condition explicitly and note that the resonance function vanishes on the same set. This single step often earns 4–5 marks in a 10‑mark question. Do not attempt to prove bilinear estimates from scratch unless explicitly asked; cite the Klainerman‑Machedon or Tataru framework.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Asymptotically de Sitter and Anti‑de Sitter Spacetimes

On Lorentzian manifolds with a cosmological constant Λ, the Schrödinger equation is replaced by the Klein‑Gordon equation (□g + m2)φ = 0. For asymptotically de Sitter (dS) spacetimes (Λ > 0), the conformal boundary is spacelike (future/past infinity ℐ±), and scattering theory maps Cauchy data on a spacelike slice to asymptotic data on ℐ+. The key tool is the conformal compactification: g = Ω−2ĝ with Ω a defining function for ℐ. The rescaled field Φ = Ω−(d−1)/2φ satisfies a regular equation up to ℐ. The scattering operator S: ℋin → ℋout is unitary between the energy spaces at ℐ− and ℐ+. For asymptotically anti‑de Sitter (AdS) spacetimes (Λ < 0), the conformal boundary is timelike, and boundary conditions must be imposed (Dirichlet, Neumann, or Robin). The AdS/CFT correspondence identifies the boundary value of the bulk field with the source of a dual CFT operator. The scattering matrix is replaced by the holographic renormalization group flow. In the Schwarzschild‑AdS black hole background, quasinormal modes correspond to poles of the retarded Green's function in the complex frequency plane, and their distribution governs thermalization in the dual CFT. The method of complex scaling (or the method of matched asymptotic expansions) computes these modes. For pure AdS, the spectrum is discrete and there is no scattering in the usual sense; instead one studies the Poincaré map or the boundary‑to‑boundary map. Examiners may ask for the conformal weight Δ = (d−1)/2 + √((d−1)2/4 + m2L2), the relation between the bulk mass and the boundary operator dimension, or the difference between dS and AdS scattering (spacelike vs timelike boundary, unitary S‑matrix vs boundary‑value problem). A common confusion is treating AdS as having a standard S‑matrix; it does not, because waves reflect off the timelike boundary.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Exponential Type and the Trudinger‑Moser Inequality

In two spatial dimensions, the energy‑critical NLS is replaced by exponential nonlinearities because the Sobolev embedding H1(ℝ2) Lp fails for p = ∞. The Trudinger‑Moser inequality states sup‖∇u‖2≤1 ∫ (e4π|u|2 − 1) < ∞, and 4π is sharp. The nonlinear Schrödinger equation with exponential nonlinearity i∂tu + Δu = u(eα|u|2 − 1) arises in the mean‑field limit of bosonic systems and in the Chern‑Simons‑Schrödinger model. For α < 4π, the nonlinearity is subcritical and standard Strichartz estimates yield local well‑posedness and scattering for small data. At the critical α = 4π, the problem is delicate: the nonlinearity is not locally Lipschitz in H1, and one must work in the Orlicz space associated with the Young function Φ(s) = e4πs2 − 1. The concentration‑compactness method of Ibrahim‑Masoudi‑Nakanishi (2011) and others shows that if the mass is below a threshold (related to the ground state of the elliptic problem −ΔQ + Q = Q(e4π|Q|2 − 1)), then solutions are global and scatter. The proof uses a refined profile decomposition in H1 with the Trudinger‑Moser inequality controlling the exponential tails. The key estimate is a Strichartz‑type bound for the nonlinearity in the dual Orlicz space. For the focusing case, blowup can occur at the critical mass, and the blowup profile is given by the ground state. Examiners may ask for the sharp constant in the Trudinger‑Moser inequality, the definition of the Orlicz norm, or the mass threshold for scattering. A frequent mistake is to apply the standard Lp Strichartz estimates to the exponential nonlinearity without passing to Orlicz spaces; the duality pairing fails because the nonlinearity is not in any Lp'.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Logarithmic Type

The logarithmic Schrödinger equation i∂tu + Δu = u log|u|2 appears in quantum information theory (Bialynicki‑Birula‑Mycielski), nuclear physics, and effective models of superfluidity. The nonlinearity is homogeneous of degree zero under scaling u → λu, so the equation is scale‑invariant in a weak sense. The energy functional E(u) = ∫ |∇u|2 − ∫ |u|2 log|u|2 is well‑defined on H1 ∩ {∫ |u|2 log|u|2 < ∞}. The logarithmic nonlinearity satisfies a gauge invariance: if u solves the equation, then eiθu also solves it. Global well‑posedness in H1 was proved by Cazenave‑Haraux (1980) using a compactness argument and the fact that the nonlinearity is locally Lipschitz from H1 to H−1. Scattering in the standard sense (convergence to a free solution in H1) fails because the nonlinearity does not decay in time; instead, one has modified scattering: u(t) = e−i log t v(t) where v(t) approaches a free solution. The phase correction log t arises from the long‑range nature of the logarithmic nonlinearity. The modified wave operators are constructed by solving the asymptotic ODE i∂ta = a log|a|2 which gives a(t) = a0 e−i log t. For the inhomogeneous equation with a potential, the Dollard‑type modification includes the potential's long‑range part. Examiners may ask to derive the modified scattering ansatz, to show that the standard wave operators do not exist, or to compute the asymptotic phase. A common trap is to claim that the solution scatters to a free solution without the logarithmic phase correction; the nonlinearity is long‑range in the sense that ∫∞ ‖u log|u|2‖H−1 dt diverges.

  • Confusing the energy‑critical exponent 4/(d−2) with the mass‑critical exponent 4/d; the former preserves Ḣ1, the latter preserves L2.
  • Applying standard Strichartz estimates to quadratic derivative nonlinearities without verifying the null condition; the derivative loss is not compensated at critical regularity.
  • Treating asymptotically AdS spacetimes as having a standard unitary S‑matrix; the timelike boundary requires boundary conditions and the scattering map is a boundary‑to‑boundary map, not an S‑matrix.
  • Using Lp spaces for exponential nonlinearities at the Trudinger‑Moser critical exponent; the correct setting is the Orlicz space associated with e4πs2 − 1.
  • Claiming standard scattering for the logarithmic NLS without the modified phase e−i log t; the nonlinearity is long‑range and the standard wave operators do not exist.
  • Overlooking the gauge invariance of the logarithmic nonlinearity when constructing modified wave operators; the phase correction must respect the U(1) symmetry.
  • Misstating the conformal weight formula for AdS/CFT; the correct relation is Δ = (d−1)/2 + √((d−1)2/4 + m2L2), not Δ = d/2 + √(d2/4 + m2L2).

Scattering Theory for the Mass‑Critical Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation and the Pseudoconformal Transformation

The mass‑critical nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) in dimension d takes the form i∂tu + Δu = ±|u|4/du. The nonlinearity preserves the L2 norm (mass), and the scaling symmetry uλ(t,x) = λd/2u(λ2t, λx) leaves the L2 norm invariant, making L2 the critical space. This distinguishes it from the energy‑critical NLS (quintic in d=3) where Ḣ1 is scaling‑critical. The pseudoconformal transformation is a hidden symmetry of the free Schrödinger equation: if v solves i∂tv + Δv = 0, then u(t,x) = (it)−d/2ei|x|2/(4t)v(−1/t, x/t) also solves it. For the mass‑critical NLS, this symmetry extends to the nonlinear equation, mapping solutions to solutions. This transformation is the key to understanding the blowup profile: the minimal‑mass blowup solution is the pseudoconformal image of the ground state Q solving −ΔQ + Q = |Q|4/dQ. The sharp Gagliardo‑Nirenberg inequality ‖u‖L2+4/d2+4/d ≤ CGN‖∇u‖L22‖u‖L24/d with optimal constant CGN = (2+4/d)/2 ‖Q‖L2−4/d governs the threshold for global existence versus blowup. If the mass is strictly below ‖Q‖L2, solutions are global and scatter (Dodson, 2016 for d=1,2; earlier results by Weinstein, Grillakis). At the critical mass ‖Q‖L2, the pseudoconformal blowup solution u(t,x) = (T−t)−d/2ei|x|2/(4(T−t))Q(x/(T−t)) exhibits log‑log or log‑log‑log corrections depending on dimension. The Kenig‑Merle concentration‑compactness/rigidity method, adapted to the mass‑critical setting by Killip‑Tao‑Visan (2008) and others, proves that any non‑scattering solution must concentrate mass at the rate of the ground state. Examiners often ask for the derivation of the pseudoconformal transformation, the sharp Gagliardo‑Nirenberg constant, or the classification of minimal‑mass blowup solutions. A common error is confusing the mass‑critical exponent 4/d with the energy‑critical exponent 4/(d−2); the former preserves L2, the latter preserves Ḣ1.

Scattering Theory for the Choquard Equation and the Schrödinger‑Newton System

The Choquard equation (also called the Schrödinger‑Newton or Hartree equation with Riesz potential) reads i∂tu + Δu = (|x|−γ * |u|2)u for 0 < γ < d. The convolution kernel |x|−γ is the Riesz potential of order γ; for γ = d−2 it reduces to the Coulomb potential in d≥3, and for γ = 1 in d=3 it models the gravitational self‑interaction of a quantum wavefunction (the Schrödinger‑Newton system). The equation is L2‑critical when γ = 2 (mass‑critical Hartree), Ḣ1‑critical when γ = 4 (energy‑critical Hartree), and intercritical otherwise. The Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality ‖|x|−γ * |f|2‖Lp ≤ CHLS‖f‖L2p/(2p−γ)2 controls the nonlinearity. The sharp constant CHLS is attained by the optimizer of the corresponding variational problem, which satisfies the elliptic Choquard equation −ΔQ + Q = (|x|−γ * |Q|2)Q. For the mass‑critical case γ = 2, the ground state Q gives the threshold for global existence versus blowup: if ‖u0‖L2 < ‖Q‖L2, solutions are global and scatter (Guo‑Huo, 2014; Cho‑Ozawa, 2015). For the energy‑critical case γ = 4, the threshold is given by the Ḣ1 norm of the ground state. The long‑range nature of the Riesz potential (decay |x|−γ) requires modified wave operators when γ ≤ 1; for γ > 1, standard wave operators exist under suitable decay assumptions on the initial data. The inverse scattering transform for the Choquard equation is not available in general, but the equation is completely integrable in d=1 for specific γ (the Benjamin‑Ono type limits). The Schrödinger‑Newton system couples the Choquard equation with the Poisson equation for the gravitational potential: i∂tψ + Δψ = φψ, −Δφ = |ψ|2. This system arises in the mean‑field limit of bosonic stars and in Penrose's model of gravity‑induced wavefunction collapse. Scattering for the Schrödinger‑Newton system is subtle because the potential φ does not decay in time; instead, one has modified scattering with a logarithmic phase correction similar to the logarithmic NLS. Examiners may ask for the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality, the classification of ground states, or the distinction between standard and modified scattering for long‑range nonlocal potentials. A frequent mistake is applying Lp Strichartz estimates directly to the nonlocal nonlinearity without using the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality to bound the convolution in dual Strichartz spaces.

Worked Example: Sharp Gagliardo‑Nirenberg for Mass‑Critical NLS
Let d=2. The mass‑critical NLS is i∂tu + Δu = ±|u|2u. Derive the sharp Gagliardo‑Nirenberg inequality ‖u‖L44 ≤ CGN‖∇u‖L22‖u‖L22 and identify the optimal constant CGN in terms of the ground state Q solving −ΔQ + Q = |Q|2Q.
Step 1: The ground state Q > 0 solves −ΔQ + Q = Q3. Multiply by Q and integrate: ‖∇Q‖22 + ‖Q‖22 = ‖Q‖44. Step 2: Pohozaev identity (multiply by x·∇Q and integrate) gives ‖∇Q‖22 = ‖Q‖44/2. Hence ‖Q‖22 = ‖Q‖44/2. Step 3: For any u ∈ H1, consider the scaling uλ(x) = λu(λx) which preserves L2 norm. The functional J(u) = ‖∇u‖22‖u‖22 / ‖u‖44 is scale‑invariant. Minimizing J yields the Euler‑Lagrange equation −Δu + ωu = λ|u|2u, which after rescaling becomes the ground state equation. The minimizer is Q (up to symmetries). Step 4: Compute J(Q) = ‖∇Q‖22‖Q‖22 / ‖Q‖44 = (‖Q‖44/2)(‖Q‖44/2) / ‖Q‖44 = ‖Q‖44/4. Thus ‖u‖44 ≤ (4/‖Q‖44) ‖∇u‖22‖u‖22. Using ‖Q‖44 = 2‖Q‖22, we get CGN = 2/‖Q‖22. The sharp inequality is ‖u‖44 ≤ (2/‖Q‖22) ‖∇u‖22‖u‖22.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for the scattering threshold in a critical NLS, first identify the critical space (L2 for mass‑critical, Ḣ1 for energy‑critical). Write down the corresponding ground state elliptic equation. The threshold norm is always the norm of the ground state in the critical space. For mass‑critical NLS, the threshold is ‖Q‖L2; for energy‑critical, it is ‖∇Q‖L2. Memorize the sharp Gagliardo‑Nirenberg constant CGN = (2+4/d)/2 ‖Q‖L2−4/d and the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev constant for Choquard. If the nonlinearity is long‑range (decay ≤ |x|−1), immediately flag modified scattering with a logarithmic phase correction.

Scattering Theory for the Gross‑Pitaevskii Hierarchy and the Derivation of the Cubic NLS

The Gross‑Pitaevskii (GP) hierarchy is an infinite system of equations for the k‑particle density matrices γ(k)(t) of a Bose gas in the mean‑field limit: i∂tγ(k) = [−∑j=1kΔxj, γ(k)] + b∑j=1kTrk+1[δ(xj−xk+1), γ(k+1)]. Here b is the scattering length of the two‑body interaction potential. The hierarchy arises from the BBGKY hierarchy by taking the limit N→∞, b∼N−1 (for 3D) or b∼1/|log N| (for 2D). A factorized solution γ(k) = |φ⟩⟨φ|⊗k reduces the GP hierarchy to the cubic NLS i∂tφ + Δφ = b|φ|2φ. The central mathematical problem is uniqueness of solutions to the GP hierarchy in a suitable class (e.g., the space of admissible density matrices with finite energy per particle). The Klainerman‑Machedon boardgame argument (2008) and the quantum de Finetti theorem (Lewin‑Nam‑Rougerie, 2014) provide two distinct approaches to uniqueness. The boardgame argument uses spacetime bounds (Strichartz estimates) on the collision operators to control the Duhamel expansion of the hierarchy. The quantum de Finetti theorem represents any symmetric sequence of density matrices as a convex combination of factorized states, reducing the problem to the NLS. Scattering theory for the GP hierarchy means proving that solutions with small initial data in the energy space scatter to free solutions of the hierarchy, which in turn implies scattering for the cubic NLS. The key estimate is the spacetime bound ‖Bj,k+1U(k+1)(t)γ(k+1)‖L2tH1x ≤ C‖γ(k+1)‖H1 where B is the collision operator and U the free propagator. This bound fails at the endpoint in 3D, requiring the use of the UpVp spaces or the Fourier restriction norm method (Bourgain spaces). The derivation of the GP hierarchy from the many‑body Schrödinger equation with a short‑range potential VN(x) = N2V(Nx) (3D) or VN(x) = N2βV(Nβx) (2D) was completed by Erdős‑Schlein‑Yau (2007) and Kirkpatrick‑Schlein‑Staffilani (2011). Examiners may ask for the structure of the GP hierarchy, the role of the quantum de Finetti theorem, or the difference between the 3D and 2D scaling limits. A common trap is confusing the GP hierarchy (infinite system for density matrices) with the BBGKY hierarchy (finite N system); the former is the limit of the latter, and uniqueness for the GP hierarchy is the hard part.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Double Power Type

Double power nonlinearities take the form i∂tu + Δu = |u|p1u − |u|p2u with 0 < p1 < p2. The competition between a focusing lower‑order term and a defocusing higher‑order term (or vice versa) creates rich dynamics not present in single‑power NLS. The energy functional E(u) = ½‖∇u‖22 − (1/(p1+2))‖u‖p1+2p1+2 + (1/(p2+2))‖u‖p2+2p2+2 is conserved. When p1 is L2‑subcritical (p1 < 4/d) and p2 is L2‑supercritical (p2 > 4/d), the equation exhibits a mass threshold for global existence versus blowup, but the threshold is not simply the ground state of a single equation. Instead, one considers the variational problem d(m) = inf{E(u) : ‖u‖22 = m}. The function d(m) determines the dynamics: if d(m) < 0, blowup can occur; if d(m) ≥ 0, solutions are global. The ground state for the double power equation solves −ΔQ + Q = |Q|p1Q − |Q|p2Q. For the focusing‑defocusing case (lower power focusing, higher power defocusing), there exists a unique positive radial ground state for each mass m in a certain interval. The scattering theory for small data follows from standard Strichartz estimates. For large data, the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method must be adapted to handle the two competing nonlinearities. The key difficulty is that the profile decomposition must account for the fact that the two nonlinearities scale differently. The work of Colin‑Ohta (2006), Bellazzini‑Jeanjean‑Luo (2019), and others classifies the ground states and determines the sharp threshold for scattering versus blowup. In the defocusing‑focusing case (lower power defocusing, higher power focusing), the equation can support stable standing waves even in the L2‑supercritical regime, a phenomenon absent in single‑power NLS. The virial identity d2/dt2 ∫ |x|2|u|2 = 8E(u) − 4d(p1−4/d)/(p1+2) ∫ |u|p1+2 + 4d(p2−4/d)/(p2+2) ∫ |u|p2+2 shows that the sign of the virial depends on the competition between the two terms. Examiners may ask for the virial identity, the variational characterization of the ground state, or the condition for global existence in terms of the energy‑mass functional d(m). A frequent mistake is applying the single‑power blowup criteria (e.g., negative energy implies blowup) without checking the sign of the higher‑order term, which can prevent collapse.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Fractional Power Type and the Fractional Gagliardo‑Nirenberg Inequality

Fractional power nonlinearities i∂tu + Δu = |u|pu with non‑integer p > 0 arise in models of nonlocal media and in the study of regularity of solutions. The nonlinearity F(u) = |u|pu is not smooth at u=0 when p is not an even integer, which complicates the use of Taylor expansions in perturbation theory. The fractional Gagliardo‑Nirenberg inequality ‖u‖Lp+2p+2 ≤ C‖∇u‖L2dp/2‖u‖L2p+2−dp/2 holds for p > 0, but the sharp constant and the existence of optimizers depend on the range of p. For p < 4/d (L2‑subcritical), optimizers exist and are ground states of −ΔQ + Q = |Q|pQ. For p = 4/d (mass‑critical), the optimizer is the same as for integer p. For p > 4/d (supercritical), the inequality fails in H1 but holds in Ḣsc with sc = d/2 − 2/p. The local well‑posedness theory in H1 requires p to be an even integer or the use of fractional chain rules (Kato‑Ponce, Christ‑Weinstein). For non‑integer p, the nonlinearity is not C2 in H1, so the standard contraction mapping argument in CtH1x fails; one must work in Bourgain spaces Xs,b or use the UpVp framework. The scattering theory for small data in H1 (subcritical p) follows from the Strichartz estimates and the fractional chain rule ‖F(u)‖Ws,r ≤ C‖u‖Lp1p‖∇u‖Lp2 with appropriate exponents. For large data, the concentration‑compactness method requires the profile decomposition to handle the lack of smoothness of the nonlinearity; the nonlinear profiles satisfy the same fractional NLS. The work of Guo‑Huo‑Shimomura (2014) and others establishes scattering for fractional power NLS below the energy‑critical exponent. A key technical point is the use of the fractional Leibniz rule and the Kato‑Ponce inequality to estimate the nonlinearity in the dual Strichartz space. Examiners may ask for the range of p for which the nonlinearity is locally Lipschitz in H1, the fractional Gagliardo‑Nirenberg inequality, or the adaptation of the Kenig‑Merle method to non‑smooth nonlinearities. A common trap is assuming that the standard C2 nonlinearity theory applies; for non‑integer p, the second derivative of F blows up at zero, so one cannot use the Taylor expansion F(u+v) = F(u) + F'(u)v + O(|v|2) in H1.

  • Confusing the mass‑critical exponent 4/d with the energy‑critical exponent 4/(d−2); the former preserves L2, the latter preserves Ḣ1.
  • Applying standard Lp Strichartz estimates to the Choquard nonlinearity without using the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality to bound the convolution in dual Strichartz spaces.
  • Treating the Gross‑Pitaevskii hierarchy as identical to the BBGKY hierarchy; the GP hierarchy is the infinite‑N limit, and uniqueness for the GP hierarchy is the hard part.
  • Applying single‑power blowup criteria (negative energy implies blowup) to double power NLS without checking the sign of the higher‑order term, which can prevent collapse.
  • Assuming the standard C2 nonlinearity theory applies to fractional power NLS; for non‑integer p, the second derivative blows up at zero, invalidating Taylor expansions in H1.
  • Overlooking the long‑range nature of the Riesz potential in the Choquard equation; for γ ≤ 1, standard wave operators do not exist and modified scattering with logarithmic phase is required.
  • Misstating the sharp Gagliardo‑Nirenberg constant for mass‑critical NLS; the correct constant is CGN = (2+4/d)/2 ‖Q‖L2−4/d, not the subcritical constant.

Scattering Theory for the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation with Nonzero Boundary Conditions and the Riemann‑Hilbert Problem

The inverse scattering transform (IST) for the focusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) i∂tu + ∂x2u + 2|u|2u = 0 with nonzero boundary conditions (NZBC) u(x,t) → A±eiθ± as x → ±∞ (with A± > 0) requires a fundamentally different spectral analysis than the zero‑background case. The Lax pair Ψx = UΨ, Ψt = VΨ with U = −iλσ3 + Q, Q = (0 u; −ū 0) has continuous spectrum covering the real λ‑axis when u → 0, but for NZBC the continuous spectrum becomes the union of the real axis and a segment on the imaginary axis (or a more general contour) determined by the asymptotic values. The Jost solutions Ψ±(x,λ) are defined by their asymptotic behavior Ψ± ∼ e−iλxσ3M± where M± diagonalizes the asymptotic Lax matrix. The scattering matrix S(λ) relates Ψ− and Ψ+ and its entries are analytic in the upper/lower half‑planes cut along the continuous spectrum. The Riemann‑Hilbert problem (RHP) is formulated on this contour: find a sectionally analytic matrix M(λ) with jump M+ = M−J where J is built from the reflection coefficients, and normalization M → I as λ → ∞. The solution u(x,t) is recovered from the large‑λ expansion of M. The RHP approach, developed by Zhou (1989) and refined by Biondini‑Kovačič (2014) and others, handles the non‑trivial topology of the spectral plane (genus‑one Riemann surface for symmetric NZBC) and allows the rigorous derivation of long‑time asymptotics via the Deift‑Zhou nonlinear steepest descent method. Key phenomena include dark solitons (intensity dips on a non‑zero background), grey solitons, and the modulational instability of the continuous wave background, which manifests as the growth of eigenvalues off the continuous spectrum. The spectral data include discrete eigenvalues (solitons) and reflection coefficients on the continuous spectrum; the time evolution is linear: r(λ,t) = r(λ,0)e4iλ2t. Examiners often test the distinction between the RHP for zero and nonzero backgrounds, the structure of the continuous spectrum, and the reconstruction formula u = 2i limλ→∞ λ M12.

Scattering Theory for the Davey‑Stewartson System and the d‑bar Dressing Method

The Davey‑Stewartson (DS) system i∂tu + ∂x2u + ∂y2u + 2(|u|2 + ∂xϕ)u = 0, ∂y2ϕ + ∂x2ϕ = −2∂x∂y|u|2 (DS‑II, integrable case) is a (2+1)‑dimensional generalization of NLS describing weakly nonlinear waves in deep water. Its Lax pair involves a non‑local operator: Ψy = ∂xΨ + uΨ2, Ψt = .... The inverse scattering transform for DS was developed by Ablowitz‑Fokas (1983) using the ∂‑bar (d‑bar) formalism because the spectral problem is not a standard ODE but a ∂‑bar equation ∂k̄μ = R(k) ei(kx+k̄y) μ for the eigenfunction μ(x,y,k), where R(k) is the scattering data (reflection coefficient) defined on the complex k‑plane. The d‑bar equation replaces the Riemann‑Hilbert problem; its solution is given by the Fredholm integral equation μ = 1 + (1/π) ∫∫ (R(k') ei(k'x+k̄'y) μ(k'))/(k'−k) d2k'. The potential is recovered via u(x,y) = (1/π) ∫∫ R(k) ei(kx+k̄y) μ(k) d2k. The time evolution of R is R(k,t) = R(k,0) e4i(k3+k̄3)t. The d‑bar method handles the non‑locality of the spectral problem and avoids the need for a Riemann surface. Soliton solutions correspond to R having pole singularities; the d‑bar dressing method (Zakharov‑Manakov, 1985) constructs multi‑soliton solutions by adding poles to a trivial solution. The DS system admits lump solutions (rationally decaying in all directions) and line solitons. The long‑time asymptotics via the d‑bar steepest descent (Perry, 2015) show decay t−1 for generic data. Examiners may ask for the d‑bar equation, the reconstruction formula, the difference between DS‑I (elliptic) and DS‑II (hyperbolic) spectral problems, or the role of the non‑local term ϕ in the Lax pair.

Worked Problem: Continuous Spectrum for NLS with NZBC
Consider the focusing NLS with symmetric nonzero boundary conditions u(x,t) → A as x → ±∞ (A > 0 real). The Lax matrix at infinity is U∞ = −iλσ3 + (0 A; −A 0). Find the continuous spectrum (the set of λ ∈ ℂ where U∞ has purely imaginary eigenvalues) and describe its topology.
The eigenvalues of U∞ satisfy det(−iλσ3 + Q∞ − μI) = 0 with Q∞ = (0 A; −A 0). This gives μ2 = −λ2 − A2. For the Jost solutions to be bounded (neither growing nor decaying exponentially), μ must be purely imaginary, i.e., μ = iν with ν ∈ ℝ. Then −ν2 = −λ2 − A2 ⇒ λ2 = ν2 − A2. As ν runs over ℝ, λ2 runs over [−A2, ∞). Thus the continuous spectrum in the λ‑plane is the set {λ ∈ ℂ : λ2 ∈ [−A2, ∞)}. This consists of the real axis (−∞, −A] ∪ [A, ∞) (where λ2 ≥ A2) and the imaginary segment [−iA, iA] (where λ2 ∈ [−A2, 0]). The spectrum is a cross‑shaped contour dividing the plane into four quadrants; the Riemann surface for √(λ2+A2) is genus zero (two sheets), but the uniformization variable z = λ + √(λ2+A2) maps it to the unit circle. The jump contour for the RHP is this cross, oriented appropriately.
Exam Strategy: NZBC Spectral Plane
When asked to sketch the continuous spectrum for NLS with NZBC, first compute the eigenvalues of the asymptotic Lax matrix U∞. The condition for continuous spectrum is that these eigenvalues are purely imaginary (bounded Jost solutions). This yields an equation λ2 = ν2 − A2. Plot the resulting set in the complex λ‑plane: it is always symmetric under λ → −λ and λ → λ̄. For symmetric NZBC it is a cross; for asymmetric NZBC (A+ ≠ A−) it becomes a more general contour (an ellipse or hyperbola). Memorize the symmetric case cross and the uniformization z = λ + √(λ2+A2) which maps the spectral plane to the unit circle — this is a frequent shortcut for solving the RHP.

Scattering Theory for the Kadomtsev‑Petviashvili Equation and the Inverse Scattering Transform

The Kadomtsev‑Petviashvili (KP) equation (∂tu + ∂x3u + 6u∂xu)x + 3σ2∂y2u = 0 with σ2 = ±1 (KP‑II for σ2 = 1, KP‑I for σ2 = −1) is a (2+1)‑dimensional integrable generalization of KdV describing weakly two‑dimensional long waves. Its Lax pair is Lψ = λψ, ψt = Mψ with L = ∂x2 + u + σ∂y∂x−1 (non‑local operator). The inverse scattering transform for KP was developed by Ablowitz‑Kaup‑Newell‑Segur (1974) for KP‑II and by Manakov (1976) for KP‑I using the ∂‑bar formalism. For KP‑II, the spectral problem Lψ = λψ with ψ ∼ eikx+ik2y as x → −∞ leads to a scattering transform defined by the Jost solutions ψ±. The scattering data consist of a reflection coefficient R(k,l) on the real (k,l) plane and discrete eigenvalues (solitons). The inverse problem is a ∂‑bar equation in the spectral variables: ∂k̄μ = R(k,l) ei(kx+l y) μ. The potential is recovered by u = 2∂x2 log τ where τ is the Fredholm determinant of the integral operator with kernel R. For KP‑I, the spectral problem is elliptic and the scattering data live on a different contour; the ∂‑bar equation involves both ∂k̄ and ∂l̄. The KP equation admits line solitons (KP‑II) and lump solutions (KP‑I, rationally decaying in all directions). The τ‑function formalism (Hirota bilinear method) gives multi‑soliton solutions as Wronskian or Gram determinants. The long‑time asymptotics for KP‑II (Ablowitz‑Villarroel, 1991) show that the solution decomposes into a finite number of solitons plus a dispersive tail decaying as t−1. The KP hierarchy and its reduction to the KdV hierarchy (by y‑independence) are central to the theory of integrable systems. Examiners may test the non‑local operator ∂x−1, the distinction between KP‑I and KP‑II spectral problems, the ∂‑bar equation for the inverse problem, or the τ‑function representation of solitons.

Scattering Theory for the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation on the Half‑Line and the Unified Transform Method

The initial‑boundary value problem (IBVP) for the focusing NLS i∂tu + ∂x2u + 2|u|2u = 0 on the half‑line x > 0 with boundary condition u(0,t) = g0(t) (Dirichlet) or ∂xu(0,t) = g1(t) (Neumann) cannot be solved by the standard IST on the line because the spatial domain is not translation invariant. The unified transform method (Fokas method, 1997) extends the IST to IBVPs by using the Lax pair to derive a global relation coupling the initial data u0(x) and the boundary values g0(t), g1(t). For the NLS Lax pair Ψx = UΨ, Ψt = VΨ with spectral parameter λ, one defines eigenfunctions Ψj(x,t,λ) (j = 1,2,3) normalized at the three corners of the domain: (0,0), (∞,0), (0,T). The global relation is ∫0∞ e2iλx [u0(x) Ψ2(x,0,λ)] dx = e2iλ2T ∫0T e−2iλ2t [g0(t) Ψ1(0,t,λ) + g1(t) Ψ1(0,t,λ)] dt (schematic). This relation holds for all λ in the complex plane and allows one to express the unknown boundary values in terms of the given data by analyzing the analyticity domains of the eigenfunctions. The solution is then given by a Riemann‑Hilbert problem formulated on the union of the real λ‑axis and the boundary of the analyticity domains. The method yields explicit integral representations for the solution and provides a rigorous framework for well‑posedness (existence, uniqueness, continuous dependence). For linearizable boundary conditions (e.g., g1 = αg0), the global relation can be solved algebraically. The unified transform has been extended to the nonlinear Schrödinger equation with NZBC on the half‑line (Biondini‑Fokas, 2018) and to other integrable PDEs (KdV, sine‑Gordon). Examiners often ask for the global relation, the definition of the three eigenfunctions, the analyticity domains in the λ‑plane, or the distinction between the unified transform and the standard IST on the line.

Scattering Theory for the Nonlinear Schrödinger Equation and the Soliton Resolution Conjecture

The soliton resolution conjecture (SRC) is a central open problem in the theory of dispersive PDEs: for a large class of initial data, the solution of an integrable or non‑integrable nonlinear dispersive equation decomposes asymptotically as t → ±∞ into a finite sum of solitons (localized, non‑dispersive waves) plus a radiation term that disperses to zero in local norms. For the focusing cubic NLS in one dimension, the conjecture is a theorem for generic initial data in H1(ℝ) (or L2 for mass‑subcritical cases) due to the complete integrability and the IST. The proof uses the Deift‑Zhou nonlinear steepest descent analysis of the Riemann‑Hilbert problem: the long‑time asymptotics of the solution are obtained by deforming the jump contour to steepest descent paths for the phase θ(λ) = λx/t + 2λ2. The discrete spectrum (eigenvalues) gives rise to solitons; the continuous spectrum (reflection coefficient) gives the dispersive radiation. The asymptotic description is u(x,t) = ∑j=1N Qj(x − vjt) + O(t−1/2) in L∞, where Qj are soliton profiles. For non‑integrable equations (e.g., NLS with non‑integrable perturbations, or higher‑dimensional NLS), the SRC is largely open. Major progress includes: (i) the energy‑critical NLS in ℝ3 (Kenig‑Merle, 2006; Duyckaerts‑Kenig‑Merle, 2014) where soliton resolution holds for radial data below the ground state threshold; (ii) the mass‑critical NLS in ℝ2 (Killip‑Visan, 2010); (iii) the cubic NLS in ℝ2 (Dodson, 2016) for L2 data. The key tools are concentration‑compactness, rigidity theorems, and the linear profile decomposition. The conjecture fails for data leading to blow‑up (finite‑time singularity) or for non‑generic data (e.g., multi‑soliton interactions with resonant frequencies). Examiners may ask for the statement of the SRC, the role of the IST in proving it for 1D cubic NLS, the difference between integrable and non‑integrable cases, or the connection to the Kenig‑Merle roadmap (scattering vs. blow‑up dichotomy).

  • Confusing the continuous spectrum for NLS with zero boundary conditions (real λ‑axis) and nonzero boundary conditions (cross‑shaped contour including an imaginary segment).
  • Assuming the Riemann‑Hilbert problem for the Davey‑Stewartson system is formulated on a contour in the complex plane; it is a ∂‑bar equation on the whole plane.
  • Stating that the KP‑I and KP‑II equations have the same spectral problem; KP‑I is elliptic, KP‑II is hyperbolic, leading to different ∂‑bar formulations.
  • Thinking the unified transform method for the half‑line NLS reduces to the standard IST when the boundary condition is zero; the global relation still couples initial and boundary data, and the RHP is on a different contour.
  • Claiming the soliton resolution conjecture is proved for all nonlinear dispersive equations; it is a theorem only for completely integrable 1D equations and for specific non‑integrable cases with symmetry assumptions.
  • Misidentifying the time evolution of the scattering data for NZBC NLS; it is r(λ,t) = r(λ,0)e4iλ2t, not e−4iλ2t as in some conventions.
  • Overlooking that the d‑bar dressing method for DS constructs solitons by adding poles to the reflection coefficient, whereas the standard IST uses discrete eigenvalues.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Caffarelli‑Silvestre Extension

The fractional Schrödinger operator (−Δ)^s + V(x) with 0 < s < 1 introduces a nonlocal kinetic term that cannot be reduced to a differential operator of finite order. The Caffarelli‑Silvestre extension realizes (−Δ)^s as a Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map for a degenerate elliptic problem in one higher dimension: for a function u(x) on ℝ^n, define U(x,y) on ℝ^n × (0,∞) solving div(y1‑2s ∇U) = 0 with U(x,0) = u(x). Then (−Δ)^s u = −C_s limy→0 y1‑2s ∂_y U. This extension transforms the nonlocal scattering problem into a local one on a weighted half‑space, allowing the use of standard trace‑class and limiting‑absorption techniques. The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e-itH0 exist and are complete provided the potential V belongs to a suitable Kato class adapted to the fractional order, for instance V ∈ Ln/(2s),∞(ℝ^n) when n > 2s. The S‑matrix is defined as S = Ω_+* Ω_- and inherits unitarity from the self‑adjointness of H. Analytic continuation of the resolvent (H − z)-1 across the continuous spectrum yields resonances as poles of the meromorphic continuation on the logarithmic cover of ℂ\[0,∞). The Birman‑Schwinger principle adapts to the fractional setting: the number of bound states below zero equals the number of eigenvalues of the compact operator |V|1/2 (H0 + κ)-1 |V|1/2 exceeding one, where κ > 0 is a spectral parameter. Recent work shows that the spectral shift function ξ(λ) satisfies a trace formula ξ(λ) = (1/π) Im tr log S(λ) and obeys the Krein‑type identity ∫ ξ(λ) φ(λ) dλ = tr(φ(H) − φ(H0)) for suitable test functions φ. These results provide a rigorous framework for inverse scattering: knowledge of the scattering amplitude at all energies determines the potential V uniquely within the class of compactly supported, sufficiently regular functions.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Manifolds with Asymptotically Euclidean Ends and the Radiation Condition

Let (M,g) be a complete non‑compact Riemannian manifold that coincides with ℝ^n outside a compact set, i.e. M = K ∪ (ℝ^n B_R) with a smooth metric g that approaches the Euclidean metric at infinity. The Laplace‑Beltrami operator Δ_g is essentially self‑adjoint on C_c^∞(M). The free Hamiltonian H0 = −Δ_g on the Euclidean end is unitarily equivalent to the standard Laplacian on ℝ^n via the natural identification. The perturbed Hamiltonian H = H0 + V, where V is a short‑range potential supported in K, defines a scattering system. The radiation condition of Sommerfeld type is formulated using the distorted plane waves ei⟨x,ω⟩ + o(|x|-(n‑1)/2) as |x|→∞ uniformly in ω ∈ Sn‑1. Existence and completeness of the wave operators follow from the Mourre estimate with the conjugate operator A = (1/2)(x·∇ + ∇·x) on the Euclidean end, extended by a smooth cutoff to the whole manifold. The S‑matrix acts on L2(Sn‑1) and its kernel S(ω,ω';λ) is the scattering amplitude. The high‑energy asymptotics of S(λ) are governed by the geodesic flow on the trapped set; if the trapped set is hyperbolic, the scattering matrix is a Fourier integral operator associated with the canonical relation of the broken geodesic flow. The Poisson operator P(λ) maps boundary data on the sphere at infinity to generalized eigenfunctions, and the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map Λ(λ) = P(λ)^* P(λ) encodes the scattering matrix via S(λ) = I − 2π i Λ(λ). Inverse scattering on such manifolds reduces to the boundary rigidity problem: the scattering matrix at all energies determines the metric g up to a diffeomorphism fixing the boundary at infinity, provided the manifold has no conjugate points and the trapped set is of measure zero.

Worked Example: High‑Energy Asymptotics of the Scattering Amplitude
Consider the Schrödinger operator H = −Δ + V on ℝ3 with V ∈ C_c^∞(ℝ3). Show that the scattering amplitude f(ω,ω';λ) admits the expansion f(ω,ω';λ) = −(1/4π) ∫ℝ3 e-i√λ ⟨ω‑ω',x⟩ V(x) dx + O(λ-1) as λ → ∞, uniformly for ω,ω' ∈ S2.
Start from the Lippmann‑Schwinger equation for the distorted plane wave ψ_+(x,ω,λ) = ei√λ ⟨ω,x⟩ − (1/4π) ∫ℝ3 ei√λ |x‑y|/|x‑y| V(y) ψ_+(y,ω,λ) dy. For large λ, the oscillatory factor ei√λ |x‑y| forces the main contribution from the region where the phase is stationary, i.e. y ≈ x. Replace ψ_+ by the incident wave ei√λ ⟨ω,y⟩ in the integral (Born approximation). Then ψ_+(x,ω,λ) = ei√λ ⟨ω,x⟩ − (1/4π) ∫ ei√λ (|x‑y|+⟨ω,y⟩) V(y)/|x‑y| dy + O(λ-1). For |x| → ∞, |x‑y| = |x| − ⟨x/|x|,y⟩ + O(|y|2/|x|). The far‑field pattern is f(ω,ω';λ) = −(1/4π) ∫ e-i√λ ⟨ω',y⟩ V(y) ei√λ ⟨ω,y⟩ dy + O(λ-1) = −(1/4π) ∫ e-i√λ ⟨ω'‑ω,y⟩ V(y) dy + O(λ-1). The remainder estimate follows from integration by parts using the smooth compact support of V, yielding the claimed O(λ-1) bound uniformly in directions.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the leading high‑energy term of the scattering amplitude, write the Born integral immediately; the stationary‑phase argument is standard and earns full credit if the remainder estimate is mentioned. Remember that the O(λ-1) bound requires V ∈ C_c^∞; for merely L1 potentials the remainder is only o(1).

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time‑Dependent Magnetic Fields and the Gauge Covariant Wave Operators

A magnetic Schrödinger operator with a time‑dependent vector potential A(t,x) and scalar potential V(t,x) reads H(t) = (i∇ + A(t,x))2 + V(t,x) on ℝ^n. The gauge transformation ψ → eiχ(t,x) ψ with χ solving ∂_t χ + A·∇χ + (1/2)|∇χ|2 = 0 removes the magnetic field from the kinetic term at the cost of introducing a modified scalar potential. The gauge‑covariant wave operators are defined by Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ U(t,0) U0(t,0)^*, where U(t,s) is the propagator for H(t) and U0(t,s) for the free Hamiltonian H0 = −Δ. Existence of Ω_± requires that the magnetic field B = ∇×A and the electric field E = −∂_t A − ∇V decay sufficiently fast in time, e.g. ‖B(t)‖L^∞ + ‖E(t)‖L^∞ ≤ C⟨t⟩-1‑ε. Under these conditions the Cook‑Kuroda method yields strong limits, and completeness follows from the Enss geometric method adapted to the magnetic flow. The S‑matrix S = Ω_+^* Ω_- is unitary on the absolutely continuous subspace of H0. Its integral kernel S(ω,ω';λ) satisfies the magnetic eikonal equation at high energies: the phase is the classical action ∫ (|p|2/2 + A·p + V) dt along magnetic trajectories. The Aharonov‑Bohm effect appears as a topological phase factor exp(i∮ A·dx) in the scattering amplitude when the magnetic flux is confined to a solenoid. For periodic time dependence A(t+T,x) = A(t,x), Floquet theory provides a quasi‑energy spectrum; the scattering matrix becomes a block operator on the Fourier coefficients in time, and resonances correspond to poles of the meromorphic continuation of the Floquet‑S‑matrix. The Birman‑Schwinger principle extends: the number of bound states of the time‑averaged Hamiltonian equals the number of eigenvalues of the time‑averaged Birman‑Schwinger operator exceeding one.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Critical Sobolev Exponent and the Profile Decomposition

Consider the focusing nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂_t u + Δu + |u|4/(n‑2) u = 0 on ℝ^n, n ≥ 3, where the power is the Sobolev‑critical exponent. The conserved energy E(u) = (1/2)∫|∇u|2 − (n‑2)/(2n) ∫|u|2n/(n‑2) and mass M(u) = ∫|u|2 control the dynamics. Scattering theory aims to prove that for initial data u0 ∈ H1 with energy below the ground state threshold, the solution exists globally and scatters to a free solution in both time directions. The concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Kenig‑Merle provides the roadmap: assume a minimal‑energy non‑scattering solution exists; by profile decomposition (Bahouri‑Gérard) one extracts a sequence of profiles that are either dispersive or concentrate. The linear profile decomposition writes a bounded sequence in H1 as a sum of rescaled, translated, and modulated profiles plus a remainder small in the Strichartz norm. The nonlinear profiles are constructed by solving the NLS with each linear profile as initial data. The critical element is a solution that is almost periodic modulo symmetries; its orbit is precompact in H1. Rigidity theorems (virial identity, localized virial) then rule out such an element unless it is the ground state soliton. Consequently, any solution with energy below the ground state scatters. The scattering operator maps the asymptotic state u_+ ∈ H1 to u_- ∈ H1 and is a homeomorphism on the scattering manifold. For data above the threshold, blow‑up or soliton‑plus‑radiation dynamics occur; the soliton resolution conjecture predicts decomposition into a finite sum of solitary waves plus a dispersive term. Recent advances using the concentration‑compactness method and the linear profile decomposition have established scattering for the energy‑critical NLS in ℝ3 for radial data and for the mass‑critical NLS in ℝ2.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Non‑Self‑Adjoint Boundary Conditions and the Jost Function

On the half‑line x > 0, consider the operator H = −d2/dx2 + V(x) with a complex‑valued potential V ∈ L1(ℝ_+) and a non‑self‑adjoint boundary condition ψ'(0) = h ψ(0), where h ∈ ℂ ℝ. The Jost solution f_+(x,k) satisfies f_+(x,k) ∼ eikx as x → ∞ for Im k ≥ 0. The Jost function is defined as F(k) = f_+'(0,k) − h f_+(0,k). Zeros of F(k) in the upper half‑plane correspond to eigenvalues (bound states) of H; zeros on the real axis correspond to spectral singularities where the resolvent fails to be bounded. The scattering matrix for this non‑self‑adjoint problem is S(k) = F(-k)/F(k) for real k, which is no longer unitary but satisfies |S(k)| = 1 only when h ∈ ℝ. The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e-itH0 exist and are complete on the absolutely continuous subspace provided V satisfies a weighted integrability condition ∫0^∞ (1+x) |V(x)| dx < ∞. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) is defined via the Krein trace formula tr(φ(H) − φ(H0)) = ∫ ξ(λ) φ'(λ) dλ for φ ∈ C_c^∞(ℝ). For non‑self‑adjoint H, ξ(λ) can be complex; its real part gives the usual density of states shift, while its imaginary part measures the non‑unitarity of the scattering matrix. Inverse scattering on the half‑line with non‑self‑adjoint boundary conditions is solved by the Marchenko integral equation with a modified kernel that incorporates the boundary parameter h. Uniqueness holds: the scattering data {S(k), bound state norming constants} together with h determine V uniquely. This framework underlies the analysis of PT‑symmetric quantum mechanics where h is chosen to make the spectrum real despite non‑Hermiticity.

  • Confusing the fractional Laplacian (−Δ)^s with a local differential operator of order 2s; the Caffarelli‑Silvestre extension is essential for trace‑class arguments.
  • Assuming the radiation condition on asymptotically Euclidean manifolds is identical to the Euclidean Sommerfeld condition; the metric perturbation modifies the phase at order |x|-(n‑1)/2.
  • Neglecting the gauge‑covariant phase in time‑dependent magnetic scattering; the Aharonov‑Bohm factor appears only after a proper gauge transformation.
  • Applying the standard profile decomposition to the energy‑critical NLS without rescaling the profiles to the critical Sobolev norm; the scaling symmetry must be included.
  • Treating the Jost function for non‑self‑adjoint boundary conditions as if it were real‑valued on the real axis; its zeros can lie on the real line, producing spectral singularities.
  • Overlooking that the S‑matrix for non‑self‑adjoint half‑line problems is not unitary; the spectral shift function becomes complex.
  • Assuming completeness of wave operators for long‑range magnetic fields without the Dollard modification; the phase correction is mandatory.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order on Bounded Domains and the Caffarelli‑Silvestre Extension

The extension of scattering theory to bounded domains with fractional Laplacian potentials requires a careful blend of the Caffarelli‑Silvestre extension technique and the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map. On a smooth bounded domain Ω ⊂ ℝⁿ, the operator (−Δ)ˢ with 0 < s < 1 is realized as the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann operator for the degenerate elliptic problem div(y¹⁻²ˢ∇U) = 0 in the cylinder Ω × (0,∞) with U = u on Ω × {0}. The scattering matrix S(λ) is then defined via the generalized eigenfunctions of the extended problem, and its meromorphic continuation to the complex plane yields resonances as poles of the meromorphic continuation of the resolvent (H − λ)⁻¹. The key analytical tool is the Poisson operator P(λ) mapping boundary data to solutions of the extended equation; its adjoint gives the trace of the normal derivative weighted by y¹⁻²ˢ. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limₜ→±∞ eⁱᵗᴴ e⁻ⁱᵗᴴ⁰ exist and are complete on the absolutely continuous spectrum provided the potential V belongs to the Kato class Kₛ(Ω) and satisfies a weighted integrability condition ∫Ω (1+dist(x,∂Ω))|V(x)| dx < ∞. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) for this setting is expressed through the Birman‑Krein formula det S(λ) = e⁻²πⁱ ξ(λ), and its regularity reflects the smoothness of V. Recent work by Caffarelli, Salsa, and Silvestre (2008) and later by Chang‑Lara and Dávila (2014) established the existence and completeness of wave operators for fractional Schrödinger operators on bounded Lipschitz domains, using a Mourre estimate adapted to the extended problem. The scattering amplitude f(θ,ω;λ) admits an asymptotic expansion in powers of λ⁻¹/², with the leading term given by the Fourier transform of V composed with the Poisson kernel. This framework also accommodates non‑local boundary conditions of Robin type, where the boundary parameter enters the definition of the Jost function and modifies the unitarity of the S‑matrix.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time‑Dependent Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Modulation Space Method

When the nonlinearity is both nonlocal and explicitly time‑dependent, the standard Strichartz estimates are insufficient to control the long‑time dynamics. The modulation space Mp,q_s(ℝⁿ), introduced by Feichtinger (1983) and later refined by Gröchenig, provides a frequency‑uniform decomposition that captures the interplay between dispersion and nonlocal interaction. For the equation i∂ₜu + Δu = (V * |u|²)u + F(t,x,u), where V ∈ L¹(ℝⁿ) is a convolution kernel and F is a time‑dependent perturbation, one constructs the wave operators in the modulation space setting by proving that the Duhamel term defines a contraction on a suitable ball in Mp,q_s. The key estimate is the boundedness of the linear propagator eⁱᵗΔ on Mp,q_s, which holds for 1 ≤ p,q ≤ ∞ and s ∈ ℝ, together with the convolution estimate ‖V * f‖Mp,q_s ≤ C‖V‖L¹‖f‖Mp,q_s. The time‑dependence of F is handled by assuming F(t,·) belongs to a modulation space uniformly in t and satisfies a Lipschitz condition in u with a small constant. Under these hypotheses, the wave operators Ω± exist as strong limits in Mp,q_s and are asymptotically complete, meaning the range of Ω± coincides with the absolutely continuous subspace of the full Hamiltonian. The scattering operator S = Ω₊*Ω₋ is then a unitary operator on the modulation space, and its matrix elements can be expressed via a time‑ordered exponential involving the interaction picture. This approach, developed by Wang, Zhao, and Guo (2019), extends earlier results of Kato and Yajima (1989) on time‑dependent potentials to the nonlocal nonlinear regime and provides a robust framework for proving scattering for a wide class of Hartree‑type equations with external driving fields.

Worked Example: Verifying the Modulation Space Contraction
Consider the Cauchy problem i∂ₜu + Δu = (|x|⁻¹ * |u|²)u on ℝ³ with initial data u₀ ∈ M2,10(ℝ³). Show that the Duhamel operator Φ(u)(t) = -i∫₀ᵗ eⁱ(ᵗ⁻ˢ)Δ (|x|⁻¹ * |u(s)|²)u(s) ds defines a contraction on the ball B_R = {u ∈ C(ℝ; M2,10) : supₜ ‖u(t)‖M2,10 ≤ R} for sufficiently small R.
Step 1: Use the boundedness of eⁱᵗΔ on M2,10, i.e., ‖eⁱᵗΔ f‖M2,10 ≤ C‖f‖M2,10. Step 2: Apply the convolution estimate with V(x)=|x|⁻¹ ∈ L3,∞ (weak‑L³) and the embedding M2,10 L^∞ to obtain ‖(|x|⁻¹ * |u|²)u‖M2,10 ≤ C‖u‖M2,10³. Step 3: Estimate the Duhamel term: ‖Φ(u)(t)‖M2,10 ≤ C∫₀ᵗ ‖u(s)‖M2,10³ ds ≤ C T R³ for t ∈ [0,T]. Step 4: Choose T = 1 and R such that C R² < 1/2, then Φ maps B_R into itself and is a contraction. By Banach fixed‑point theorem a unique global solution exists in C(ℝ; M2,10). The wave operators follow by standard Cook’s method using the same estimates on the difference of interacting and free evolutions.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for scattering in a modulation space, first identify the linear propagator bounds on that space, then check the nonlinear estimate using the algebra property of the modulation space (often Mp,1_s is an algebra for s > n(1/2 - 1/p)). Write the contraction mapping argument explicitly; examiners award marks for each estimate cited.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Non‑Self‑Adjoint Boundary Conditions on Metric Graphs and the Jost Matrix

Metric graphs (quantum graphs) with non‑self‑adjoint vertex conditions provide a rich laboratory for scattering theory beyond the standard self‑adjoint setting. On a finite or infinite graph Γ with edges e ≅ [0,ℓₑ] (ℓₑ possibly infinite), the Hamiltonian acts as H = -d²/dx² + Vₑ(x) on each edge, coupled at vertices by boundary conditions of the form A Ψ(v) + B Ψ'(v) = 0, where Ψ(v) and Ψ'(v) collect the values and outgoing derivatives at the vertex v. When the matrices A, B are not such that the vertex condition is self‑adjoint (i.e., A B* ≠ B A*), the operator H is non‑self‑adjoint and its spectrum may contain complex eigenvalues and spectral singularities. The Jost matrix J(k) is defined by matching the Jost solutions fₑ(x,k) ∼ eikx on each outgoing lead at infinity; its determinant det J(k) is the Jost function whose zeros in the upper half‑plane correspond to bound states (complex eigenvalues) and zeros on the real axis correspond to spectral singularities where the resolvent fails to be bounded. The scattering matrix S(k) = J(-k) J(k)⁻¹ is no longer unitary but satisfies the symmetry S(k)* S(k) = I + 2i Im(k) J(k)*⁻¹ Im(J(k)) J(k)⁻¹, reflecting the non‑conservation of probability flux. Wave operators Ω± = s‑limₜ→±∞ eitH e-itH₀ exist on the absolutely continuous subspace provided the potentials Vₑ satisfy ∑ₑ ∫₀ℓₑ (1+x) |Vₑ(x)| dx < ∞ and the vertex matrices satisfy a uniform non‑degeneracy condition det(A + i k B) ≠ 0 for real k. Completeness holds under a Mourre estimate adapted to the graph geometry, using the conjugate operator A = (1/2)(p·x + x·p) on each edge. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) becomes complex‑valued; its real part gives the usual density‑of‑states shift, while its imaginary part quantifies the non‑unitarity of S(λ). Inverse scattering on such graphs is solved by a system of coupled Marchenko equations incorporating the vertex scattering data; uniqueness of the potential and vertex conditions from the scattering matrix on the leads has been proved by Berkolaiko, Carlson, and Fulling (2012) for star graphs and extended to general graphs by Kostrykin and Schrader (2015).

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Relativistic Corrections in the Presence of Strong Magnetic Fields and the Foldy‑Wouthuysen Transformation

For a Dirac particle in a strong external magnetic field B(x) = ∇ × A(x), the Foldy‑Wouthuysen (FW) transformation block‑diagonalizes the Dirac Hamiltonian H_D = α·(p - eA) + βm + eφ into even and odd parts up to a chosen order in 1/m. The resulting effective Schrödinger‑Pauli Hamiltonian H_FW = (p - eA)²/2m + eφ - (e/2m) β Σ·B + (1/8m³)[(p - eA)², [(p - eA)², eφ]] + … contains the Zeeman term, the Darwin term, and higher‑order relativistic corrections.
