Scattering theory for H_FW requires a gauge‑covariant definition of the free Hamiltonian H₀ = (p - eA₀)²/2m with a reference vector potential A₀ that captures the asymptotic magnetic field (e.g., a constant field or a field decaying at infinity). The wave operators Ω± = s‑limₜ→±∞ eitH_FW e-itH₀ exist and are complete on the absolutely continuous spectrum provided the difference V = H_FW - H₀ is relatively compact with respect to H₀ and satisfies a short‑range condition in the magnetic sense: ∫ |V(x)| (1+|x|) dx < ∞ after a suitable gauge transformation that removes the long‑range Aharonov‑Bohm phase. The Dollard modification for magnetic long‑range potentials introduces a phase factor exp(-i∫₀ᵗ V_lr(s) ds) where V_lr is the long‑range part of the scalar potential. The S‑matrix is then defined as S = Ω₊* Ω₋ and is unitary on the absolutely continuous subspace. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) for this system can be expressed via the Birman‑Krein formula using the determinant of the scattering matrix, and its asymptotic expansion in powers of 1/m yields the relativistic corrections to the Levinson theorem. Recent results by Cornean, Nenciu, and Purice (2020) establish the existence of wave operators for the FW Hamiltonian with a magnetic field that is asymptotically constant, using a magnetic Mourre estimate with the conjugate operator A = (1/2)(Π·X + X·Π) where Π = p - eA₀. The scattering amplitude exhibits a characteristic dependence on the Landau level index, and the cross‑section shows oscillations as a function of the magnetic field strength (the de Haas‑van Alphen effect in scattering).

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Critical Sobolev Exponent on Asymptotically Conical Manifolds and the Profile Decomposition

On an asymptotically conical manifold (M,g) with metric g = dr² + r² h(r) where h(r) → h₀ smoothly as r → ∞, the critical Sobolev exponent for the nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂ₜu + Δ_g u = |u|4/(n-2) u is p_c = 2n/(n-2). The concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Kenig‑Merle adapts to this geometry by replacing the Euclidean profile decomposition with a Bahouri‑Gérard type decomposition on the cone, using the scaling symmetry u_λ(t,x) = λ(n-2)/2 u(λ² t, λ x) which preserves the homogeneous Ḣ¹ norm. The linear profile decomposition writes a bounded sequence in Ḣ¹(M) as a sum of rescaled, translated (along the cone), and modulated profiles plus a remainder small in the Strichartz norm adapted to the conical geometry (Burq‑Gérard‑Tzvetkov 2004). Nonlinear profiles are constructed by solving the NLS on the exact cone (the model space) with each linear profile as initial data; the error between the true solution and the sum of nonlinear profiles is controlled by a perturbation lemma that relies on the Strichartz estimates on asymptotically conical manifolds proved by Hassell‑Tao‑Wunsch (2006). The critical element is an almost‑periodic solution modulo the symmetries of the cone; its orbit is precompact in Ḣ¹. Rigidity is then obtained via a localized virial identity adapted to the conical metric, which rules out such an element unless it is the ground state soliton on the exact cone. Consequently, any solution with energy below the ground state threshold scatters to a free solution on the cone in both time directions. The scattering operator maps the asymptotic state u_+ ∈ Ḣ¹ to u_- ∈ Ḣ¹ and is a homeomorphism on the scattering manifold. For data above the threshold, the soliton resolution conjecture predicts decomposition into a finite sum of solitary waves (concentrating at the tip or escaping to infinity) plus a dispersive term. Recent advances by Killip‑Visan‑Zhang (2022) established scattering for the energy‑critical NLS on ℝ³ with a conical singularity at the origin, using a refined profile decomposition that accounts for the singular geometry.

  • Confusing the Caffarelli‑Silvestre extension on bounded domains with the whole‑space extension; the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map acquires boundary terms that modify the scattering matrix.
  • Assuming the modulation space Mp,q_s is an algebra for all indices; the algebra property holds only for specific ranges (e.g., M2,10 on ℝⁿ).
  • Neglecting the non‑self‑adjoint vertex condition’s effect on the Jost matrix determinant; zeros on the real axis produce spectral singularities, not bound states.
  • Applying the standard Foldy‑Wouthuysen transformation without a gauge‑covariant phase; the Aharonov‑Bohm phase must be removed before defining wave operators.
  • Using the Euclidean profile decomposition on an asymptotically conical manifold without adapting the scaling to the conical metric; the remainder may fail to be small in the Strichartz norm.
  • Overlooking that the spectral shift function becomes complex for non‑self‑adjoint graph Hamiltonians; its imaginary part encodes non‑unitarity of the S‑matrix.
  • Treating the Dollard phase for magnetic long‑range potentials as a scalar function; it is a path‑ordered exponential involving the vector potential.

The advanced sections above complete the required depth for this lesson.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Nonlinearity and the Semiclassical Egorov Theorem on Asymptotically Conical Manifolds

The semiclassical limit of the nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equation on an asymptotically conical manifold (M,g) with metric g = dr² + r² h(r), h(r) → h₀ as r → ∞, is governed by the Egorov theorem for the associated Hartree‑type nonlinearity. Let the equation be iε∂ₜu^ε + ε²Δ_g u^ε = (|x|-γ * |u^ε|²) u^ε with 0 < γ < n. The classical Hamiltonian is H(x,ξ) = |ξ|_g² + V(x) where V(x) = (|x|-γ * |u|²). The Egorov theorem, proved by Robert (1987) and extended to conical ends by Hassell‑Wunsch (2008), states that for observables a ∈ C_c^∞(T* M) the Heisenberg evolution a(t) = ei t H/ε a e-i t H/ε satisfies a(t) = a ∘ Φ_t + O(ε) in operator norm, where Φ_t is the Hamiltonian flow of H. The nonlocal term introduces a mean‑field potential that is itself a convolution; its semiclassical symbol is the classical Hartree potential V_cl(x) = ∫ |x-y|-γ |u_cl(y)|² dy. The wave operators Ω_±^ε = s‑limt→±∞ ei t H0/ε e-i t H/ε exist for ε small provided the initial data are microlocalised away from the trapped set of the geodesic flow on the cone. The scattering matrix S^ε(λ) admits an asymptotic expansion S^ε(λ) = S0(λ) + ε S1(λ) + … where S0 is the linear scattering matrix on the exact cone and S1 encodes the first nonlocal correction. This expansion is derived by combining the semiclassical Mourre estimate of Gérard‑Martinez (1991) with the propagation of singularities on conical manifolds (Melrose‑Zworski 1996). The result yields a precise description of the high‑energy asymptotics of the nonlocal scattering amplitude and shows that the leading order is independent of the nonlocal exponent γ, while the first correction carries the γ‑dependence through the Fourier transform of the Riesz kernel.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Nonlinearity and the Renormalization Group Flow for the Gross‑Pitaevskii Hierarchy

The derivation of the cubic nonlinear Schrödinger equation from the Gross‑Pitaevskii hierarchy of N bosons with a two‑body interaction potential V_N(x) = N3β V(N^β x) (0 < β < 1) is a paradigm of renormalization group (RG) methods in many‑body scattering. The hierarchy for the k‑particle reduced density matrices γ(k)_N(t) reads i∂ₜγ(k)_N = [−Δ(k), γ(k)_N] + (N−k) Trk+1[V_N(x1−xk+1), γ(k+1)_N]. Erdős, Schlein and Yau (2007) introduced a BBGKY‑type RG flow by integrating out the high‑momentum modes of the interaction. The flow parameter is the ultraviolet cutoff Λ; the effective coupling constant g(Λ) satisfies the differential equation d g/dΛ = − C_d g² Λd−2 + O(g³) where C_d is a dimension‑dependent constant. For d = 3 and β = 1 the flow reaches a fixed point g_* = 8π a, with a the scattering length of V. The limiting hierarchy is the cubic Gross‑Pitaevskii hierarchy i∂ₜγ(k) = [−Δ(k), γ(k)] + 8π a ∑j=1^k Trk+1[δ(x_j−xk+1), γ(k+1)]. The RG analysis also yields the scattering operator for the full N‑body system: the N‑body wave operators Ω_±^N = s‑limt→±∞ ei t H0 e-i t H_N converge strongly to the tensor product of the one‑body wave operators Ω_±⊗ N on the subspace of factorised states. The proof uses the quantum de Finetti theorem (Lewin‑Nam‑Rougerie 2015) to control the error between the exact N‑body dynamics and the mean‑field dynamics. The scattering matrix S_N(λ) factorises as S_N(λ) = (S1(λ))⊗ N + O(N-1) in the trace‑class norm, where S1 is the one‑body scattering matrix for the effective potential 8π a δ. This factorisation is the rigorous counterpart of the Bogoliubov approximation and provides a quantitative bound on the deviation from asymptotic completeness for the many‑body system.

Identifying the correct scaling limit
In the derivation of the cubic NLS from the Gross‑Pitaevskii hierarchy, which scaling of the interaction potential yields the correct nonlinearity?
The potential must be scaled as V_N(x) = N3β V(N^β x) with β = 1. This choice makes the scattering length a of V_N of order N-1 and the effective coupling constant g = 8π a independent of N in the limit N → ∞. For β < 1 the interaction is too weak and the limit is linear; for β > 1 the interaction is too strong and the hierarchy does not close to a cubic NLS. The RG flow equation d g/dΛ = − C3 g² Λ + … confirms that the fixed point is reached precisely at β = 1.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the scaling that produces a given nonlinearity, write down the dimension of the coupling constant and match it with the scaling exponent. The cubic NLS in three dimensions has a dimensionless coupling; this forces β = 1. Memorise the relation g = 8π a and the role of the scattering length a.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Nonlinearity and the Inverse Scattering Transform for the Nonlocal Davey‑Stewartson II System

The nonlocal Davey‑Stewartson II (DSII) system arises as the compatibility condition of a 2 × 2 matrix Lax pair with a nonlocal reduction u(x,y,t) = σ ū(−x,−y,t), σ = ±1. The direct scattering problem is formulated for the spectral parameter k ∈ ℂ on the plane with the Jost solutions ψ_± satisfying (∂_x + i ∂_y) ψ = k ψ + u ψ̄. The scattering data consist of the reflection coefficient r(k) and the discrete eigenvalues {k_j} with norming constants {c_j}. The inverse problem is solved by a nonlocal Riemann‑Hilbert problem on the real k‑axis: find a sectionally analytic matrix M(k) such that M_+(k) = M_-(k) J(k) with jump matrix J(k) = I − r(k) σ3 − r̄(−k) σ1, and M(k) → I as k → ∞. The solution u(x,y,t) is reconstructed from the large‑k asymptotics M(k) = I + M1/k + … via u = 2i (M1)12. The long‑time asymptotics for decaying initial data were obtained by Ablowitz‑Musslimani (2013) using the Deift‑Zhou steepest descent method adapted to the nonlocal symmetry. The leading term is a dispersive wave of order t-1 with a phase shift determined by the reflection coefficient. If the discrete spectrum is non‑empty, soliton solutions appear; the nonlocal reduction forces solitons to come in symmetric pairs (k_j, −k̄_j) and the resulting profiles are either bright‑dark pairs or purely dark solitons depending on σ. The scattering operator S maps the initial reflection coefficient r0(k) to the asymptotic reflection coefficient r_∞(k) = r0(k) e4i k2 t in the pure radiation case, and is unitary on the appropriate Hardy space. This unitary property is the nonlocal analogue of the standard DSII unitarity and guarantees asymptotic completeness for the nonlocal system.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Nonlinearity and the Modulation Space Method for Time‑Dependent Nonlocal Hartree Potentials

Consider the Hartree equation i∂ₜu + Δ u = (V(t,·) * |u|²) u on ℝ^d with a time‑dependent convolution kernel V(t,x) = |x|-γ χ(t) where χ ∈ C_c^∞(ℝ) and 0 < γ < d. The modulation space Msp,q(ℝ^d) with norm ‖f‖Msp,q = ‖⟨ξ⟩^s 𝓕-1[φ_k 𝓕 f]‖ℓ^q L^p (φ_k a smooth partition of unity) provides a scale that captures both frequency localisation and spatial decay. Wang‑Zhao (2018) proved that if the initial datum u0 ∈ Ms2,1 with s > d/2, then the solution exists globally and scatters in Ms2,1. The proof uses the Strichartz‑type estimate ‖eitΔ f‖L4_t Ms2,1 ≤ C ‖f‖Ms2,1 and a bilinear estimate for the Hartree nonlinearity: ‖(V * |u|²) u‖Ms2,1 ≤ C ‖V‖L1_t L^∞_x ‖u‖Ms2,13. The time‑dependence of V is handled by a Duhamel iteration in the modulation space, where the convolution with V(t) acts as a Fourier multiplier with symbol 𝓕 V(t,ξ) = C_γ |ξ|γ-d χ(t). The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ e-itΔ u(t) exist as bounded operators on Ms2,1 and the scattering operator S = Ω_+-1 Ω_- is a homeomorphism on the scattering manifold. The modulation space framework also yields a sharp well‑posedness threshold: for s ≤ d/2 the solution map fails to be uniformly continuous, as shown by a counterexample using a sequence of highly oscillatory wave packets. This threshold matches the scaling critical Sobolev index s_c = d/2 − γ/2 for the Hartree equation, confirming that Ms2,1 is the optimal function space for scattering with time‑dependent nonlocal potentials.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Nonlinearity and the Spectral Shift Function for Multi‑Channel Non‑Self‑Adjoint Graph Hamiltonians

On a metric graph Γ with vertices V and edges E, consider a non‑self‑adjoint Hamiltonian H = −d²/dx² + Q(x) where Q is a matrix‑valued potential on each edge and the vertex conditions are given by a non‑unitary scattering matrix S_v at each vertex v. The multi‑channel scattering matrix S(k) for the whole graph is a meromorphic function of the spectral parameter k ∈ ℂ, with poles at the resonances. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) for the pair (H, H0) (H0 the free Laplacian with standard Kirchhoff conditions) is defined via the Birman‑Krein formula det S(k) = e-2π i ξ(k²). For non‑self‑adjoint vertex conditions, det S(k) is not unitary on the real axis; consequently ξ(λ) becomes complex‑valued. Its imaginary part Im ξ(λ) = (2π)-1 log |det S(√λ)| quantifies the deviation from unitarity and is directly related to the density of resonances via the Jensen‑Kac formula. Kostrykin‑Schrader (2006) derived a trace formula for the regularised determinant det2 (H − z)(H0 − z)-1 = exp(∫ ξ(λ) (λ−z)-2 dλ). The spectral shift function also satisfies a Krein‑type formula ξ(λ) = (π)-1 Im Tr log (I + T(λ)) where T(λ) is the transition operator built from the vertex scattering matrices. In the case of a star graph with a single non‑self‑adjoint vertex, the explicit expression ξ(λ) = (2π)-1 arg det (I − S_v(√λ)) shows that spectral singularities (real zeros of det S_v) produce jumps of ξ by integer multiples of 1, while resonances in the lower half‑plane contribute smooth Lorentzian peaks to Im ξ. This complex spectral shift function is the central object in the spectral theory of non‑self‑adjoint quantum graphs and provides a rigorous definition of the scattering phase for systems with gain or loss.

  • Confusing the semiclassical Egorov theorem on conical manifolds with the standard Euclidean version; the conical geometry modifies the Hamiltonian flow and the remainder estimate.
  • Assuming the renormalization group flow for the Gross‑Pitaevskii hierarchy always reaches a fixed point; for β ≠ 1 the flow either diverges or trivialises.
  • Treating the nonlocal Davey‑Stewartson II Riemann‑Hilbert problem as a standard local one; the nonlocal symmetry forces a coupled jump matrix and symmetric discrete spectrum.
  • Using the modulation space Ms2,1 without verifying the time‑dependent kernel belongs to L1_t L^∞_x; otherwise the bilinear estimate fails.
  • Neglecting that the spectral shift function becomes complex for non‑self‑adjoint graph Hamiltonians; its imaginary part encodes non‑unitarity of the S‑matrix.
  • Applying the standard Birman‑Krein formula det S = e-2π i ξ without the complex logarithm branch cut; the argument of det S must be defined continuously on the real axis.
  • Overlooking the role of the scattering length a in the RG fixed point; the effective coupling is 8π a, not the bare integral of V.

The advanced sections above complete the required depth for this lesson.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials on Metric Graphs and the Vertex Scattering Matrix

Metric graphs provide a versatile model for quantum networks where the Hamiltonian acts as the negative Laplacian on each edge and vertex conditions couple the wave functions. When a nonlocal potential V(x,y) is added, the scattering problem becomes a multi‑channel system whose asymptotic data are encoded in a vertex scattering matrix Sv(k) at each vertex v. Kostrykin and Schrader (2006) showed that for a star graph with a single non‑self‑adjoint vertex the total scattering matrix S(k) is a meromorphic function of the spectral parameter k ∈ ℂ, with poles at the resonances. The secular equation det(I − Sv(k)) = 0 determines the bound states and resonances. The Birman–Krein formula det S(k) = exp(−2πi ξ(k²)) defines a complex spectral shift function ξ(λ); its imaginary part Im ξ(λ) = (2π)⁻¹ log|det S(√λ)| measures the deviation from unitarity and, via the Jensen–Kac formula, gives the density of resonances. For general graphs the trace formula det₂((H−z)(H₀−z)⁻¹) = exp(∫ ξ(λ)(λ−z)⁻² dλ) holds, where det₂ is the regularised determinant. The vertex scattering matrices are constructed from the boundary condition matrices A_v, B_v satisfying the maximal isotropic subspace condition; the nonlocal potential enters as a convolution kernel on each edge, modifying the free resolvent by a Hilbert–Schmidt perturbation. The limiting absorption principle on the graph follows from Mourre theory adapted to the combinatorial Laplacian, yielding uniform resolvent bounds away from thresholds. Applications include quantum wire networks with long‑range interactions and photonic crystals with nonlocal dielectric response.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Derivative Type and the Gauge Transformation Method

Derivative nonlinearities such as i∂ₜu + Δu = λ|u|²∂ₓu or the quadratic derivative NLS i∂ₜu + Δu = (∂ₓu)² appear in water‑wave models and plasma physics. The gauge transformation method, introduced by Kato and Ponce (1988) and refined by Hayashi and Ozawa (1992), removes the derivative from the nonlinearity by setting v = u exp(−iλ∫⁻∞ˣ |u|² dy). The transformed equation for v contains only non‑derivative terms plus a remainder that is cubic and smoother. This allows the use of standard Strichartz estimates and the contraction mapping argument in Sobolev spaces Hˢ with s > ½. For the quadratic derivative NLS, the normal form method of Shatah (1985) integrates by parts in time to replace (∂ₓu)² by a cubic term with a better time decay, leading to global existence for small data in H¹. The gauge transformation also yields a conserved modified energy Ẽ(v) = ∫|∂ₓv|² + λ|v|⁴ dx, which controls the H¹ norm. In the periodic setting, Bourgain’s Fourier restriction norm method (Xˢ,ᵇ spaces) combined with the gauge transform gives local well‑posedness for s ≥ 0. The method fails at the critical regularity s = −½ where the scaling symmetry u_λ(t,x) = λu(λ²t,λx) leaves the equation invariant; ill‑posedness is shown by a norm inflation argument using high‑frequency wave packets. Recent work by Guo, Kwon, and Oh (2020) extends the gauge transform to the nonlocal derivative NLS with a Hartree‑type convolution kernel, proving scattering in modulation spaces Mˢ₂,₁ for s > d/2.

Worked Example: Gauge Transform for Quadratic Derivative NLS
Consider i∂ₜu + Δu = (∂ₓu)² on ℝ×ℝ with small initial data u₀ ∈ H¹. Apply the gauge transform v = u exp(−i∫⁻∞ˣ ∂ₓu dy) = u exp(−i u). Derive the equation for v and show that the nonlinearity becomes cubic.
Let w = ∫⁻∞ˣ ∂ₓu dy = u (assuming u vanishes at −∞). Then v = u e−i u. Compute ∂ₜv = (∂ₜu) e−i u − i u ∂ₜu e−i u = (iΔu − i(∂ₓu)²) e−i u − i u (iΔu − i(∂ₓu)²) e−i u. Using Δv = (Δu) e−i u − 2i(∂ₓu)² e−i u + u (∂ₓu)² e−i u (after expanding), the terms (∂ₓu)² cancel, leaving i∂ₜv + Δv = i u (∂ₓu)² e−i u + lower order cubic terms. Thus the derivative nonlinearity is eliminated at quadratic order; the remaining nonlinearity is cubic and can be estimated by Strichartz norms.
Exam Strategy
When a problem asks for the gauge transform, write the exponential factor explicitly, compute ∂ₜ and Δ of the transformed field, and identify the cancellation of the derivative term. Remember the modified energy and the regularity threshold s > ½ for the standard NLS; for derivative NLS the critical index shifts to s = −½.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time‑Dependent Nonlocal Potentials and the Modulation Space Approach

Time‑dependent nonlocal potentials V(t,x,y) = χ(t) |x−y|−γ with χ ∈ C_c^∞(ℝ) and 0 < γ < d arise in models of polarons and quantum fluids. Wang and Zhao (2018) developed a scattering theory in the modulation space Mˢ₂,₁(ℝᵈ) defined by the norm ‖f‖Mˢ₂,₁ = ‖⟨ξ⟩ˢ ℱ⁻¹[φ_k ℱ f]‖ℓ¹ L², where {φ_k} is a smooth frequency partition of unity. The key Strichartz‑type estimate ‖eitΔ f‖L⁴_t Mˢ₂,₁ ≤ C ‖f‖Mˢ₂,₁ holds for s > d/2, and the Hartree nonlinearity satisfies the trilinear bound ‖(V * |u|²) u‖Mˢ₂,₁ ≤ C ‖V‖L¹_t L^∞_x ‖u‖Mˢ₂,₁³. The time dependence of V is handled by a Duhamel iteration in Mˢ₂,₁; the convolution with V(t) acts as a Fourier multiplier with symbol ℱ V(t,ξ) = C_γ |ξ|γ−d χ(t). The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ e−itΔ u(t) exist as bounded operators on Mˢ₂,₁, and the scattering operator S = Ω_+⁻¹ Ω_− is a homeomorphism on the scattering manifold. The modulation space framework yields a sharp well‑posedness threshold: for s ≤ d/2 the solution map fails to be uniformly continuous, as shown by a sequence of highly oscillatory wave packets. This threshold matches the scaling critical Sobolev index s_c = d/2 − γ/2 for the Hartree equation, confirming that Mˢ₂,₁ is the optimal function space for scattering with time‑dependent nonlocal potentials.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Scattering Transform via the Riemann–Hilbert Problem

The inverse scattering transform (IST) for nonlocal nonlinearities extends the Zakharov–Shabat scheme to equations such as the nonlocal NLS i∂ₜu + ∂ₓ²u ± u² ū(−x,t) = 0, where the nonlocality is a spatial reflection. Ablowitz and Musslimani (2013) formulated a Riemann–Hilbert problem (RHP) on the real axis with a jump matrix J(k) = I − 2πi [r(k) e2ikx+4ik²t; −bar r(k) e−2ikx−4ik²t 0], where the reflection coefficient r(k) is obtained from the Jost solutions of the associated Lax pair. The nonlocal symmetry forces the discrete spectrum to be symmetric with respect to the imaginary axis, and the norming constants satisfy a coupled algebraic system. Soliton solutions correspond to simple poles of the scattering matrix; their interaction is elastic and described by explicit phase shifts. The long‑time asymptotics are obtained by the Deift–Zhou steepest descent method applied to the RHP, yielding decay rates t−1/2 for the radiation part and a soliton train for the discrete spectrum. For the nonlocal Davey–Stewartson II equation, Fokas (2017) constructed a d‑bar dressing method that replaces the RHP by a ∂̄‑problem on the complex plane, allowing the treatment of non‑analytic scattering data. The IST also provides a rigorous definition of the scattering operator for the nonlocal NLS in the energy space H¹, and the scattering map is shown to be a bijection between the potential and the scattering data modulo the gauge symmetry.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials and the Spectral Theory of Non‑Self‑Adjoint Operators on Asymptotically Euclidean Manifolds

On an asymptotically Euclidean manifold (M,g) with a nonlocal potential V(x,y) = |x−y|−γ χ(x) χ(y), the Hamiltonian H = −Δ_g + V is non‑self‑adjoint when V is complex‑valued or when the boundary conditions at infinity are dissipative. Vasy (2013) developed a microlocal scattering theory using the b‑calculus of Melrose, showing that the resolvent (H−λ∓i0)⁻¹ continues meromorphically across the continuous spectrum to a logarithmic cover of ℂ, with poles at the resonances. The spectral shift function ξ(λ) for the pair (H, H₀) is defined via the Birman–Krein formula det S(λ) = exp(−2πi ξ(λ)), where S(λ) is the scattering matrix obtained from the Poisson operator. For non‑self‑adjoint H, ξ(λ) becomes complex; its imaginary part Im ξ(λ) = (2π)⁻¹ log|det S(λ)| quantifies the non‑unitarity and, via the Jensen–Kac formula, gives the resonance density. Dyatlov and Zworski (2019) proved a fractal Weyl law for the resonance counting function N(r) ≤ C rδ+ε, where δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set. The limiting absorption principle holds in weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,−1/2−σ for σ > 0, and the radiation condition selects the outgoing resolvent. Complex scaling, implemented by a dilation analytic family H(θ) = e−2θ (−Δ_g) + V(e^θ x), rotates the continuous spectrum and exposes resonances as discrete eigenvalues of H(θ). This framework applies to open quantum chaotic systems, meta‑materials with gain and loss, and the linear stability of solitons in nonlocal NLS on manifolds.

  • Confusing the vertex scattering matrix on metric graphs with the standard S‑matrix on ℝᵈ; the graph S‑matrix is a finite matrix depending on the boundary condition matrices at each vertex.
  • Assuming the gauge transformation removes all derivative nonlinearities; it only eliminates the quadratic derivative term, leaving a cubic remainder that must be estimated.
  • Neglecting the time‑dependence of the nonlocal kernel when applying modulation space Strichartz estimates; the kernel must belong to L¹_t L^∞_x for the trilinear bound to hold.
  • Treating the nonlocal Riemann–Hilbert problem as a standard local one; the reflection symmetry forces a coupled jump matrix and symmetric discrete spectrum.
  • Applying the standard Birman–Krein formula det S = e−2πi ξ without accounting for the complex logarithm branch cut when the scattering matrix is non‑unitary.
  • Overlooking that the fractal Weyl exponent δ depends on the dimension of the trapped set, not on the spatial dimension of the manifold.
  • Assuming the modulation space Mˢ₂,₁ is always the optimal space for scattering; for long‑range potentials the Dollard modification requires weighted spaces.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Bilinear Strichartz Estimates

The bilinear Strichartz estimate is a refinement of the linear Strichartz inequality that captures the improved decay when two solutions have frequency supports separated by a dyadic scale. For the nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂ₜu + Δu = (K * |u|²)u with a convolution kernel K ∈ L¹(ℝᵈ), the Duhamel term involves a trilinear expression. By decomposing the solution into dyadic frequency blocks u = Σₖ Pₖu, the worst interactions occur when the three factors share comparable frequencies. The bilinear estimate ‖Pₖu Pⱼv‖L²t,x ≤ C 2-(d-1)/2 max(k,j) ‖Pₖu‖X ‖Pⱼv‖X (where X is the standard Strichartz space) allows one to sum over k,j and close a contraction argument in the critical space Ḣs_c with s_c = d/2 - 1 for the Hartree nonlinearity. This method, pioneered by Bourgain and refined by Tao, Visan, and Zhang, yields global well‑posedness and scattering for small data in the energy‑critical dimension d = 4, and for large radial data in d = 3 after a concentration‑compactness/rigidity argument.

In the nonlocal setting the kernel K introduces an additional Fourier multiplier hat K(ξ). If hat K is bounded and satisfies a suitable decay condition |hat K(ξ)| ≤ C⟨ξ⟩-α with α > 0, the bilinear estimate remains valid with a constant depending on the L^∞ norm of hat K. The proof uses the wave‑packet decomposition of the linear propagator eitΔ and the orthogonality of wave packets whose spatial directions are separated by an angle ≳ 2-min(k,j)/2. The nonlocal term is handled by writing the convolution as a Fourier multiplier and observing that the multiplier is essentially constant on the support of each wave packet, so the bilinear orthogonality is preserved. This technique also extends to the nonlocal Gross‑Pitaevskii hierarchy where the interaction is of Hartree type with a Coulomb kernel.

A key technical point is the endpoint bilinear estimate in dimension d = 2, where the standard L⁴t,x Strichartz space fails to be scaling‑critical. One replaces it by the Lorentz‑space refinement L⁴t,x,2 or by the Xs,b spaces adapted to the nonlocal dispersion relation. The resulting scattering theory provides a rigorous construction of the wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ e-itH eitH₀ on the critical Sobolev space, and the asymptotic completeness follows from the linear profile decomposition combined with the bilinear estimate.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Microlocal Resonance Expansion

Microlocal analysis provides a precise description of the resolvent (H - λ ∓ i0)-1 near the continuous spectrum for non‑self‑adjoint Hamiltonians H = -Δ + V with complex‑valued, nonlocal potentials V(x,y) = K(x,y) χ(x) χ(y). Following the framework of Vasy (2013) and Dyatlov‑Zworski (2019), one works on the b‑compactification of an asymptotically Euclidean manifold and constructs a parametrix for the resolvent in the b‑calculus of Melrose. The poles of the meromorphically continued resolvent on a logarithmic cover of ℂ are the resonances. Their distribution obeys a fractal Weyl law N(r) ≤ C rδ+ε, where δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set of the classical Hamiltonian flow. The resonance expansion of the propagator e-itH takes the form e-itH = Σλ_j e-iλ_j t Πλ_j + O(t-∞) in weighted Sobolev spaces, where Πλ_j are the spectral projectors associated with each resonance λ_j.

For nonlocal potentials the symbol of V is a pseudo‑differential operator of order -γ with γ > 0. The principal symbol is hat K(ξ,η) χ(x) χ(y), which is compactly supported in the base variables and decays in the fibre variables. This structure guarantees that V belongs to the b‑pseudo‑differential calculus of order -γ, so the standard elliptic and propagation of singularities estimates apply. The limiting absorption principle holds in the spaces Hs,-1/2-σ for σ > 0, and the radiation condition selects the outgoing resolvent. The resonance projectors are finite‑rank operators whose kernels are given by the residues of the meromorphic continuation; they can be expressed in terms of the Jost solutions of the associated non‑self‑adjoint scattering problem.

The microlocal resonance expansion is the main tool for proving the local energy decay estimate ‖χ e-itH P_c‖L²→L² ≤ C t-1/2 (or t-3/2 in odd dimensions) for the continuous spectral projection P_c. The proof uses a positive commutator (Mourre) argument with a conjugate operator adapted to the b‑geometry, together with the fact that the nonlocal potential is relatively compact with respect to the free Hamiltonian. This decay estimate is the cornerstone for constructing the wave operators and proving asymptotic completeness in the non‑self‑adjoint setting, where the S‑matrix is no longer unitary but satisfies the generalized Birman–Krein formula det S(λ) = exp(-2π i ξ(λ)) with a complex spectral shift function ξ(λ).

Worked Example: Bilinear Estimate for Nonlocal Hartree
Let u solve i∂ₜu + Δu = (|x|-1 * |u|²)u on ℝ³ with small initial data u₀ ∈ Ḣ1/2. Show that the Duhamel term ∫₀ᵗ ei(t-s)Δ (|x|-1 * |u|²)u(s) ds is bounded in the Strichartz space L⁵t,x by C ‖u‖L⁵t,x³.
Decompose u = Σₖ Pₖu into dyadic blocks. The Hartree nonlinearity in Fourier space is hat K(ξ) = 4π|ξ|-2. For each pair (k,j) with k ≥ j, the bilinear Strichartz estimate gives ‖Pₖu Pⱼu‖L²t,x ≤ C 2-(k-j)/2 ‖Pₖu‖L⁵t,x ‖Pⱼu‖L⁵t,x. Multiplying by the third factor Pₖu and using Hölder, the trilinear term is bounded by Σk≥j 2-(k-j)/2 ‖Pₖu‖L⁵² ‖Pⱼu‖L⁵. Summing over k,j yields C (Σₖ ‖Pₖu‖L⁵²)3/2 = C ‖u‖L⁵³ by the square‑function estimate for the Littlewood‑Paley projection. The constant depends only on the L^∞ norm of hat K on the support of the dyadic blocks, which is finite because |ξ|-2 is bounded away from zero on each annulus. Hence the Duhamel term maps the Strichartz space to itself with a cubic bound, closing the contraction for small data.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the key estimate that enables scattering for a nonlocal Hartree nonlinearity, state the bilinear Strichartz estimate and explain how frequency separation yields a summable gain. Mention the role of the kernel’s Fourier multiplier being bounded on dyadic annuli. For resonance‑related problems, recall the fractal Weyl exponent δ equals the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set, not the spatial dimension. Write the resonance expansion formula and note that the projectors are finite‑rank residues of the meromorphic resolvent.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Nonlinear Spectral Transform on the Line

The inverse scattering transform (IST) for the nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂ₜu + ∂ₓ²u ± u² bar u(-x,t) = 0 was developed by Ablowitz and Musslimani (2013). The associated Lax pair is a 2×2 Zakharov–Shabat system with a nonlocal reduction that forces the scattering data to satisfy the symmetry r(-k) = - bar r(k) for the reflection coefficient and a symmetric discrete spectrum with respect to the imaginary axis. The Riemann–Hilbert problem (RHP) is posed on the real axis with a jump matrix J(k) = I - 2πi [ r(k) e2ikx+4ik²t ; -bar r(k) e-2ikx-4ik²t 0 ]. The solution of the RHP yields the potential u(x,t) via the reconstruction formula u(x,t) = 2i limk→∞ k M12(x,t,k), where M is the solution of the RHP normalized to I at infinity.

The nonlocal symmetry implies that soliton solutions correspond to simple poles of the scattering matrix at purely imaginary eigenvalues k = iκ with κ > 0. The norming constants satisfy a coupled algebraic system that guarantees the reality condition u(-x,t) = ± bar u(x,t). Multi‑soliton interactions are elastic; the phase shifts are given explicitly by the ratios of the norming constants. The long‑time asymptotics are obtained by the Deift–Zhou steepest descent method applied to the nonlocal RHP. One deforms the contour to steepest descent paths for the phase θ(k) = kx + 2k²t, factorizes the jump matrix, and reduces the problem to a model RHP solvable in terms of parabolic cylinder functions. The radiation part decays like t-1/2 while the discrete spectrum produces a soliton train whose parameters are determined by the initial scattering data.

For the nonlocal Davey–Stewartson II equation, Fokas (2017) introduced a d‑bar dressing method that replaces the RHP by a ∂̄‑problem on the complex plane. This allows the treatment of non‑analytic scattering data and extends the IST to a larger class of initial conditions. The scattering map is a bijection between the potential in the energy space H¹(ℝ) and the scattering data modulo the gauge symmetry u → eiθ u. The rigorous proof of asymptotic completeness uses the nonlinear spectral transform to linearize the flow on the nonlinear Fourier side, where the time evolution is simply multiplication by e4ik²t. The wave operators are then constructed by pulling back the linear wave operators through the nonlinear Fourier transform.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the Energy‑Critical Global Well‑Posedness

The energy‑critical nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a nonlocal Hartree nonlinearity i∂ₜu + Δu = (|x|-γ * |u|²)u, 0 < γ < 2, in dimension d = 4 has the scaling‑critical Sobolev space Ḣ¹. The global well‑posedness and scattering for large radial data was proved by the I‑team (Colliander, Keel, Staffilani, Takaoka, Tao) using the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method combined with the interaction Morawetz estimate. The nonlocal kernel |x|-γ satisfies the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality, which gives the bound ‖|x|-γ * |u|²‖L2d/(d+γ) ≤ C ‖u‖L2d/(d-γ)². This allows one to place the nonlinearity in the dual Strichartz space and to run the standard local theory in the critical space.

The concentration‑compactness argument produces a minimal‑mass blow‑up solution that is almost periodic modulo symmetries. The interaction Morawetz estimate ∫∫ |x-y|-γ-1 |u(t,x)|² |u(t,y)|² dx dy dt ≤ C ‖u‖L^∞_t Ḣ¹⁴ rules out the existence of such a solution by showing that the potential energy must vanish, forcing u ≡ 0. The proof of the Morawetz estimate for the nonlocal kernel uses the identity ∂_t ∫ x·Im(bar u ∇u) dx = 2∫ |∇u|² dx - (γ/2) ∫∫ |x-y|-γ-2 (x-y)·(∇_x - ∇_y) |u|² |u|² dx dy, together with the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality to control the nonlocal term. The radial assumption is used to obtain the improved decay ‖u‖L^∞_x ≤ C |x|-(d-1)/2 ‖u‖Ḣ¹, which is essential for the virial‑type computation.

Once global well‑posedness is established, scattering follows from the linear profile decomposition and the perturbative lemma. The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ e-itH eitH₀ exist and are asymptotically complete on the energy space Ḣ¹. The S‑matrix is defined by S = Ω_+* Ω_- and is unitary on the absolutely continuous subspace. For non‑radial data the problem remains open; the main obstacle is the lack of a suitable Morawetz estimate without symmetry. Recent work by Dodson and Murphy (2020) extended the result to the non‑radial case in dimension d = 5 for the local quintic NLS, suggesting that a similar extension may be possible for the nonlocal Hartree equation using the bilinear Strichartz estimates discussed earlier.

  • Confusing the bilinear Strichartz gain 2-(k-j)/2 with the linear Strichartz constant; the bilinear gain is essential for summing over dyadic blocks.
  • Assuming the fractal Weyl exponent δ equals the spatial dimension d; δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set and can be strictly smaller.
  • Treating the nonlocal Riemann–Hilbert problem as a standard local one; the reflection symmetry forces a coupled jump matrix and a symmetric discrete spectrum.
  • Neglecting the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev condition γ < d when applying the interaction Morawetz estimate; the estimate fails for γ ≥ d.
  • Overlooking that the nonlocal kernel’s Fourier multiplier must be bounded on dyadic annuli for the bilinear estimate to hold uniformly.
  • Assuming the wave operators exist on the whole energy space without proving asymptotic completeness; the linear profile decomposition is required.
  • Misapplying the Birman–Krein formula det S = e-2π i ξ to non‑unitary S‑matrices without specifying the branch of the complex logarithm.
The advanced sections above provide the mechanisms, estimates, and spectral tools needed to tackle research‑level scattering problems in competitive examinations.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Spectral Zeta Function

The fractional Laplacian (−Δ)^s with 0 < s < 1 generates a nonlocal dispersion relation that modifies the free Hamiltonian H₀ = (−Δ)^s. The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e−itH₀ exist under the Kato‑smoothness condition ∫ℝ ‖⟨x⟩−σ e−itH₀ f‖² dt ≤ C‖f‖² for σ > 1/2, a result due to Yajima (1995) extended to fractional powers by Frank‑Lenzmann‑Silvestre (2016). The spectral shift function ξ(λ) for the pair (H, H₀) admits a representation via the spectral zeta function ζ_H(s) = Tr(H−s) − Tr(H₀−s), which converges for Re s > d/(2s). The Birman–Krein formula det S(λ) = e−2π i ξ(λ) remains valid, and the zeta‑regularized determinant of the scattering matrix yields the scattering phase ∫ ξ(λ) dλ = (1/2π i) log det S. In the semiclassical limit h → 0 the leading term of ξ(λ) is given by the Weyl law for the fractional operator: ξ(λ) ∼ (2π)−d Vol{(x,ξ): |ξ|2s + V(x) ≤ λ} − Vol{(x,ξ): |ξ|2s ≤ λ}. This asymptotic connects the spectral shift to the classical phase‑space volume and underlies trace formulas for resonances in non‑self‑adjoint perturbations.

Key Estimates

  • Limiting absorption principle: supε>0 ‖⟨x⟩−σ (H − λ ∓ iε)−1 ⟨x⟩−σ‖ ≤ C_λ for λ > 0, σ > 1/2.
  • Spectral zeta asymptotics: ζ_H(s) = (2π)−d ∫ℝ^d ∫ℝ^d (|ξ|2s + V(x))−s dξ dx + O(1) as s → d/(2s)+.
  • Resonance counting: N(r) = #{ζ_j: |ζ_j| ≤ r} ≤ C rd/s for non‑self‑adjoint fractional Schrödinger operators (Zworski 2017).

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Manifolds with Asymptotically Conical Ends and the Resolvent Estimates

Let (M,g) be a complete non‑compact Riemannian manifold whose ends are diffeomorphic to (0,∞)_r × Y with metric dr² + r² h(y) + o(r²) as r → ∞, where (Y,h) is a compact manifold without boundary. The Laplace–Beltrami operator Δ_g on such a space has absolutely continuous spectrum [0,∞) and no embedded eigenvalues under a non‑trapping condition on the geodesic flow. Vasy (2013) proved a high‑energy resolvent estimate ‖⟨r⟩−1/2−σ (Δ_g − λ ∓ i0)−1 ⟨r⟩−1/2−σ‖L²→L² ≤ C λ−1/2 for λ ≥ 1 and σ > 0, which implies the existence and completeness of the wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ e^{it√{Δ_g}} e^{−it√{Δ₀}} where Δ₀ is the model cone Laplacian. The scattering matrix S(λ) acts on L²(Y) and is a Fourier integral operator associated with the broken geodesic flow on the cone. Its principal symbol is the scattering map of the classical billiard on Y. The Poisson operator P(λ) maps boundary data on Y to generalized eigenfunctions, and the Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map Λ(λ) = ∂_r P(λ) satisfies the Riccati equation ∂_r Λ + Λ² + r−2 Δ_Y + V = λ. The meromorphic continuation of Λ(λ) to the complex plane yields the resonance set, and the fractal Weyl law N(r) ≤ C rδ holds with δ the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set (Dyatlov‑Zworski 2016).

Resolvent Structure

  • Low‑energy expansion: (Δ_g − λ)−1 = G₀ + λ G₁ + O(λ²) in weighted spaces, where G₀ is the Green operator of the model cone.
  • Propagation of singularities: the wave front set of the resolvent kernel is contained in the union of the diagonal and the forward/backward flowout of the conormal bundle of the boundary.
  • Scattering matrix asymptotics: S(λ) = I + i λ(d−1)/2 A(λ) + o(λ(d−1)/2) with A(λ) a pseudodifferential operator of order 0 on Y.
Worked Example: Resolvent Estimate on a Conical End
Consider the model cone ℝ_+ × S¹ with metric dr² + r² dθ². Compute the limiting absorption principle for the Laplacian Δ = ∂_r² + r−1 ∂_r + r−2 ∂_θ² at energy λ = k² > 0. Show that for σ > 1/2 the weighted resolvent ⟨r⟩−σ (Δ − k² ∓ i0)−1 ⟨r⟩−σ is bounded on L² uniformly in k ≥ 1.
Separate variables u(r,θ) = Σm∈ℤ u_m(r) eimθ. Each radial mode satisfies (−∂_r² − r−1 ∂_r + m² r−2 − k²) u_m = f_m. The Jost solutions are Hankel functions H|m|(1)(kr) (outgoing) and H|m|(2)(kr) (incoming). The Green kernel is G_m(r,r') = (i/4) H|m|(1)(k r_>) H|m|(2)(k r_<). Using the uniform asymptotics H_ν(1)(z) ∼ √{2/(π z)} ei(z−νπ/2−π/4) for z → ∞, one obtains |G_m(r,r')| ≤ C k−1 (r r')−1/2 for r,r' ≥ 1. Summing over m and applying Schur's test with weight ⟨r⟩−σ yields the bound ‖⟨r⟩−σ (Δ − k² ∓ i0)−1 ⟨r⟩−σ‖ ≤ C k−1 ≤ C for k ≥ 1. The estimate fails for σ ≤ 1/2 because the weight does not compensate the r−1/2 decay of the kernel.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a resolvent estimate on an asymptotically conical manifold, first identify the model cone and separate variables. Write the radial ODE, recognise the Bessel/Hankel functions, and use their large‑argument asymptotics. The critical weight σ = 1/2 appears from the r−1/2 decay of the free resolvent kernel; any σ > 1/2 gives a uniform bound. Memorise the statement of Vasy's high‑energy estimate and the role of the non‑trapping hypothesis.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Critical Exponent and the Kenig–Merle Roadmap

The energy‑critical nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂_t u + Δ u = ± |u|4/(d−2) u in dimension d ≥ 3 has scaling symmetry u_λ(t,x) = λ(d−2)/2 u(λ² t, λ x) that preserves the Ḣ¹ norm. Kenig and Merle (2006) introduced a concentration‑compactness/rigidity scheme to prove global well‑posedness and scattering for radial data in the defocusing case and for data below the ground state threshold in the focusing case. The roadmap consists of three pillars: (1) a linear profile decomposition (Bahouri‑Gérard 1999) that writes any bounded sequence in Ḣ¹ as a sum of decoupled profiles plus a remainder small in the Strichartz norm; (2) a perturbation lemma showing that an approximate solution close to a true solution in the critical Strichartz space can be corrected to an exact solution; (3) a rigidity theorem based on a localized virial/Morawetz identity that rules out the existence of a minimal‑mass blow‑up solution which is almost periodic modulo symmetries. The almost periodic solution satisfies a compactness property: its orbit {u(t): t ∈ ℝ} is precompact in Ḣ¹. The virial identity ∂_t² ∫ |x|² |u|² dx = 8 ∫ |∇u|² dx − (4d/(d+2)) ∫ |u|2d/(d−2) dx together with the Gagliardo‑Nirenberg inequality forces the kinetic energy to vanish, implying u ≡ 0. For non‑radial data the main obstacle is the lack of a suitable Morawetz estimate; recent progress by Killip‑Visan (2010) and Dodson (2019) uses bilinear Strichartz estimates and a frequency‑localized interaction Morawetz to extend scattering to the non‑radial defocusing case in d = 4,5.

Critical Strichartz Spaces

  • Admissible pair (q,r): 2/q + d/r = d/2, 2 ≤ q,r ≤ ∞, (q,r,d) ≠ (2,∞,2).
  • Critical space: L_t^q L_x^r with scaling exponent s_c = 1. The endpoint (q,r) = (2, 2d/(d−2)) is excluded in d ≥ 4 but admissible in d = 3 via the Keel‑Tao endpoint.
  • Bilinear estimate: ‖eitΔ PN1 f · eitΔ PN2 g‖L²t,x ≤ C (N1/N2)1/2 ‖f‖₂ ‖g‖₂ for N1 ≪ N2.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time‑Dependent Potentials and the Magnus Expansion

For a Hamiltonian H(t) = H₀ + V(t) with V(t) a bounded operator on L²(ℝ^d) strongly continuous in t, the propagator U(t,s) satisfies i∂_t U(t,s) = H(t) U(t,s), U(s,s) = I. The Magnus expansion expresses U(t,0) = exp(Ω(t)) where Ω(t) = Σn≥1 Ω_n(t) is a series of nested commutators: Ω₁(t) = −i ∫0^t V(τ) dτ, Ω₂(t) = (−i)²/2 ∫0^t ∫0τ₁ [V(τ₁), V(τ₂)] dτ₂ dτ₁, etc. Convergence holds for ∫0^T ‖V(τ)‖ dτ < π (Moan‑Niesen 2008). The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ U(t,0) e−itH₀ exist if V(t) decays in time, e.g. ‖V(t)‖ ≤ C ⟨t⟩−1−ε. In the adiabatic regime V(t) = V(ε t) with ε → 0, the Magnus series reduces to the adiabatic expansion Ω(t) = −i ∫0^t V(ε τ) dτ + O(ε). The scattering matrix S = Ω_+* Ω_- is unitary on the absolutely continuous subspace of H₀. For periodic V(t+T)=V(t) the Floquet theory replaces the Magnus expansion: the monodromy operator U(T,0) has a logarithm iH_F (Floquet Hamiltonian) and the quasi‑energy spectrum determines the scattering channels. The Kato‑Yajima theory for long‑range time‑dependent potentials requires a Dollard‑type phase correction Φ(t) = ∫0^t Vlr(τ) dτ where Vlr is the long‑range part; the modified wave operators Ω_±mod = s‑limt→±∞ U(t,0) e−iΦ(t) e−itH₀ exist under the condition ∫^∞ ‖∂_t Vsr(t)‖ dt < ∞ for the short‑range component.

Magnus Convergence Criterion

  • If ∫0^T ‖V(τ)‖ dτ < π then the series converges absolutely in operator norm.
  • For V(t) = λ W(t) with small λ, the radius of convergence is proportional to 1/‖W‖L¹.
  • The truncated expansion Ω[N](t) yields an approximation U_N(t,0) = exp(Ω[N](t)) with error O(∫0^t ‖V‖N+1).

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Non‑Self‑Adjoint Potentials and the Pseudospectrum

When the potential V is complex‑valued, the Hamiltonian H = −Δ + V is non‑self‑adjoint. The spectrum σ(H) may contain eigenvalues with positive imaginary part (exponential growth) and the resolvent (H − z)−1 can be large far from the spectrum. The ε‑pseudospectrum σ_ε(H) = {z ∈ ℂ: ‖(H − z)−1‖ > ε−1} captures this transient amplification. For Schrödinger operators with compactly supported V, the pseudospectrum satisfies σ_ε(H) ⊂ {z: dist(z, σ(H)) ≤ C ε1/2} in the semiclassical limit h → 0 (Davies 2007). The scattering matrix S(z) defined via the Jost solutions is meromorphic in ℂ with poles at resonances (complex eigenvalues of the dilated operator). The Birman–Krein formula generalises to det S(z) = exp(−2π i ξ(z)) where ξ(z) is the spectral shift function defined via the regularised determinant of (H − z)(H₀ − z)−1. The wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e−itH₀ exist on the absolutely continuous subspace of H₀ provided the potential is short‑range and satisfies a limiting absorption principle in weighted spaces. The S‑matrix is no longer unitary but satisfies S(z)* S(z) = I − 2π i Γ(z) where Γ(z) is the positive‑definite defect operator related to the imaginary part of V. The time‑delay operator Q = −i S* ∂_E S remains self‑adjoint and its trace gives the Eisenbud–Wigner–Smith formula Tr Q = 2π ξ(E) even for non‑self‑adjoint scattering, provided the branch of the logarithm is chosen consistently.

Pseudospectral Bounds

  • For V ∈ L^∞_c(ℝ^d), ‖(H − z)−1‖ ≤ C |Im z|−1 for Im z ≠ 0.
  • Semiclassical estimate: σ_ε(H_h) ⊂ {z: dist(z, σ(H_h)) ≤ C h−1 ε} for h → 0.
  • Resonance free strip: if V is analytic and satisfies a non‑trapping condition, there exists γ > 0 such that no resonances lie in {Im z > −γ} for |Re z| large.
  • Confusing the fractional Laplacian symbol |ξ|2s with the standard Laplacian symbol |ξ|² when writing the Weyl law for the spectral shift function.
  • Assuming the limiting absorption principle holds with weight σ = 1/2 on asymptotically conical manifolds; the critical weight is strictly greater than 1/2.
  • Neglecting the endpoint Strichartz pair (2, 2d/(d−2)) in the Kenig–Merle roadmap; it is admissible only in dimension three.
  • Applying the Magnus expansion without checking the convergence condition ∫ ‖V‖ < π; the series may diverge for strong time‑dependent potentials.
  • Treating the non‑self‑adjoint S‑matrix as unitary and using det S = e−2π i ξ without specifying the branch of the complex logarithm.
  • Overlooking that the pseudospectrum can be much larger than the spectrum for non‑normal operators, leading to incorrect stability conclusions.
  • Misidentifying the trapped set dimension δ in the fractal Weyl law; δ is the Minkowski dimension of the trapped set, not the spatial dimension d.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Subcritical Exponent and the Global Well‑Posedness

For the nonlinear Schrödinger equation (NLS) i∂ₜu + Δu = λ|u|p‑1u on ℝᵈ with 1 < p < 1 + 4/d (the L²‑subcritical range), the Cauchy problem is globally well‑posed in the energy space H¹(ℝᵈ) for any initial data. The proof combines the Strichartz estimates for the linear propagator eitΔ with a contraction mapping argument in the space C(ℝ; H¹) ∩ Lq_t Lr_x where (q,r) is an admissible Strichartz pair. The key mechanism is that the nonlinearity is locally Lipschitz from H¹ to H¹ and the conserved mass M(u) = ∫|u|² dx and energy E(u) = ½∫|∇u|² dx + (λ/(p+1))∫|u|p+1 dx provide a priori bounds that prevent finite‑time blow‑up. In the defocusing case λ > 0 the energy is coercive, yielding global existence for all data. In the focusing case λ < 0 the Gagliardo‑Nirenberg inequality ‖u‖p+1p+1 ≤ C‖∇u‖₂d(p‑1)/2‖u‖₂p+1‑d(p‑1)/2 shows that if the mass is below the ground‑state threshold, the energy controls the H¹ norm and global well‑posedness follows. The scattering operator Ω₊ = s‑limt→∞ e-itΔ u(t) exists and is a homeomorphism on H¹, with the asymptotic profile u₊ ∈ H¹ satisfying u(t) = eitΔu₊ + oH¹(1). The proof of asymptotic completeness uses the interaction Morawetz estimate ∫∫ |u|p+1 / |x| dx dt ≤ C‖u₀‖H¹², which rules out non‑scattering solutions. Examiners often test the distinction between mass‑critical (p = 1+4/d) and subcritical regimes: in the subcritical case the scaling symmetry leaves the H¹ norm invariant only at the critical exponent, so the subcritical nonlinearity is perturbative with respect to the linear flow.

  • Strichartz admissible pairs satisfy 2/q + d/r = d/2, 2 ≤ q,r ≤ ∞, (q,r,d) ≠ (2,∞,2).
  • Ground state Q solves −ΔQ + Q = |Q|p‑1Q; its mass M(Q) is the threshold for global existence in the focusing case.
  • The interaction Morawetz estimate was introduced by Colliander‑Keel‑Staffilani‑Takaoka‑Tao (2004) for the cubic NLS in d=3.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Supercritical Exponent and the Blow‑Up Analysis

When p ≥ 1 + 4/(d‑2) (the H¹‑supercritical range for d ≥ 3) the NLS i∂ₜu + Δu = λ|u|p‑1u exhibits finite‑time blow‑up for certain focusing initial data. The virial identity V(t) = ∫|x|²|u|² dx gives V''(t) = 8E(u) − 4(d(p‑1)‑2)/(p+1) ∫|u|p+1 dx. If the energy is negative and the variance is finite, V''(t) becomes negative, forcing V(t) to reach zero in finite time, which implies ‖∇u(t)‖₂ → ∞. The sharp threshold for blow‑up versus global existence is given by the ground state Q solving −ΔQ + Q = |Q|p‑1Q. The Kenig‑Merle concentration‑compactness/rigidity method shows that for radial data in the energy‑supercritical case, solutions with energy below E(Q) and ‖∇u₀‖₂ < ‖∇Q‖₂ are global and scatter, while those with ‖∇u₀‖₂ > ‖∇Q‖₂ and negative energy blow up. The profile decomposition of bounded sequences in Ḣ¹ (Bahouri‑Gérard) isolates a minimal‑energy blow‑up solution, which is then shown to be almost periodic modulo symmetries. A rigidity theorem (using the localized virial estimate) rules out such almost‑periodic solutions, completing the classification. In the non‑radial setting the threshold problem remains open for many supercritical exponents. Examiners frequently ask to derive the virial identity, to state the condition d(p‑1) > 2 for the sign of the nonlinear term in V''(t), and to explain why the concentration‑compactness argument fails without radial symmetry (lack of compactness in the embedding Ḣ¹ Lp+1).

  • Virial identity: V''(t) = 16E(u) − 4(d(p‑1)‑2)/(p+1) ∫|u|p+1 dx.
  • Ground state Q minimizes the action S(u) = E(u) + ½M(u) under the constraint ‖∇u‖₂ = ‖∇Q‖₂.
  • Kenig‑Merle roadmap: (1) profile decomposition, (2) existence of minimal blow‑up solution, (3) rigidity via localized virial/Morawetz.
Worked Example: Virial Blow‑Up Criterion
Consider the focusing NLS i∂ₜu + Δu = -|u|p‑1u in ℝ³ with p = 3 (energy‑critical). Let u₀ ∈ H¹ have finite variance and energy E(u₀) < 0. Show that the solution blows up in finite time.
Compute V(t) = ∫|x|²|u|² dx. Differentiate twice using the equation: V'(t) = 4 Im ∫ x·∇u u dx. V''(t) = 8 ∫|∇u|² dx - 2(p‑1)/(p+1) ∫|u|p+1 dx. For p=3, (p‑1)/(p+1) = 1/2, so V''(t) = 8∫|∇u|² dx - ∫|u|⁴ dx. The energy is E = ½∫|∇u|² dx - ¼∫|u|⁴ dx. Hence V''(t) = 16E(u). Since E(u₀) < 0 and energy is conserved, V''(t) = 16E(u₀) < 0 constant. Thus V(t) is a concave quadratic with negative second derivative, so V(t) reaches zero at some finite T > 0. At that time ‖∇u(t)‖₂ must diverge, proving finite‑time blow‑up.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the blow‑up criterion, write the virial identity first, then substitute the energy expression. Remember the factor 16E appears only for the energy‑critical exponent p = 1+4/(d‑2); for other supercritical p the coefficient of the nonlinear term differs. Memorize the scaling relation p_c = 1+4/(d‑2) and the sign condition d(p‑1) > 2. If the problem gives radial data, mention the Kenig‑Merle classification; if non‑radial, note the open threshold.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Nonlinearities and the I‑Method

The I‑method (almost conservation law) extends global well‑posedness and scattering results to low‑regularity spaces for NLS with nonlocal nonlinearities such as the Hartree equation i∂ₜu + Δu = (|x|-γ * |u|²)u, 0 < γ < min(2,d). The idea is to modify the conserved energy by a smoothing operator I_N that acts as a Fourier multiplier with symbol m_N(ξ) = 1 for |ξ| ≤ N and m_N(ξ) = (N/|ξ|)1‑s for |ξ| > N, where s < 1 is the target regularity. The modified energy E(I_N u) = ½∫|∇I_N u|² dx + ¼∫ (|x|-γ * |I_N u|²) |I_N u|² dx is almost conserved: its time derivative is bounded by N-α for some α > 0, provided the solution satisfies suitable multilinear estimates. By iterating the local theory on intervals of length ∼ N-α and summing the increments, one obtains global control of the modified energy up to any time T, yielding global well‑posedness in H^s for s > s_c where s_c = (d‑2)/2 - γ/2 is the scaling critical index. For the cubic NLS (γ = 0) this recovers the classical I‑method of Colliander‑Keel‑Staffilani‑Takaoka‑Tao. The scattering operator is then constructed by proving that the modified wave operators Ω₊I = s‑limt→∞ e-itΔ I_N u(t) exist in H^s and that I_N is invertible on the range of the solution. Key technical tools include the fractional chain rule for I_N, the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality for the convolution kernel, and the bilinear Strichartz estimate ‖eitΔPN₁f · eitΔPN₂g‖L²t,x ≤ C (N₁/N₂)1/2 ‖f‖₂ ‖g‖₂ for N₁ ≪ N₂. Examiners often test the definition of the multiplier m_N, the computation of the almost conservation law error term, and the role of the bilinear estimate in closing the bootstrap.

  • Multiplier m_N(ξ) = 1 (|ξ| ≤ N), (N/|ξ|)1‑s (|ξ| > N).
  • Modified energy increment: |E(I_N u(t)) - E(I_N u(0))| ≤ C N-α ‖u‖X4 where X is the Strichartz space.
  • Scaling critical regularity s_c = (d‑2)/2 - γ/2; global well‑posedness holds for s > s_c.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Derivative Type and the Normal Form Method

Derivative nonlinear Schrödinger equations (DNLS) such as i∂ₜu + ∂ₓ²u = i∂ₓ(|u|²u) on ℝ appear in plasma physics and optical fibers. The derivative in the nonlinearity destroys the standard Strichartz estimates because the nonlinearity is not bounded from H^s to H^s for s < ½. The normal form method (Shatah, 1985) removes the worst derivative interaction by a nonlinear change of variables v = u + 𝒩(u), where 𝒩 is a multilinear operator chosen so that the equation for v contains only cubic and higher terms without derivatives on the highest frequency factor. Concretely, for the DNLS the resonant set where the phase Φ(ξ₁,ξ₂,ξ₃) = ξ₁² - ξ₂² + ξ₃² - (ξ₁-ξ₂+ξ₃)² vanishes is exactly the set where the derivative nonlinearity is non‑integrable. By integrating by parts in time (the normal form transformation) one gains a factor of Φ in the denominator, which is bounded away from zero off the resonant set. The remaining resonant part is a total derivative and can be absorbed into a gauge transformation (Kaup‑Newell). After the transformation, the equation for v satisfies a cubic NLS with a non‑derivative nonlinearity, to which standard Strichartz theory applies. Global well‑posedness in H^s for s ≥ ½ and scattering in H^s for s > ½ follow. The method also works for the generalized DNLS i∂ₜu + ∂ₓ²u = i∂ₓ(|u|2ku) and for the Benjamin‑Ono equation. Examiners frequently ask to write the phase function, to identify the resonant set, and to explain why the gauge transformation is needed to handle the resonant contribution. A common trap is to forget that the normal form transformation is only valid for small data in the non‑periodic setting; for large data one must use the inverse scattering transform (IST) which exploits complete integrability.

  • Phase function Φ(ξ₁,ξ₂,ξ₃) = ξ₁² - ξ₂² + ξ₃² - (ξ₁-ξ₂+ξ₃)² = 2(ξ₁-ξ₂)(ξ₁+ξ₃).
  • Resonant set: ξ₁ = ξ₂ or ξ₁ = -ξ₃.
  • Normal form transformation: v = u + i ∫₀ᵗ ei(t-τ)∂ₓ² ∂ₓ(|u|²u) dτ (formal).

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Critical Exponent and the Kenig‑Merle Roadmap

At the mass‑critical exponent p = 1 + 4/d (L²‑critical) and the energy‑critical exponent p = 1 + 4/(d‑2) (H¹‑critical for d ≥ 3), the NLS i∂ₜu + Δu = λ|u|p‑1u is invariant under the scaling u_λ(t,x) = λ2/(p‑1) u(λ²t, λx) which preserves the critical norm (‖u‖L² for mass‑critical, ‖∇u‖L² for energy‑critical). The Kenig‑Merle roadmap (2006) provides a unified strategy to prove global well‑posedness and scattering for all data with critical norm below the ground‑state threshold. The three pillars are: (1) a linear profile decomposition for bounded sequences in the critical space (L² or Ḣ¹), (2) a stability theory (long‑time perturbation lemma) showing that an approximate solution with small error in the Strichartz norm stays close to a true solution, and (3) a rigidity theorem excluding the existence of a minimal‑mass (or minimal‑energy) non‑scattering solution that is almost periodic modulo symmetries. The almost‑periodic solution is shown to have a precompact orbit in the critical space, which forces it to be either a soliton or a high‑frequency cascade. The localized virial/Morawetz estimate rules out both possibilities, establishing scattering. For the focusing mass‑critical NLS in d=1,2 the ground state Q is the unique optimizer of the Gagliardo‑Nirenberg inequality, and the threshold is M(u) < M(Q). For the energy‑critical NLS in d=3,4,5 the threshold is E(u) < E(Q) and ‖∇u‖₂ < ‖∇Q‖₂. The roadmap has been adapted to many other dispersive equations (wave, Klein‑Gordon, Hartree). Examiners test the statement of the profile decomposition, the definition of the critical Strichartz norm (e.g., L2(d+2)/dt,x for mass‑critical), and the role of the localized virial estimate in the rigidity step. A frequent mistake is to confuse the mass‑critical and energy‑critical Strichartz spaces; the former uses the scaling‑invariant Lebesgue space L2(d+2)/dt,x, the latter uses L2(d+2)/(d‑2)t,x.

  • Mass‑critical Strichartz space: L2(d+2)/dt,x(ℝ×ℝᵈ).
  • Energy‑critical Strichartz space: L2(d+2)/(d‑2)t,x(ℝ×ℝᵈ).
  • Profile decomposition: any bounded sequence in the critical space splits into a sum of rescaled profiles plus a remainder small in the Strichartz norm.
  • Confusing the scaling‑critical exponent with the L²‑subcritical range when stating the global well‑posedness theorem.
  • Omitting the gauge transformation in the normal form method for DNLS, leading to an uncontrolled resonant term.
  • Applying the I‑method without verifying the multilinear estimate that controls the modified energy increment.
  • Misidentifying the resonant set for derivative nonlinearities; the phase function must be computed correctly.
  • Using the mass‑critical Strichartz space for an energy‑critical problem or vice versa.
  • Assuming the Kenig‑Merle rigidity argument works without the localized virial estimate; the latter is essential to rule out the almost‑periodic solution.
  • Neglecting the condition d(p‑1) > 2 in the virial blow‑up criterion, which determines the sign of the nonlinear contribution.

These advanced topics illustrate how modern scattering theory combines harmonic analysis, concentration compactness, and integrable systems techniques to treat a wide spectrum of nonlinear dispersive equations.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Hartree‑Fock Type and the Exchange Term

The Hartree‑Fock equation arises in the mean‑field description of many‑fermion systems. It takes the form i∂ₜψ = H₀ψ + (V * |ψ|²)ψ − (V * (ψ̄ψ))ψ where the first nonlinearity is the direct (Hartree) term and the second is the exchange (Fock) term. The exchange term is nonlocal and acts as an integral operator with kernel V(x−y)ψ(y)ψ̄(x). This structure destroys the gauge invariance ψ ↦ eⁱᶿψ that the pure Hartree equation enjoys, and it introduces a sign‑indefinite contribution to the energy functional. For repulsive potentials V ≥ 0 the direct term is defocusing while the exchange term is focusing, creating a competition that can lead to collapse even when the Hartree term alone would guarantee global existence. The scattering theory for this system requires controlling the exchange term in Strichartz spaces. A key estimate is ‖(V * (ψ̄φ))χ‖L²ₜLˣ ≤ C‖V‖Lʳ‖ψ‖LᵖₜLˣ‖φ‖LᵠₜLˣ‖χ‖LˢₜLˣ with appropriate exponents determined by the Hardy‑Littlewood‑Sobolev inequality. When V(x) = |x|⁻ᵞ with 0 < γ < d, the exchange term is bounded from Hˢ × Hˢ → Hˢ⁻ᵞ for s > γ/2. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limₜ→±∞ eⁱᵗᴴ e⁻ⁱᵗᴴ⁰ exist and are asymptotically complete for small data in Hˢ with s > s_c = (d−2)/2 − γ/2. The proof follows the standard contraction mapping argument in the interaction picture, but the exchange term forces one to work with the matrix‑valued density ρ(t,x,y) = ψ(t,x)ψ̄(t,y) and to estimate its evolution in mixed‑norm spaces. The asymptotic state is described by a modified scattering operator that includes a phase correction from the long‑range part of the direct term, while the exchange term is short‑range in the sense of Dollard when γ > 1. For the Coulomb case γ = 1 in d = 3 the exchange term is borderline long‑range and requires a logarithmic phase correction analogous to the Dollard modification. The S‑matrix is not diagonal in the momentum representation because the exchange term couples different momentum modes; its kernel S(p,p′) = δ(p−p′) + iT(p,p′) satisfies the optical theorem Im T(p,p) = π∫|T(p,q)|²dq. In the semiclassical limit ℏ → 0 the Hartree‑Fock dynamics converges to the Vlasov equation for the Wigner transform of the density matrix, and the scattering cross‑section can be computed from the classical transport equation with a self‑consistent field.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation on Waveguide Manifolds and the Mode Decomposition

Waveguide manifolds are Riemannian manifolds of the form M = ℝ × Σ where Σ is a compact manifold with or without boundary. The Laplacian on M separates as Δ_M = ∂ₓ² + Δ_Σ. The spectrum of −Δ_Σ consists of eigenvalues 0 = λ₀ < λ₁ ≤ λ₂ ≤ … with eigenfunctions φ_j forming an orthonormal basis of L²(Σ). Any solution of the linear Schrödinger equation i∂ₜu + Δ_M u = 0 can be expanded as u(t,x,y) = Σ_j u_j(t,x)φ_j(y) where u_j satisfies i∂ₜu_j + ∂ₓ²u_j − λ_j u_j = 0. This reduces the problem to a family of one‑dimensional Schrödinger equations with shifted energies. For the nonlinear equation i∂ₜu + Δ_M u = |u|p−1u the mode decomposition yields a coupled system i∂ₜu_j + ∂ₓ²u_j − λ_j u_j = Σj₁,j₂,j₃ Cj j₁ j₂ j₃ uj₁ūj₂uj₃ where the coupling coefficients are integrals of products of four eigenfunctions on Σ. The scattering theory depends critically on whether the nonlinearity is energy‑subcritical or critical relative to the transverse direction. When p < 1 + 4/(d−1) (where d = dim M) the problem is subcritical and small data scattering in H¹(M) follows from Strichartz estimates on the waveguide, which take the form ‖eⁱᵗΔ_M u₀‖L^qₜL^rₓL²_y ≤ C‖u₀‖H^s with admissible pairs (q,r) satisfying 2/q + (d−1)/r = (d−1)/2 − s. The threshold λ₁ > 0 creates a spectral gap: modes with λ_j > 0 are dispersive in the x‑direction with effective mass √λ_j, while the zero mode λ₀ = 0 behaves like a free one‑dimensional Schrödinger equation. This leads to a two‑speed dispersion phenomenon where high transverse modes disperse faster. For large data, the zero mode can concentrate and form solitons while higher modes radiate away. The wave operators Ω± exist on the subspace of L²(M) orthogonal to bound states of the linearized operator around a solitary wave. The S‑matrix decomposes as a block matrix Sjk(k,k′) describing scattering from incoming mode j with longitudinal momentum k to outgoing mode k with momentum k′. Energy conservation forces k′² + λ_k = k² + λ_j, so only channels with λ_k ≤ k² + λ_j are open. The scattering amplitude for the zero mode exhibits a resonance at k = 0 when the nonlinearity supports a bound state at zero energy. The mode decomposition also enables a rigorous derivation of effective one‑dimensional equations (such as the nonlinear Schrödinger equation with a nonlocal nonlinearity) by projecting onto the zero mode and treating higher modes as a perturbation. This reduction is justified on long time scales using normal form transformations that eliminate non‑resonant couplings between modes.

Worked Example: Mode Coupling Threshold
Consider the cubic NLS i∂ₜu + ∂ₓ²u − Δ_Σ u = |u|²u on M = ℝ × Σ with dim Σ = 2. The eigenvalues of −Δ_Σ are λ_j = j² for j ≥ 0. Determine the condition on the initial data for the zero mode u₀ to decouple from the first excited mode u₁ at leading order in a normal form expansion.
The coupling coefficient for the interaction u₀ ū₀ u₁ is C = ∫_Σ φ₀² φ₁² dy. Since φ₀ is constant (λ₀ = 0) and φ₁ has zero mean, C = 0 by orthogonality. The leading coupling between u₀ and u₁ comes from terms like u₀ ū₁ u₁ with coefficient ∫ φ₀ φ₁² φ₁ dy = ∫ φ₁³ dy. This vanishes if φ₁ is odd with respect to a symmetry of Σ. If Σ = S¹ with standard metric, φ₁ = sin(y) or cos(y), and ∫ sin³(y) dy = 0. Thus the zero mode decouples from the first excited mode at cubic order when the transverse eigenfunctions have definite parity. The first non‑vanishing coupling appears at quintic order or through the term u₀ ū₂ u₂ with λ₂ = 4. The resonance condition for energy transfer from u₀ to u₁ is k₀² = k₁² + 1. For small longitudinal momenta |k| ≪ 1 this cannot be satisfied, so the zero mode is dynamically isolated on long time scales. The normal form transformation removes the non‑resonant cubic terms, yielding an effective equation for u₀ with a nonlocal nonlinearity of order |u₀|⁴u₀.
Exam Strategy: Waveguide Scattering
When a problem involves a product manifold ℝ × Σ, immediately separate variables using the eigenbasis of Δ_Σ. Identify the zero mode (λ = 0) and the spectral gap λ₁. The zero mode governs the long‑time dynamics because it has the slowest dispersion. For nonlinear problems, check whether the nonlinearity couples the zero mode to itself at the lowest order — if the coupling coefficient vanishes by symmetry, the effective nonlinearity is of higher order, which improves scattering prospects. Always write the resonance condition k_out² + λ_out = k_in² + λ_in to determine open channels.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Combined Power and Logarithmic Type

Logarithmic nonlinearities appear in models of quantum mechanics with logarithmic Schrödinger equations (Bialynicki‑Birula and Mycielski, 1976) and in effective descriptions of Bose‑Einstein condensates with non‑polynomial interactions. The equation i∂ₜu + Δu = λ|u|p−1u + μ u log|u|² combines a power nonlinearity with a logarithmic term. The logarithmic term is scale‑invariant in any dimension: under u_κ(t,x) = κd/2u(κ²t,κx) the term u log|u|² transforms as κd/2u log|κ^d u|² = κd/2(u log|u|² + d u log κ), so the equation is not scale‑invariant unless μ = 0. However, the logarithmic nonlinearity has the remarkable property that it preserves the Gaussian shape of solutions: if u₀(x) = A exp(−α|x|² + iβ·x) then the solution remains Gaussian for all time with time‑dependent parameters. This allows explicit computation of the scattering operator for the pure logarithmic equation. For the combined equation, the scattering theory depends on the relative strength of the two terms. When p < 1 + 4/d (mass‑subcritical power) the power term is short‑range and the logarithmic term dominates the long‑time asymptotics. The wave operators exist in H¹(ℝᵈ) for small data, and the asymptotic state is described by a modified free evolution with a logarithmic phase correction: u(t) ∼ eⁱᵗΔ (e⁻ⁱμ t log t φ₊) as t → ∞. The proof uses the pseudo‑conformal transformation to map the logarithmic term to a time‑dependent potential, followed by a Dollard‑type modification. When p = 1 + 4/d (mass‑critical) the two terms compete at the same scaling level. The ground state equation −ΔQ + Q = λ|Q|p−1Q + μ Q log|Q|² has a unique positive radial solution for certain parameter ranges. The scattering threshold is determined by the mass of the ground state: if M(u₀) < M(Q) and E(u₀) < E(Q) then the solution scatters; if M(u₀) = M(Q) and E(u₀) = E(Q) the solution is the standing wave eⁱᵗQ. The concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Kenig‑Merle extends to this setting by adding the logarithmic term to the virial identity. The key modification is that the logarithmic term contributes ∫ |u|² log|u|² dx to the virial, which is controlled by the entropy ∫ |u|² log|u|² dx ≤ C‖u‖H¹² log‖u‖H¹. For p > 1 + 4/d (mass‑supercritical) the power term dominates and the logarithmic term is a lower‑order perturbation. The S‑matrix for the pure logarithmic equation can be computed explicitly using the Gaussian ansatz: the scattering amplitude is a Gaussian in momentum space with a width that depends on μ. For the combined equation, the S‑matrix is not explicitly known but its analytic properties (unitarity, crossing symmetry) follow from the general theory of nonlinear scattering for Hamiltonian PDEs with scale‑invariant nonlinearities.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Convolution Type and the Fourier Multiplier Approach

Nonlocal potentials of convolution type V(x) = (K * ρ)(x) where ρ(x) = |u(x)|² appear in the Hartree equation and in models of nonlocal nonlinear optics. The linear scattering problem with a potential V(x) = (K * f)(x) for a fixed density f can be analyzed using the Fourier multiplier representation. The Lippmann‑Schwinger equation for the scattering state ψ⁺(k,x) = eⁱᵏ·ˣ + ∫ G⁺(k;x,y)V(y)ψ⁺(k,y)dy becomes, after Fourier transform in x, ψ̂⁺(k,p) = δ(p−k) + (|p|²−k²−i0)⁻¹ (K̂(p−q) f̂(p−q) * ψ̂⁺(k,q)). This is a convolution equation in momentum space. The scattering amplitude is T(k,k′) = K̂(k−k′) f̂(k−k′) + ∫ K̂(k−q) f̂(k−q) (|q|²−k²−i0)⁻¹ T(q,k′) dq. When K̂ is a rational function (e.g., Yukawa potential K(x) = e⁻ᵐ|ˣ|/|x| with K̂(p) = 4π/(|p|²+m²)) the integral equation reduces to a system of algebraic equations for the moments of T. The Born series converges in operator norm when ‖K̂ f̂‖L¹ < 1. For the time‑dependent Hartree equation i∂ₜu + Δu = (K * |u|²)u the scattering theory is developed in weighted Sobolev spaces Hs,σ = {u : ‖⟨x⟩^σ ⟨∇⟩^s u‖L² < ∞}. The wave operators Ω± exist and are asymptotically complete for small data in H0,1 when K̂ ∈ L¹ ∩ L^∞ and K̂(p) ∼ |p|⁻ᵞ near p = 0 with γ < 2. The long‑range nature of the potential (γ ≤ 1) requires a Dollard modification with a phase Φ(t,k) = ∫₀ᵗ (K̂ * |û₊|²)(k,s) ds. The asymptotic state û₊ satisfies a nonlinear integral equation that can be solved by contraction in a suitable function space. The S‑matrix is given by S = Ω₊* Ω₋ and its kernel S(k,k′) = δ(k−k′) + iT(k,k′) where T satisfies the nonlinear Lippmann‑Schwinger equation. For the Yukawa potential in d = 3, the scattering amplitude at low energy behaves as T(k,k′) ∼ −4πa δ(k−k′) where a is the scattering length, which depends nonlinearly on the asymptotic density |û₊|². The inverse scattering problem for the Hartree equation asks to recover K from the scattering data. This is severely ill‑posed because the mapping K ↦ S is nonlinear and non‑injective: different potentials can produce the same S‑matrix if they differ by a function orthogonal to all products û₊(k) û₊(k′). Uniqueness holds if the asymptotic states form a complete set in L², which is true for generic initial data.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Time‑Dependent Potentials and the Magnus Expansion

Time‑dependent potentials V(t,x) arise in the interaction of quantum systems with external fields (laser pulses, oscillating traps). The evolution operator U(t,s) satisfies i∂ₜU(t,s) = (H₀ + V(t))U(t,s) with U(s,s) = I. The Magnus expansion expresses U(t,0) = exp(Ω(t)) where Ω(t) = Σn=1^∞ Ω_n(t) is a series of nested commutators: Ω₁(t) = −i∫₀ᵗ V(τ)dτ, Ω₂(t) = −½∫₀ᵗ dτ₁ ∫₀τ₁ dτ₂ [V(τ₁),V(τ₂)], etc. This expansion converges in operator norm when ∫₀ᵗ ‖V(τ)‖ dτ < π. For scattering theory, one considers the limit t → ∞. The wave operators Ω± = s‑limₜ→±∞ U(0,t) e⁻ⁱᵗᴴ⁰ exist if V(t) decays sufficiently fast in time. A standard condition is ∫₀^∞ ‖V(t)‖H¹→H⁻¹ dt < ∞ for short‑range potentials, or ∫₀^∞ ‖V(t)⟨x⟩1+ε‖H¹→H⁻¹ dt < ∞ for long‑range potentials. When V(t,x) = f(t)W(x) with f ∈ L¹(ℝ) the problem reduces to the stationary case with an effective potential ∫ f(t)dt W(x). For periodic potentials V(t+T,x) = V(t,x) the Floquet theory applies: the evolution over one period defines the monodromy operator U(T,0), whose eigenvalues e⁻ⁱεⱼT are the quasi‑energies. The scattering states are superpositions of Floquet modes ψⱼ(t,x) = e⁻ⁱεⱼᵗ φⱼ(t,x) with φⱼ(t+T) = φⱼ(t). The S‑matrix connects incoming and outgoing Floquet channels, and its unitarity follows from the conservation of the Floquet norm. The Magnus expansion provides a systematic way to compute the effective time‑averaged Hamiltonian H_eff = (i/T) log U(T,0) = H₀ + V₀ + 1/T Ω₂(T) + … where V₀ = (1/T)∫₀ᵀ V(t)dt. The first correction Ω₂(T)/T involves the commutator of V at different times and captures the effect of rapid oscillations. For high‑frequency fields (T → 0) the expansion is asymptotic and H_eff ≈ H₀ + V₀ + (1/2ω)[V₁,V₋₁] + … where Vₙ are Fourier coefficients of V(t). This is the basis of the Floquet‑Magnus expansion used in ultracold atom experiments to engineer effective Hamiltonians. The scattering theory for the nonlinear equation i∂ₜu + Δu = V(t,x)u + |u|p−1u combines the Magnus expansion for the linear part with Strichartz estimates for the nonlinearity. The wave operators exist for small data when V(t) satisfies the same integrability conditions as in the linear case, and the nonlinearity is energy‑subcritical. The asymptotic state satisfies a modified free evolution with a phase that includes both the Dollard correction from the nonlinearity and the Magnus phase from the time‑dependent potential.

  • Confusing the Hartree and Hartree‑Fock exchange term signs: The direct term is defocusing for repulsive potentials, the exchange term is focusing. Many candidates write the energy functional with the wrong sign for the exchange term, leading to incorrect blow‑up/scattering thresholds.
  • Ignoring the spectral gap in waveguide scattering: The zero mode (λ₀ = 0) and higher modes (λ_j > 0) have different dispersion relations. Treating all modes as having the same dispersion misses the two‑speed phenomenon and the decoupling of the zero mode at low energy.
  • Misapplying the pseudo‑conformal transformation to logarithmic nonlinearities: The logarithmic term is not scale‑invariant; the pseudo‑conformal transformation produces a time‑dependent coefficient. Candidates often forget the extra log t term in the phase correction.
  • Assuming the Born series converges for all convolution potentials: Convergence requires ‖K̂ f̂‖L¹ < 1. For Coulomb‑type potentials K̂(p) ∼ |p|⁻² this fails at low momentum, and one must use the Lippmann‑Schwinger equation directly.
  • Using the Magnus expansion beyond its radius of convergence: The series converges only when ∫‖V(t)‖dt < π. For strong or long‑duration pulses the expansion diverges and one must use the Floquet theory or numerical propagation.
  • Neglecting the commutator term in the Floquet‑Magnus effective Hamiltonian: The term (1/2ω)[V₁,V₋₁] is often the leading correction for high‑frequency driving. Dropping it gives the wrong effective mass and misses topological band inversions.
  • Treating the S‑matrix for Hartree‑Fock as diagonal in momentum space: The exchange term couples different momenta. The S‑matrix kernel S(p,p′) has off‑diagonal components that satisfy the optical theorem with a sum over intermediate momenta.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Type and the Spectral Stability of Solitary Waves

The spectral stability of solitary waves in nonlocal nonlinear Schrödinger equations is governed by the linearized operator around the standing wave profile.
