For a standing wave u(t,x) = eiωtφω(x) with φω solving ωφ + Δφ + (K * |φ|2)φ = 0, the linearization yields a matrix Hamiltonian L = ( L+ 0 ; 0 L− ) where L+ = −Δ + ω − (K * φω2) − 2φω(K * φω·) and L− = −Δ + ω − (K * φω2). The Vakhitov–Kolokolov criterion d/dω ‖φω‖L22 > 0 provides a necessary condition for spectral stability when the kernel of L+ is spanned by ∂xφω. For convolution kernels K with positive Fourier transform (e.g., Yukawa K̂(ξ) = (|ξ|2+m2)−1), the ground state is a minimizer of the energy at fixed mass, and the Grillakis–Shatah–Strauss theory (1987) guarantees orbital stability. When K̂ changes sign, the spectrum of L+ may acquire negative eigenvalues beyond the translational mode, leading to exponential instability. The Birman–Schwinger principle relates the number of negative eigenvalues of L+ to the number of bound states of a compact operator involving K̂. Rigorous results by Weinstein (1985) for power‑type nonlinearities extend to nonlocal cases via the concentration‑compactness method, showing that the ground state is a constrained minimizer precisely when the VK condition holds. Numerical Evans‑function computations confirm that the point spectrum crosses the imaginary axis exactly at the VK threshold, and the continuous spectrum remains on the imaginary axis due to the self‑adjointness of the linearized Hamiltonian.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Type and the Modulation Equations for Soliton Parameters

When a solution stays close to the solitary‑wave manifold, its dynamics can be reduced to a finite‑dimensional system for the soliton parameters: position q(t), velocity p(t), phase θ(t), and frequency ω(t). The modulation equations are derived by imposing orthogonality conditions ⟨ψ−φω, ∂ωφω⟩ = ⟨ψ−φω, ∂xφω⟩ = 0, which uniquely determine the parameters for sufficiently small perturbations. For nonlocal nonlinearities the effective Lagrangian Leff = ∫ [½|∂tψ|2 − ½|∇ψ|2 − ¼(K * |ψ|2)|ψ|2] dx yields, after substituting the ansatz ψ = φω(x−q) ei(p·x+θ), the reduced Lagrangian Lred = M(ω)ṗ·q̇ + M(ω)ω̇θ̇ − Heff(ω,p) where M(ω) = ‖φω‖L22 and Heff = E(φω) + ½M(ω)p2. The Euler–Lagrange equations give ṗ = 0, q̇ = p, ω̇ = 0, θ̇ = ω + p2/2, reproducing the free‑particle motion of the soliton centre. Radiation damping appears at higher order: the coupling to the continuous spectrum generates a friction term −γ(ω)p in the momentum equation, with γ(ω) computed via the Fermi Golden Rule (see next section). The modulation theory of Buslaev–Perelman (1995) and Soffer–Weinstein (1999) proves that for small initial data the solution decomposes as ψ = φω(t)(·−q(t))ei(p(t)·x+θ(t)) + η(t) where η satisfies Strichartz estimates and decays like t−3/2 in L∞. The nonlocal kernel enters the modulation equations only through the mass M(ω) and the effective Hamiltonian Heff; the structure of the equations is universal for any translation‑invariant nonlinearity.

Worked Example: Modulation Equations for a Yukawa Nonlocal NLS
Consider the nonlocal NLS i∂tψ = −Δψ − (K * |ψ|2)ψ in ℝ3 with Yukawa kernel K(x) = e−m|x|/(4π|x|), m > 0. The standing wave φω solves ωφ − Δφ − (K * φ2)φ = 0. Derive the leading‑order modulation equations for the soliton centre q(t) and momentum p(t) assuming the solution remains close to the solitary‑wave manifold.
Step 1: Effective Lagrangian. The Lagrangian density is ℒ = ½i(ψ̄∂tψ − ψ∂tψ̄) − |∇ψ|2 + ½(K * |ψ|2)|ψ|2. Insert the ansatz ψ = φω(x−q) ei(p·x+θ) with time‑dependent parameters. Because the kernel is translation invariant, the nonlocal term depends only on φω and not on q, p, θ. Step 2: Reduced Lagrangian. After integration, Lred = M(ω)ṗ·q̇ + M(ω)ω̇θ̇ − E(φω) − ½M(ω)p2, where M(ω) = ∫ φω2 dx. Step 3: Euler–Lagrange equations. Variation w.r.t. q gives d/dt(M(ω)p) = 0 ⇒ ṗ = 0 (since ω̇ = 0 at leading order). Variation w.r.t. p gives M(ω)q̇ = M(ω)p ⇒ q̇ = p. Variation w.r.t. ω and θ yield ω̇ = 0 and θ̇ = ω + p2/2. Result: The leading‑order modulation system is ṗ = 0, q̇ = p, ω̇ = 0, θ̇ = ω + p2/2. The soliton moves as a free particle with constant velocity p and constant frequency ω. Radiation damping would appear as a correction ṗ = −γ(ω)p at the next order.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for modulation equations, first write the conserved quantities (mass, momentum, energy) for the full PDE. Then impose the orthogonality conditions to isolate the finite‑dimensional dynamics. Remember that the nonlocal kernel only affects the coefficients M(ω) and Heff; the symplectic structure of the reduced system is universal. If the problem includes a small external potential V(x), add the term −∫ V(x)φω2(x−q) dx to Heff and compute the force −∇q of that term.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Type and the Asymptotic Completeness for Multi‑Soliton Solutions

Asymptotic completeness in the multi‑soliton sector means that any solution with sufficiently small radiation component decomposes, as t → ±∞, into a sum of decoupled solitary waves plus a dispersive term that scatters to a free solution. For the nonlocal NLS with a convolution kernel K ∈ L1 ∩ L∞ and positive Fourier transform, the work of Martel–Merle (2005) for the local gKdV extends via the concentration‑compactness/rigidity method of Kenig–Merle (2006). The key steps are: (1) a profile decomposition for bounded sequences in H1 adapted to the nonlocal nonlinearity, (2) a rigidity theorem showing that a minimal‑mass blow‑up solution must be a solitary wave, and (3) a virial‑type identity localized in space to control the interaction between solitons. The nonlocal term (K * |u|2)u is handled by the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality, which guarantees that the interaction energy between well‑separated solitons decays exponentially with their distance. Consequently, the modulation parameters for each soliton satisfy a system of ODEs with exponentially small coupling terms. The asymptotic completeness result of Gustafson–Nakanishi–Tsai (2004) for the local NLS carries over: for initial data u0 = Σj=1N φωj(·−qj)ei pj·x + ε0 with ‖ε0‖H1 sufficiently small, there exist C1 functions ωj(t), qj(t), pj(t), θj(t) such that u(t) = Σ φωj(t)(·−qj(t))ei(pj(t)·x+θj(t)) + η(t) with ‖η(t)‖H1 → 0 as t → ±∞. The proof uses a bootstrap argument on the modulation equations and Strichartz estimates for the linearized operator around the multi‑soliton profile.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Type and the Fermi Golden Rule for Radiation Damping

Radiation damping describes the irreversible loss of energy from the discrete (soliton) mode into the continuous spectrum due to resonant coupling. The Fermi Golden Rule (FGR) gives the leading‑order decay rate γ(ω) = 2π ⟨F, δ(Hc − λ) F⟩ where Hc is the continuous‑spectrum part of the linearized Hamiltonian, λ is the embedded eigenvalue associated with the neutral mode, and F is the nonlinear forcing term projected onto the continuous spectral subspace. For the nonlocal NLS, the linearized operator around φω has a simple eigenvalue at 0 (translation) and a pair of purely imaginary eigenvalues ±iλ0 (internal mode) when the kernel K̂ is sufficiently attractive. The nonlinear term generates a coupling between the internal mode and the continuum at frequency 2λ0. The FGR condition requires that the matrix element ⟨ψc(2λ0), N2(φω)⟩ ≠ 0, where N2 is the quadratic part of the nonlinearity. When FGR holds, the internal mode amplitude a(t) satisfies ȧ = −γ a + higher order, with γ > 0, leading to exponential decay of the internal mode and a corresponding drift of the soliton parameters. Rigorous justification was given by Soffer–Weinstein (1999) for the local NLS and extended to nonlocal nonlinearities by Cuccagna (2001) and Mizumachi (2007). The computation of γ involves the distorted Fourier basis of Hc and the explicit form of the nonlocal kernel; for Yukawa interaction K̂(ξ) = (|ξ|2+m2)−1 the integral can be evaluated analytically, yielding γ ∼ C ω3/2 e−2√ω/m for small ω. If FGR fails (the matrix element vanishes), the decay is algebraic (t−3/2) and higher‑order resonances must be examined.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlinearities of Nonlocal Type and the Nonlinear Scattering for the Gross‑Pitaevskii Equation with Nonzero Boundary Conditions

The Gross‑Pitaevskii (GP) equation i∂tψ = −Δψ + (|ψ|2−1)ψ models a Bose‑Einstein condensate with nonzero density at infinity. The nonlocal version replaces the cubic term by (K * |ψ|2)ψ with K a convolution kernel. The scattering theory for the GP equation with nonzero boundary conditions was developed by Bethuel–Gravejat–Smets (2008) and Gallo–Pelinovsky (2012). The key difficulty is that the linearization around the constant background ψ ≡ 1 has a Bogoliubov spectrum with a linear phonon branch ω(k) ∼ c|k| at low momentum, where c = √2 is the speed of sound. The wave operators are defined on the Fock space of phonon excitations, and the S‑matrix maps incoming to outgoing phonon states. For the nonlocal GP equation, the phonon dispersion becomes ω(k) = √( |k|2 (|k|2 + 2K̂(k)) ). If K̂(k) ∼ |k|−α with α > 0, the low‑energy behaviour changes from linear to quadratic, affecting the infrared structure of the S‑matrix. The existence of wave operators is proved by a Cook‑type argument using the dispersive estimate ‖e−itHPc‖L1→L∞ ≤ C t−3/2 for the linearized Hamiltonian H. The nonlinear term is treated in the interaction picture, and the small‑data scattering result holds for initial perturbations in the energy space E = { ψ−1 : ∇(ψ−1) ∈ L2, |ψ|2−1 ∈ L2 }. The asymptotic completeness for multi‑soliton (dark soliton) solutions follows from the modulation theory of the previous section combined with the linear phonon scattering. The inverse scattering transform for the nonlocal GP equation with nonzero boundary conditions is an open problem; however, the Riemann‑Hilbert formulation for the local GP equation (Gallo–Pelinovsky) suggests that the nonlocal kernel enters only through the modified dispersion relation in the jump matrix.

  • Confusing the Vakhitov–Kolokolov criterion with the Grillakis–Shatah–Strauss orbital stability theorem: VK is necessary but not sufficient when the kernel has negative Fourier modes.
  • Assuming the modulation equations are identical for all nonlocal kernels: the coefficients M(ω) and Heff depend explicitly on K̂, and the radiation damping coefficient γ(ω) is kernel‑dependent.
  • Neglecting the exponential separation condition in multi‑soliton asymptotic completeness: the interaction energy decays exponentially only when solitons are well separated; overlapping solitons require a different analysis.
  • Applying the Fermi Golden Rule without verifying the non‑degeneracy of the matrix element: a vanishing matrix element leads to algebraic decay, not exponential.
  • Treating the Gross‑Pitaevskii equation with nonzero boundary conditions as a standard NLS on ℝd: the background density changes the linear spectrum from quadratic to linear, invalidating standard Strichartz estimates.
  • Overlooking the role of the Hardy–Littlewood–Sobolev inequality in controlling nonlocal interaction energies: it is essential for proving that the interaction between distant solitons is exponentially small.
  • Assuming the inverse scattering transform for the nonlocal GP equation follows directly from the local case: the modified dispersion relation alters the analytic structure of the scattering data.

The advanced topics above illustrate how nonlocal nonlinearities modify each layer of scattering theory, from spectral stability to long‑time asymptotics.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Limiting Absorption Principle

The limiting absorption principle (LAP) for the fractional Schrödinger operator H = (−Δ)^s + V with s ∈ (0,1) asserts that the resolvent (H − λ ± i0)−1 admits uniform bounds in weighted L2 spaces as the spectral parameter approaches the continuous spectrum. For short‑range potentials V satisfying |V(x)| ≤ C⟨x⟩−1−ε the principle was established by Frank and Simon (2014) using a commutator method adapted to the non‑local kinetic term. The proof relies on a Mourre estimate with the conjugate operator A = (x·∇ + ∇·x)/2 and on the fact that the fractional Laplacian satisfies a strict Mourre estimate on any compact interval away from thresholds. The LAP yields the existence of the boundary values of the resolvent, which in turn gives the absolutely continuous spectrum of H and the radiation condition for the associated time‑dependent equation. A key technical point is the control of the commutator [V, A] in the fractional setting; this requires V to belong to the Rollnik class or to satisfy a suitable Kato‑type smallness condition. The LAP also implies the limiting absorption principle for the perturbed resolvent in the sense of Agmon, which is the starting point for the construction of the distorted Fourier basis and the definition of the wave operators.

When the potential has a long‑range tail, e.g. V(x) ∼ |x|−α with 0 < α ≤ 1, the standard LAP fails because the commutator [V, A] is no longer relatively compact. In this regime one must introduce a Dollard‑type phase correction or work with a modified conjugate operator adapted to the classical Hamiltonian flow of the fractional dispersion relation ξ → |ξ|2s. Recent work by Bouclet and Mizutani (2018) shows that a LAP with a logarithmic loss holds for potentials decaying like |x|−1 provided the angular dependence satisfies a non‑trapping condition. The result is sharp: for the Coulomb potential V(x) = γ|x|−1 the LAP holds only after a suitable gauge transformation, reflecting the well‑known infrared divergence in the fractional case as well.

The LAP is also the main ingredient in the proof of the absence of singular continuous spectrum for H. By the Kato‑Kuroda theorem, if the difference of the resolvents (H − z)−1 − (H0 − z)−1 is trace class for Im z ≠ 0 and the LAP holds, then the wave operators exist and are complete. For fractional operators the trace‑class condition is more restrictive because the free resolvent kernel decays only polynomially. Frank, Lewin, Lieb and Seiringer (2015) identified the optimal condition V ∈ Ld/(2s)(ℝ^d) for the existence of wave operators in dimension d > 2s. This condition coincides with the borderline for the Birman‑Schwinger principle and shows that the fractional kinetic energy does not improve the short‑range threshold compared with the local case.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Mourre Theory

Mourre theory provides a powerful framework to prove the limiting absorption principle, absence of singular continuous spectrum, and local decay estimates for self‑adjoint operators. For the fractional Schrödinger operator H = (−Δ)^s + V the conjugate operator is usually taken as the generator of dilations A = (x·p + p·x)/2 with p = −i∇. The Mourre estimate E_I(H)[H, iA]E_I(H) ≥ θ E_I(H) + K on a compact interval I away from thresholds requires a strict positivity of the commutator [H, iA] = 2s(−Δ)^s + [V, iA]. Since the free part gives 2s(−Δ)^s ≥ 2s c_s |ξ|2s, the estimate holds provided the potential term is relatively compact with respect to (−Δ)^s. Georgescu and Măntoiu (2008) proved that if V is a relatively compact perturbation of (−Δ)^s and satisfies a short‑range condition |V(x)| ≤ C⟨x⟩−1−ε, then a Mourre estimate holds on any interval I ⊂ (0, ∞) that does not contain eigenvalues. The compactness of [V, iA] follows from the fact that V is a multiplication operator decaying faster than |x|−1 and the fractional Laplacian has a symbol homogeneous of degree 2s.

When V has a long‑range component, the commutator [V, iA] is no longer compact. In this case one can use a modified conjugate operator A_V = A + Φ(x) where Φ is a pseudodifferential operator chosen to cancel the leading long‑range part of [V, iA]. This technique, developed by Skibsted (2010) for the standard Laplacian, extends to the fractional case because the principal symbol of (−Δ)^s is |ξ|2s and the Poisson bracket with a suitable Φ reproduces the classical force term. The resulting Mourre estimate holds with a remainder that is compact, yielding the LAP with a loss of derivatives that matches the classical propagation estimates. An important application is the proof of the absence of singular continuous spectrum for potentials in the Kato class Kd,2s, which includes the critical Coulomb potential in three dimensions for s > 1/2.

The Mourre theory also gives local decay estimates of the form ‖⟨A⟩−σ e−itH P_c ψ‖ ≤ C|t|−1/2 ‖⟨A⟩σ ψ‖ for σ > 1/2, where P_c projects onto the continuous spectral subspace. These estimates are the fractional analogue of the classical local decay results of Enss and Sigal. They imply that the wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e−itH0 exist and are asymptotically complete for short‑range potentials. For long‑range potentials the same method, combined with a Dollard modifier, yields modified wave operators. The Mourre approach is particularly robust because it does not require explicit kernel estimates for the resolvent; it only needs the commutator structure and a suitable class of potentials.

Worked Example: Mourre Estimate for a Fractional Schrödinger Operator
Let H = (−Δ)3/4 + V on L2(ℝ3) with V(x) = −γ(1+|x|2)−1, γ > 0. Show that a Mourre estimate holds on any compact interval I ⊂ (0, ∞) not containing eigenvalues, using the conjugate operator A = (x·p + p·x)/2.
1. Compute the free commutator: [ (−Δ)3/4, iA ] = (3/2)(−Δ)3/4 because the symbol |ξ|3/2 is homogeneous of degree 3/2 and the Poisson bracket with the dilation generator gives the degree. 2. The potential commutator is [V, iA] = x·∇V = 2γ x2 (1+|x|2)−2, which is a bounded multiplication operator decaying like |x|−2. 3. Since V is relatively compact with respect to (−Δ)3/4 (Kato‑Rellich), the operator [V, iA] is compact. 4. On any interval I bounded away from zero, the free part satisfies E_I(H0)(−Δ)3/4E_I(H0) ≥ c_I > 0. By the stability of the Mourre estimate under relatively compact perturbations (Mourre’s theorem), there exists θ > 0 and a compact K such that E_I(H)[H, iA]E_I(H) ≥ θ E_I(H) + K. 5. The absence of eigenvalues in I can be checked via the Birman‑Schwinger principle; for small γ there are none. Hence the Mourre estimate holds on I.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a Mourre estimate, first identify the conjugate operator (usually the dilation generator). Compute the free commutator using homogeneity of the symbol. Then check the potential commutator: if it is relatively compact, the estimate follows by standard perturbation theory. If the potential is long‑range, mention the need for a modified conjugate operator and cite the Skibsted‑type construction. Always state the interval away from thresholds and the compact remainder.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Propagation Estimates

Propagation estimates describe how the probability density of a quantum state moves in space under the fractional Schrödinger flow e−itH. Unlike the local Schrödinger equation, the fractional dispersion relation ω(ξ) = |ξ|2s yields a group velocity ∇_ξ ω(ξ) = 2s|ξ|2s−2ξ that grows without bound as |ξ| → ∞. Consequently, the standard finite‑speed‑of‑propagation property fails; instead one has estimates that quantify the decay of the solution outside a light‑cone that expands super‑linearly. Bouclet and Mizutani (2018) proved that for H = (−Δ)^s + V with a short‑range potential, the solution satisfies ‖⟨x⟩−σ e−itH P_c ψ‖L2 ≤ C|t|−σ/(2s) ‖⟨x⟩σ ψ‖L2 for any σ > 1/2. This estimate reflects the fact that high‑frequency components travel faster, so the tail of the wave packet spreads with a rate t1/(2s). The proof uses a positive commutator argument with a conjugate operator adapted to the classical Hamiltonian flow, namely A = f(x·ξ/|ξ|) where f is a smooth cut‑off. The method yields also a local smoothing estimate of Kato type: ∫_ℝ ‖⟨x⟩−σ (−Δ)s/2 e−itH P_c ψ‖L22 dt ≤ C ‖ψ‖L22.

For long‑range potentials the propagation estimates acquire a phase correction. The classical trajectories solve ẋ = 2s|ξ|2s−2ξ, ξ̇ = −∇V(x). If V decays like |x|−α with α > 1, the trajectories are asymptotic to straight lines and the same polynomial decay holds. If α = 1 (Coulomb‑type), the trajectories have a logarithmic distortion and the propagation estimate gains a logarithmic loss. These results are the fractional counterparts of the classical Enss‑Sigal propagation estimates and are essential for proving asymptotic completeness of the wave operators via the Enss method. In particular, one shows that for any ψ ∈ L2 the state e−itHψ eventually leaves any bounded region, which implies that the continuous spectral subspace coincides with the space of states that propagate to infinity.

Propagation estimates also play a role in the study of resonances. By complex scaling the Hamiltonian, one can relate the resonance poles to the eigenvalues of the dilated operator. The propagation estimates for the dilated flow give bounds on the resolvent near the real axis, which in turn control the width of resonances. For the fractional Schrödinger operator with a dilation‑analytic potential, the resonance counting function satisfies N(r) ≤ C rd/(2s), a Weyl‑type law that reflects the phase‑space volume of the classical trapped set. This bound was established by Sjöstrand and Zworski (2002) for the local case and extended to the fractional case by Datchev and Vasy (2012) using the propagation estimates for the complex‑scaled dynamics.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Local Smoothing Effect

The local smoothing effect, also known as the Kato smoothing estimate, asserts that the solution of the Schrödinger equation gains half a derivative locally in space when integrated in time. For the fractional Schrödinger equation i∂_t u = (−Δ)^s u + V u the optimal local smoothing estimate reads ∫_ℝ ‖⟨x⟩−σ (−Δ)s/2 e−itH P_c ψ‖L22 dt ≤ C ‖ψ‖L22 for σ > 1/2. This was proved by Cho, Lee and Ozawa (2015) for s ∈ (1/2,1) and short‑range potentials using the Mourre theory with the conjugate operator A = (x·p + p·x)/2. The key observation is that the commutator [H, iA] = 2s(−Δ)^s + [V, iA] controls the fractional derivative (−Δ)s/2 because (−Δ)^s = ((−Δ)s/2)2. The estimate fails for s ≤ 1/2 in dimensions d ≥ 2 due to the insufficient decay of the free propagator kernel; in that regime one needs a weaker smoothing with a logarithmic loss or a Morawetz‑type estimate.

Local smoothing is a crucial tool for proving Strichartz estimates for the perturbed equation. By the Christ‑Kiselev lemma, the local smoothing estimate together with the dispersive estimate ‖e−itH P_c‖L1→L^∞ ≤ C|t|−d/(2s) yields the full family of admissible Strichartz pairs (q,r) satisfying 2/q + d/r = d/2 − s. For the fractional equation the admissible range is larger than in the local case because the dispersion is stronger for s > 1/2. The Strichartz estimates then allow one to treat nonlinearities via a fixed‑point argument in the critical space Ḣs_c with s_c = d/2 − 2s/(p−1). This program was carried out by Guo, Wang and Xiao (2017) for the fractional NLS with a potential, showing global well‑posedness and scattering for small data in the energy‑subcritical regime.

When the potential is long‑range, the local smoothing estimate holds with a modified conjugate operator that incorporates the classical action. The resulting estimate has the same form but with a constant that depends on the long‑range tail. For the Coulomb potential V(x) = γ|x|−1 the local smoothing estimate was proved by Kato and Yajima (1989) for the standard Laplacian and extended to the fractional case by Mochizuki (2019) using a phase‑space multiplier adapted to the Kepler problem. The estimate is essential for the construction of the Dollard‑modified wave operators and for the proof of asymptotic completeness in the long‑range setting.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Wave Operators

The existence and completeness of the wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e−itH0 for the fractional Schrödinger operator H = (−Δ)^s + V is the central result of time‑dependent scattering theory. For short‑range potentials satisfying |V(x)| ≤ C⟨x⟩−1−ε the Cook method applies directly because the free propagator satisfies the dispersive bound ‖e−itH0‖L1→L^∞ ≤ C|t|−d/(2s). The integral ∫^∞ ‖V e−itH0 ψ‖ dt converges for ψ in a dense set, yielding the existence of Ω_±. Asymptotic completeness follows from the intertwining property and the absence of singular continuous spectrum, which is guaranteed by the Mourre estimate. Yajima (2010) proved that the wave operators are bounded on L^p for 1 ≤ p ≤ ∞ and that they preserve the Sobolev spaces H^σ for |σ| < s. This regularity result is sharper than in the local case because the fractional kinetic energy controls higher derivatives.

For long‑range potentials the standard wave operators do not exist; one must introduce a Dollard modifier U_D(t) = e−itH0 e−iΦ(t) where Φ(t) is the classical action along the free trajectory. The modified wave operators Ω_±^D = s‑limt→±∞ eitH U_D(t) exist and are complete for potentials decaying like |x|−α with 0 < α ≤ 1, provided the angular part satisfies a non‑trapping condition. The construction uses the propagation estimates to show that the interaction picture evolution converges. In the critical Coulomb case α = 1 the modifier involves a logarithmic phase Φ(t) = γ log t, reflecting the well‑known infrared divergence. The modified wave operators are unitary on the continuous spectral subspace and intertwine H with H0 up to the phase.

The scattering matrix S = (Ω_+)^* Ω_− is a unitary operator on the absolutely continuous subspace of H0. In the fractional case the S‑matrix can be expressed via the stationary formula S(λ) = I − 2πi T(λ)^* T(λ) where T(λ) = V1/2 (H − λ − i0)−1 V1/2 is the transition operator. The limiting absorption principle ensures that T(λ) is well defined as a bounded operator on L2(ℝ^d). The S‑matrix inherits the analyticity properties of the resolvent; for dilation‑analytic potentials it extends meromorphically to a neighbourhood of the real axis, with poles corresponding to resonances. The Birman‑Krein formula relates the determinant of the S‑matrix to the spectral shift function ξ(λ), which counts the difference of eigenvalues of H and H0 below λ. This formula holds for the fractional operator under the same trace‑class conditions as in the local case, and it provides a powerful tool for computing the total scattering cross‑section.

  • Confusing the limiting absorption principle for the fractional Laplacian with the local case: the threshold for short‑range potentials is the same decay |x|−1−ε but the commutator estimates require the fractional Mourre theory.
  • Assuming the Mourre estimate holds for any potential in the Kato class: the conjugate operator must be chosen carefully, and long‑range tails demand a modified conjugate operator.
  • Neglecting the super‑linear propagation speed in fractional dispersion: standard Enss propagation estimates must be adapted to the t1/(2s) light‑cone.
  • Applying the Kato smoothing estimate for s ≤ 1/2 without a logarithmic loss: the estimate fails in dimensions d ≥ 2 and a weaker Morawetz‑type bound is needed.
  • Using the standard Cook method for long‑range potentials: the Dollard phase correction is essential, and for Coulomb‑type tails a logarithmic phase appears.
  • Overlooking the trace‑class condition for the Birman‑Krein formula: the difference of resolvents must be trace class, which imposes V ∈ Ld/(2s) in the fractional setting.
  • Treating the scattering matrix as a function on the sphere only: for fractional operators the S‑matrix depends on the energy λ and the direction, and its analytic continuation reveals resonances.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Spectral Shift Function

The spectral shift function ξ(λ) provides a precise measure of how the eigenvalue counting function of the perturbed Hamiltonian H = (−Δ)^s + V differs from that of the free fractional Laplacian H₀ = (−Δ)^s. For trace‑class perturbations V satisfying V ∈ Ld/(2s)(ℝ^d) ∩ L¹(ℝ^d) the Birman–Krein formula det S(λ) = e−2πi ξ(λ) holds, where S(λ) is the stationary scattering matrix at energy λ > 0. The proof adapts the abstract Birman–Krein theory to the fractional setting by establishing that the difference of resolvents (H − z)−1 − (H₀ − z)−1 belongs to the trace class for Im z ≠ 0. This requires the fractional Mourre estimate with conjugate operator A = (x·∇ + ∇·x)/2, which yields a strict Mourre inequality on any compact interval away from thresholds. The spectral shift function is then expressed as ξ(λ) = (1/π) limε→0+ arg det(I + V1/2(H₀ − λ − iε)−1V1/2). For radial potentials V(x) = v(|x|) the partial‑wave decomposition reduces ξ(λ) to a sum over angular momenta ℓ of the phase shifts δ_ℓ(λ), each obtained from the Jost solutions of the fractional radial equation. In the critical case s = 1/2 (the half‑Laplacian) the threshold behaviour ξ(λ) ∼ C λ(d−1)/(2s) as λ → 0+ reflects the modified density of states. Applications include the computation of the total scattering cross‑section σ_tot(λ) = 4π λ(d−2)/2 ξ(λ) and the derivation of Levinson’s theorem for fractional operators, which relates the number of bound states N_b to the limit ξ(0+) = N_b + (1/2)δ0,threshold.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and Resolvent Estimates in Weighted Spaces

Weighted resolvent estimates of the form ‖⟨x⟩−σ(H − λ ± i0)−1⟨x⟩−σ‖L²→L² ≤ C_σ λ−1+σ for σ > 1/2 are the cornerstone of the limiting absorption principle for fractional Schrödinger operators. The proof follows the positive commutator method: one computes the commutator i[H, A] = 2s(−Δ)^s + x·∇V and shows that on any compact interval I ⊂ (0, ∞) there exists c > 0 such that E_I(H) i[H, A] E_I(H) ≥ c E_I(H) + K, where K is compact. The fractional kinetic term 2s(−Δ)^s provides the necessary positivity, while the potential term is controlled by the assumption V ∈ Ld/(2s)_loc and a suitable decay at infinity. For long‑range potentials V(x) = O(|x|−α) with 0 < α ≤ 1 the standard Mourre estimate fails; one instead uses a modified conjugate operator A_Φ = A + Φ(x) where Φ is a bounded pseudodifferential operator chosen to cancel the long‑range part of x·∇V. This yields a Mourre estimate with a remainder that is compact relative to H. The weighted estimate then implies the existence of the boundary values of the resolvent as bounded operators between weighted L² spaces, which in turn gives the stationary representation of the scattering matrix S(λ) = I − 2πi Γ₀(λ) V (H − λ − i0)−1 V Γ₀(λ)^*, where Γ₀(λ) is the trace operator onto the energy surface. The same estimates also yield local smoothing bounds ∫_ℝ ‖⟨x⟩−σ e−itH P_c(H) ψ‖²L² dt ≤ C ‖ψ‖²L², essential for the time‑dependent construction of wave operators.

Worked Example: Spectral Shift Function for a Fractional Potential
Let H = (−Δ)1/2 + V on ℝ³ with V(x) = −γ(1+|x|²)−2, γ > 0. Compute the leading low‑energy behaviour of the spectral shift function ξ(λ) as λ → 0+.
For s = 1/2 the free resolvent kernel is G₀(λ; x, y) = C λ e−λ|x−y|/|x−y|. The Birman–Schwinger operator K(λ) = |V|1/2(H₀ − λ − i0)−1|V|1/2 is trace class. At low energy λ ≪ 1, the kernel of K(λ) is approximated by its λ = 0 limit K(0) with kernel C |V(x)|1/2 |V(y)|1/2/|x−y|. The eigenvalues μ_j(λ) of K(λ) satisfy μ_j(λ) = μ_j(0) + O(λ). The spectral shift function is ξ(λ) = ∑_j (1/π) arg(1 + μ_j(λ)). Since V ≤ 0, all μ_j(0) > 0. For small λ, 1 + μ_j(λ) > 0, so arg = 0 unless an eigenvalue crosses −1. The number of bound states N_b equals the number of eigenvalues μ_j(0) > 1. By the Birman–Schwinger principle, N_b is the number of negative eigenvalues of H. Levinson’s theorem for the half‑Laplacian gives ξ(0+) = N_b. Hence ξ(λ) = N_b + O(λ) as λ → 0+. The constant N_b can be estimated by the Cwikel–Lieb–Rozenblum bound N_b ≤ C γ3/2.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the low‑energy asymptotics of ξ(λ), first identify the fractional order s and dimension d. Recall that the threshold behaviour is governed by the free density of states λd/(2s)−1. Use the Birman–Schwinger principle to relate bound states to eigenvalues of the Birman–Schwinger operator at zero energy. Levinson’s theorem for fractional operators often appears as a multiple‑choice distractor; remember the extra 1/2 term at threshold when a zero‑energy resonance exists.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and the Inverse Scattering Transform for Fractional NLS

The inverse scattering transform (IST) for the fractional nonlinear Schrödinger equation i∂_t u + (−Δ)^s u + λ|u|p−1u = 0 with 0 < s < 1 and p ≥ 1 relies on the linear scattering theory of the fractional Lax operator L = (−Δ)^s + V, where V is a matrix‑valued potential reconstructed from the solution u. The direct scattering problem constructs the Jost solutions ψ_±(x, k) satisfying (L − |k|2s)ψ_± = 0 with asymptotics ψ_± ∼ eik·x as x·k → ∓∞. The scattering matrix S(k) = (a(k), b(k); c(k), d(k)) is defined on the spectral surface |k|2s = λ. For the focusing case λ > 0 the discrete spectrum corresponds to bound states (solitons) and is encoded in the zeros of a(k) in the upper half‑plane of the complexified spectral parameter. The time evolution of the scattering data is trivial: a(k, t) = a(k, 0), b(k, t) = b(k, 0) e−2i|k|2st. The inverse problem is solved by a Riemann–Hilbert factorization on the contour Σ = {k ∈ ℂ : |k|2s ∈ ℝ}. The solution u(x, t) is recovered from the large‑k asymptotics of the sectionally analytic matrix M(k) solving the jump condition M_+(k) = M_−(k) J(k) with J(k) = I − 2πi (0, b(k); −c(k), 0). The IST has been rigorously justified for s = 1/2 (the half‑wave equation) and for small initial data in H^σ with σ > d/2 − s. For the critical power p = 1 + 4s/d the equation is L²‑critical and the IST provides a pathway to prove soliton resolution and scattering for data below the ground state mass.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and Long‑Range Scattering Asymptotics

When the potential decays as V(x) = O(|x|−α) with 0 < α ≤ 1, the standard wave operators Ω_± = s‑limt→±∞ eitH e−itH₀ fail to exist because the phase accumulated along free trajectories diverges. The Dollard modification introduces a time‑dependent phase Φ(t, x) = ∫0^t V(x + τ v) dτ, where v = ∇_k |k|2s|k = ξ is the group velocity. The modified wave operators Ω_±^D = s‑limt→±∞ eitH U_D(t) with U_D(t) = e−itH₀ e−iΦ(t) exist and are asymptotically complete for α > 0 under a non‑trapping condition on the classical flow. For the Coulomb‑type tail α = 1 the phase contains a logarithmic divergence Φ(t) ∼ γ log t, reflecting the infrared catastrophe. The asymptotic completeness proof uses the Enss geometric method adapted to the fractional propagation speed: the free evolution spreads wave packets along cones |x| ∼ t1/(2s). One partitions phase space into incoming, outgoing, and trapped regions and shows that the trapped component is empty under the Mourre estimate. The scattering matrix for long‑range potentials is defined as S^D = (Ω_+^D)^* Ω_−^D and satisfies the stationary formula S^D(λ) = I − 2πi T^D(λ)^* T^D(λ) with a modified transition operator T^D(λ) = V1/2 (H − λ − i0)−1 V1/2 eiΦ_λ, where Φ_λ is the classical action at energy λ. The analytic continuation of S^D(λ) into the complex plane reveals resonances as poles of the meromorphically continued resolvent, with the same distribution as in the short‑range case but with modified residue structure due to the long‑range phase.

Scattering Theory for the Schrödinger Equation with Nonlocal Potentials of Fractional Order and Numerical Computation of the Scattering Matrix

Accurate numerical computation of the scattering matrix S(λ) for fractional Schrödinger operators requires discretization of the nonlocal kinetic term (−Δ)^s. Spectral methods based on the Fourier transform are natural: on a periodic box of size L the operator is diagonal in the Fourier basis with eigenvalues |2π n/L|2s. The potential V is represented on a uniform grid and the resolvent (H − λ − iε)−1 is approximated by solving the linear system (H − λ − iε) u = f using a preconditioned Krylov method. The limiting absorption principle is enforced by taking ε → 0 after discretization, with ε chosen proportional to the grid spacing h to avoid pollution. The scattering matrix elements Sℓ m(λ) are obtained from the far‑field pattern of the scattered wave: one computes the solution ψ_ℓ of (H − λ)ψ_ℓ = 0 with incoming spherical wave boundary condition Yℓ m(x̂) |x|−(d−1)/2 eiλ1/(2s)|x| and extracts the outgoing coefficient via a discrete Dirichlet‑to‑Neumann map on a sphere of radius R ≫ 1. For the fractional Laplacian the far‑field asymptotics are ψ_ℓ(x) ∼ f_ℓ(θ) |x|−(d−1)/2 eiλ1/(2s)|x| with scattering amplitude f_ℓ(θ) related to Sℓ m by Sℓ m = δℓ m − 2πi λ(d−1)/(2s) f_ℓ(θ). Convergence rates are typically O(h2s) for smooth potentials, but degrade to O(hα) for potentials with limited regularity. Adaptive mesh refinement near singularities and the use of perfectly matched layers (PML) adapted to the fractional dispersion relation improve accuracy for long‑range potentials. Validation against analytic benchmarks such as the fractional Yukawa potential V(x) = γ e−μ|x|/|x|d−2s confirms the method.

  • Confusing the spectral shift function for fractional operators with the local case: the threshold exponent changes from λd/2−1 to λd/(2s)−1.
  • Assuming the standard Mourre estimate works for any long‑range potential: a modified conjugate operator is mandatory when α ≤ 1.
  • Neglecting the logarithmic phase in the Dollard modifier for Coulomb‑type tails: the phase is γ log t, not a power law.
  • Using the free propagator decay |t|−d/2 for fractional dispersion: the correct decay is |t|−d/(2s).
  • Treating the scattering matrix as energy‑independent: for fractional operators S(λ) depends explicitly on λ through the spectral parameter |k|2s.
  • Overlooking the trace‑class condition V ∈ Ld/(2s) for the Birman–Krein formula: the exponent differs from the local case d/2.
  • Applying the standard Enss propagation estimates without adapting the light‑cone to t1/(2s): this leads to incorrect asymptotic completeness proofs.

These advanced topics provide a deeper understanding of scattering theory for fractional and nonlocal operators.
